\section{Introduction}

Let \(X, Y\) be topological spaces, \(f, g : X \to Y\) be continuous functions. Note we will use the terminology `map := continuous function'. We say that \emph{\(f\) is homotopic to \(g\)}\index{homotopic}, written \(f \simeq g\), if there exists a map \(F : X \times I \to Y\), such that
\[F \vert_{X \times \{0\}}=  f \quad and \quad F \vert_{X \times \{1\}} = g\]
Here \(I = [0, 1]\) carries its Euclidean topology. An exercise (sheet 1) is that this defines an equivalence relation on the set of maps \(X \to Y\).

\begin{definition}
    [homotopy equivalence]
    We say that \(f : X \to Y\) is a \emph{homotopy equivalence}\index{homotopy equivalence} if there exists a function \(g : Y \to X\) such that \(f\circ g \simeq \id_Y\) and \(g \circ f \simeq \id_X\). We say that \(g\) is a \emph{homotopy inverse} to \(f\).
\end{definition}

The same exercise on sheet 1 shows that homotopy equivalence is an equivalence relation on topological spaces.

\begin{example}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item If \(f : X \to Y\) is a homeomorphism, then \(f\) is also a homotopy equivalence.
        \item \(i : \{0\} \inj \R^n\) is a homotopy equivance.
        \item The inclusion \(i : S^{n-1} \inj \R^n \setminus 0\) is a homotopy equivalence. 
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
    A space \(X\) is \emph{contractible}\index{contractible} if it is homotopy equivalent to a point.
\end{definition}

\underline{Idea of Algebraic Topology:} Study spaces up to homotopy equivalence. Broadly, we are interested in ``connectivity properties'' of topological spaces.

\begin{example}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item We say a space \(X\) is \emph{path-connected}\index{path connected} if any two maps \(\{*\} \to X\) are homotopic. For example, \(\R\) is path connected, \(\R \setminus \{0\}\) is not. A corollary of this is the intermediate value theorem.
        \item We say a path connected space \(X\) is \emph{simply connected}\index{simply connected} if every map \(f : S^1 \to X\) is homotopic to a constant map. Equivalently, every two maps \(S^1 \to X\) are homotopic. Or equivalently (again) every continuous map \(S^1 \to X\) extends to a continuous map \(D^2 \to X\). For example, \(\R^2\) is simply connected, by \(\R^2 \setminus \{0\}\) is not. If \(\gamma : S^1 \to \R^2 \setminus \{0\}\) is continuous, it has a \emph{winding number}, or \emph{degree} \(\deg(\gamma) \in \Z\), such that
        \begin{itemize}
            \item It is invariant under homotopy,
            \item If \(\gamma_n(t) = \exp(2n\pi it)\), then \(\deg(\gamma_n) = n\)
        \end{itemize}
        In particular, taking \(n = 0\) gives the constant map. Therefore, a constant map has degree \(0\). A corollary of this is the fundamental theorem of algebra.
        % Insert proof of FTA here
        \item We say a path connected space \(X\) is \emph{\(k\)-connected}\index{connected@\(k\)-connected}, if for all \(i \le k\), every map \(S^i \to X\) is homotopic to a constant map. In this case, \(\R^n\) is \((n-1)\)-connected, but \(\R^n \setminus 0\) is not. More precisely, maps from \(S^{n-1} \to \R^n \setminus 0\) have a degree \(\deg(f) \in Z\), such that degree is a homotopy invariant, and the degree of the inclusion map is \(1\), and the degree of the constant map is \(0\). A corollary of this is the Brouwer fixed point theorem.
        % Insert proof of Brouwer
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\lec

\section{(Co)homology}

\subsection{Co(chain) complexes}

We will define invariants of topological spaces in two steps:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item We associate to \(X\) a (co)chain complex,
    \item We take the (co)homology of the complex
\end{enumerate}
The topology enters in step (a), whereas (b) is just homological algebra.
\begin{definition}
    [chain complex]
    A \emph{chain complex}@\index{chain complex} \((C_*, \partial)\) consists of a sequence \(\{C_i\}_{i \in \Z}\) of abelian groups, along with \emph{boundary homomorphisms}\index{boundary homomorphism} \(\partial_n : C_n \to C_{n-1}\) for all \(n\), satisfying \(\partial_n \circ \partial_{n+1} = 0\). We also write this conditions as \(\partial^2 = 0\).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    [homology group]
    The \emph{\(i\)-th homology group}\index{homology group} of a chain complex \((C_*, \partial)\) is
    \[\HH_i(C_*, \partial) = \frac{\ker(\partial_i : C_i \to C_{i-1})}{\im(\partial_{i+1} : C_{i+1} \to C_i)}\]
    We write
    \[\HH_*(C_*, \partial) = \bigoplus_{i \in \Z}\HH_i(C_*, \partial)\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    [cochain complex]
    A \emph{cochain complex}\index{cochain complex} \((C^*, \partial)\) consists of a sequence \(\{C^i\}_{i \in \Z}\) of abelian groups, along with \emph{boundary homomorphisms} \(\partial^n : C^n \to C^{n+1}\) for all \(n\), satisfying \(\partial^{n+1} \circ \partial^n = 0\). We also write this conditions as \(\partial^2 = 0\).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    [cohomology group]
    The \emph{\(i\)-th cohomology group}\index{cohomology group} of a cochain complex \((C^*, \partial)\) is
    \[\HH^i(C^*, \partial) = \frac{\ker(\partial^i : C^i \to C^{i+1})}{\im(\partial^{i-1} : C^{i-1} \to C^i)}\]
    We write
    \[\HH^*(C^*, \partial) = \bigoplus_{i \in \Z}\HH_i(C^*, \partial)\]
\end{definition}

\begin{notation}
Elements of \(\ker(\partial)\) are called \emph{cycles}\index{cycle} (in a chain complex), \emph{cocycles}\index{cocycle} (in a cochain complex). Elements of \(\im(\partial)\) are called \emph{boundaries}\index{boundary} (and \emph{coboundary}\index{coboundary} resp.).

Elements of \(\HH_*\) are called \emph{homology classes}\index{homology class}, and elements of \(\HH^*\) are called \emph{cohomology classes}\index{cohomology class}.

We will call \(\partial\) the \emph{differential}\index{differential}.
\end{notation}

\begin{definition}
    [chain map]
    Given chain complex \((C_*, \partial)\), \((D_*, \partial)\), a \emph{chain map}\index{chain map} \(f : C_* \to D_*\) comprises group homomorphism \(f_i : C_i \to D_i\), such that
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJDX2kiXSxbMiwwLCJEX2kiXSxbMCwyLCJDX3tpLTF9Il0sWzIsMiwiRF97aS0xfSJdLFswLDIsIlxccGFydGlhbCIsMl0sWzEsMywiXFxwYXJ0aWFsIl0sWzAsMSwiZl9pIl0sWzIsMywiZl97aS0xfSIsMl1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{C_i} && {D_i} \\
	\\
	{C_{i-1}} && {D_{i-1}}
	\arrow["\partial"', from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow["\partial", from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["{f_i}", from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{f_{i-1}}"', from=3-1, to=3-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    commutes.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    A chain map \(f : C_* \to D_*\) induces homomorphisms \(f_* : \HH_i(C_*) \to \HH_i(D_*)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Let \(a \in \HH_i(C_*)\), and we can choose a cycle \(\alpha \in C_i(C_*)\), with \(\partial \alpha = 0\). In this case,
    \[\partial f_i(\alpha) = f_{i-1}(\partial\alpha) = f_{i-1}(0) = 0\]
    So \(f_i(\alpha) \in D_i\) is a cycle. We set \(f_*(a) = [f_i(\alpha)] \in \HH_i(D_*)\). Next, we need to show that this is independent of choices. Suppose \(a = [\alpha] = [\alpha']\). Then \(\alpha - \alpha' \in \im(\partial)\). Therefore, we can write \(\alpha - \alpha' = \partial\tau\). In this case,
    \[f_i(\alpha) - f_i(\alpha') = f_i(\partial\tau) = \partial f_{i+1}(\tau) \in \im(\partial)\]
    So we conclude \([f_i(\alpha)] = [f_i(\alpha')] \in \HH_i(D_*)\). It is clear that \(f_*\) defines a homomorphism.
\end{proof}

Correspondingly, we can define \emph{cochain maps}\index{cochain maps} \(C^* \to D^*\), that is, \(f^i : C^i \to D^i\) such that
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwyLCJDXmkiXSxbMiwyLCJEXmkiXSxbMCwwLCJDXntpKzF9Il0sWzIsMCwiRF57aSsxfSJdLFsyLDMsImZee2krMX0iXSxbMCwxLCJmXmkiLDJdLFswLDIsIlxccGFydGlhbCJdLFsxLDMsIlxccGFydGlhbCIsMl1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{C^{i+1}} && {D^{i+1}} \\
	\\
	{C^i} && {D^i}
	\arrow["{f^{i+1}}", from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{f^i}"', from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\partial", from=3-1, to=1-1]
	\arrow["\partial"', from=3-3, to=1-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
commutes.

\underline{Exercise:} This construction is functorial. That is, \((\id_{C_*})_* = \id_{\HH_*}\), and if we have \(f : C_* \to D_*, g : D_* \to E_*\), then \((g \circ f)_* = g_* \circ f_*\).

\underline{Goal:} To associate to a topological space \(X\) (co)chain complex \(C_*(X)\) and \(C^*(X)\). The definition works for any \(X\), but the theories are better behaved for ``nicer'' \(X\).

\subsection{Singular (co)homology}

We will develop singular (co)homology.

\begin{definition}
    [standard simplex, face]
    The \emph{standard simplex}\index{standard simplex} is
    \[\Delta^n = \left\{(t_0, \dots, t_n) \in \R^{n+1} \mid t_i \ge 0, \sum_i t_i = 1\right\}\]
    The \(i\)-th face \(\Delta^n\) is
    \[\Delta_i^n = \Delta^n \cap \{t_i = 0\}\]
    and we have a canonical homeomorphism
    \begin{align*}
        \delta_i : \Delta^{n-1} &\to \Delta^{n-1}_i \\
        (t_0, \dots, t_{n-1}) & \mapsto (t_0, \dots, t_{i-1}, 0, t_i, \dots, t_{n-1})
    \end{align*}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    [singular simplex, singular chain complex]
    If \(X\) is a topological space, a \emph{singular \(n\)-simplex}\index{singular simplex} in \(X\) is a continuous map \(\sigma : \Delta^n \to X\).

    The \emph{singular chain complex}\index{singular chain complex} is given by the the free abelian groups
    \[C_i(X) = \bigoplus_{\sigma \text{ singular }n\text{-simplex}}\Z \sigma\]
    We define the boundary map \(C_i(X) \to C_{i-1}(X)\) via
    \[\partial \sigma = \sum_{j=0}^i (-1)^j \sigma\vert_{\Delta_j^i}\]
    where
    \[\sigma \vert_{\Delta_j^i} := \sigma \circ \delta_j : \Delta^{n-1} \to X\]
\end{definition}

Note if \(\{v_i\}_{i=0}^n\) are \(n+1\) ordered points in \(\R^{n+1}\), and if \(\{v_i - v_0\}_{i=1}^n\) are linearly independent, then the convex hull of \(v_0, \dots, v_n\) is an \(n\)-simplex, which we will call
\[[v_0, \dots, v_n]\]
which is given by the map
\begin{align*}
    \Delta^n &\to \R^{n+1} \\
    t &\mapsto \sum_i t_iv_i
\end{align*}
We orient the edges of the standard simplex (and thus any simplex \([v_0, \dots, v_n]\)) by saying that \(v_i < v_j\) if \(i < j\).

More concretely, for \(n = 2\), we have the oriented edges \(v_0 \to v_1, v_0 \to v_2, v_1 \to v_2\).

\begin{lemma}
    \(\partial^2 = 0\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    If \(\sigma\) is an \(n\)-simplex, defined on \([v_0, \dots, v_n]\), then
    \[\partial\sigma = \sum_j(-1)^j\sigma\vert_{[v_0, \dots, \widehat{v_j}, \dots, v_n]}\]
    and so
    \[\partial(\partial\sigma) = \sum_{i < j}(-1)^i(-1)^j\sigma\vert_{[v_0, \dots, \widehat{v_i}, \dots, \widehat{v_j}, \dots, v_n]} + \sum_{i > j}(-1)^{i+1}{-1}^j\sigma\vert_{[v_0, \dots, \widehat{v_j}, \dots, \widehat{v_i}, \dots, v_n]} = 0\]
    since the signs cancel.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    The \emph{singular homology groups of \(X\)}\index{singular homology}, \(\HH_i(X)\), are the homology groups of the singular chain complex.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    Since all we used is the topology on \(X\), \(\HH_i(X)\) is clearly a topological (read homeomorphism) invariant of \(X\).

    Moreover, if \(f : X \to Y\) is continuous, \(\sigma : \Delta^n \to X\) is an \(n\)-simplex on \(X\), then \(f \circ \sigma\) is an \(n\)-simplex on \(Y\). The fact that
    \[(f \circ \sigma) \circ \delta_j = f \circ (\sigma \circ \delta_j)\]
    shows that \(f\) induces a chain map.
\end{remark}

\lec

\begin{definition}
    [singular cochain complex]
    The \emph{singular cochain complex}\index{singular cochain complex} of \(X\) has
    \[C^i(X) = \Hom_\Z(C_i(X), \Z)\]
    and the boundary operator
    \[\partial^*\psi(\sigma) = \psi(\partial \sigma)\]
    for \(\psi \in C^i(X), \sigma \in C_{i+1}(X)\). That is, it is adjoint to \(\partial : C_i(X) \to C_{i-1}(X)\).
\end{definition}

Since \(\partial^2 = 0, (\partial^*)^2 = 0\). The \emph{singular cohomology}\index{singular cohomology} is
\[\HH^*(X) = \bigoplus_{i \ge 0}\HH^i(C^*, \partial^*)\]

If \(f : X \to Y\) continuous, then \(f\) induces a chain map \(f_* : C_*(X) \to C_*(Y)\) via \(f_*(\sigma) = f \circ \sigma\), satisfying \(\partial f_* = f_*\partial\). Similarly, \(f : X \to Y\) defines a \emph{pullback map} \(f^* : C^*(Y) \to C^*(X)\), given by
\[f^*(\psi)(\tau) = \psi(f_*(\tau))\]

Again, we have that
\[\partial^* f^*(\psi)(\tau) = f^*\psi(\partial\tau) = \psi(f_*\partial\tau) = \psi(\partial f_*\tau) = (\partial^*\psi)(f_*\tau) = f^*(\partial^*\psi)(\tau)\]
So \(f^*\) is a cochain map, i.e. \(\partial^*f^* = f^*\partial^*\). Therefore, we have an induced homomorphism on cohomology,
\[f^* : \HH^*(Y) \to \HH^*(X)\]

\underline{Warning:} by definition,
\[C^i(X) = \Hom_\Z(C_i(X), \Z)\]
but it is not true (in general) that
\[\HH^i(X) = \Hom_\Z(\HH_i(X), \Z)\]

\subsubsection{Basic computations}

\begin{lemma}
    [homology of a point]
    Let \(X = \{\bullet\}\). Then
    \[\HH_n(X) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & n = 0 \\
        0 & \text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    By definition, \(C_i(X)\) is the free abelian groups on continuous map \(\Delta^i \to \{\bullet\}\). But there is only one such map, namely the constant map (for \(i \ge 0\)). Hence the chain complex looks like
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTAsWzQsMCwiMCJdLFszLDAsIkNfMChYKSJdLFsyLDAsIkNfMShYKSJdLFsxLDAsIkNfMihYKSJdLFswLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMywxLCJcXFoiXSxbNCwxLCIwIl0sWzIsMSwiXFxaIl0sWzEsMSwiXFxaIl0sWzAsMSwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFs0LDNdLFszLDJdLFsyLDFdLFsxLDBdLFs5LDhdLFs4LDddLFs3LDVdLFs1LDZdLFswLDYsIiIsMSx7Im9mZnNldCI6LTEsInN0eWxlIjp7ImhlYWQiOnsibmFtZSI6Im5vbmUifX19XSxbMCw2LCIiLDEseyJvZmZzZXQiOjEsInN0eWxlIjp7ImhlYWQiOnsibmFtZSI6Im5vbmUifX19XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {C_2(X)} & {C_1(X)} & {C_0(X)} & 0 \\
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where each \(C_i(X)\) is \(\Z\). Computing the boundary maps,
    \[\partial(\sigma_n) = \begin{cases}
        \sigma_{n-1} & \text{if }n\text{ is even} \\
        0 &\text{if }n\text{ is odd}
    \end{cases}\]
    Hence the chain complex is
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMiwwLCJcXFoiXSxbMywwLCJcXFoiXSxbNCwwLCJcXFoiXSxbNSwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzAsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFsyLDMsIjAiXSxbMSwyXSxbMCwxLCIwIl0sWzQsMF0sWzUsNF1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & \Z & \Z & \Z & \Z & 0
	\arrow["0", from=1-5, to=1-6]
	\arrow["\id",from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow["0", from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow["\id",from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    We can then compute the homology groups explicity.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    If we write
    \[X = \bigsqcup_{\alpha \in A}X_\alpha\]
    as a disjoint union of path components, then
    \[\HH_i(X) = \bigoplus_{\alpha \in A}\HH_i(X_\alpha)\]

    Moreover, if \(X\) is path connected (and nonempty), then \(\HH_0(X) = \Z\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Since \(\Delta^i\) is path connected, the image of a continuous map \(\sigma : \Delta^i \to X\) must lie within some \(X_\alpha\). Moreover, so does the image of all of the boundary faces \(\sigma \circ \delta_j\). So in fact
    \[(C_i(X), \partial) = \left(\bigoplus_{\alpha \in A}C_i(X_\alpha), \partial\right)\]
    Note that a general element of \(C_i(X)\) is a \underline{finite} linear combination of \(i\)-simplicies in \(X\). So its image can only meet finitely many of the \(X_\alpha\).

    Now suppose \(X\) is path connected. Define a function
    \begin{align*}
        \epsilon : C_0(X) &\to \Z \\
        \sum n_i\sigma_i &\mapsto \sum n_i
    \end{align*}

    Since \(X\) is nonempty, \(\epsilon\) is surjective. On the other hand, if \(\tau  \in C_1(X)\), then \(\epsilon(\tau(1) - \tau(0)) = 0\). So by linearity, 
    \[\im(\partial : C_1(X) \to C_0(X)) \sse \ker(\epsilon)\]
    Recall
    \[\HH_0(X) = \frac{C_0(X)}{\im(\partial : C_1(X) \to C_0(X))}\]
    and so \(\epsilon\) descends to a map \(\HH_0(X) \to \Z\). Now we will use the fact that \(X\) is path connected. Suppose
    \[\sum n_i\sigma_i \in \ker(\epsilon)\]
    and fix a base point \(p \in X\). Since the \(\sigma_i\) are \(0\)-simplicies, they correspond to points in \(X\). Moreover, since \(X\) is path connected, we can choose paths
    \[\tau_i : \Delta^1 \to X\]
    such that \(\tau_i(1) = \sigma_i\) and \(\tau_i(0) = p\). In this case,
    \[\partial\left(\sum n_i\tau_i\right) = \sum n_i\partial \tau_i = \sum n_i\sigma_i - (\sum n_i) p\]
    But we assumed \(\sum n_i = 0\), and so \(\ker(\epsilon) \sse \im(\partial : C_1(X) \to C_0(X))\). This then gives the required result, as the induced map
    \[\HH_0(X) \to \Z\]
    is then an isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\underline{Informal conjecture:} We can't compute anything else.

Therefore, we need to develop some more structure about (co)homology.

\underline{What are we doing?} Consider the case of \(X\) being an annulus in \(\R^2\). We have \(1\)-simplices

\begin{center}
    \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/at3_1 (1).jpg}
\end{center}


(\(\sigma_1\) null-homotopic, \(\sigma_2\) winds around the origin)

In this case, \(\partial\sigma_1 = \partial\sigma_2 = 0\), and so they define homology classes.

\begin{center}
    \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/at3_2.jpg}
\end{center}

Moreover, \([\partial\sigma_1] = 0\), since we have triangles \(\tau_0, \tau_1, \tau_2\) such that
\[\partial(\tau_0 + \tau_1 + \tau_2) = \sigma_1\]

From this intuition, \([\sigma_2] \ne 0\). But to show even this, we need some structure theorems.

\lec

\subsection{Fundamental properties}

(Co)homology is useful by virtue of various structural properties.

\begin{theorem}
    [homotopy invariance]
    \index{homotopy invariance!of (co)homology}
    If \(f, g : X \to Y\) are homotopic, then the induced maps \(f_*, g_*\) on homology, and the induced maps \(f^*, g^*\) on cohomology agree.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}
    If \(X \simeq Y\), then \(H_*(X) \simeq Y\), and if \(f\) is a homotopy equivalence, then \(f_*\) induces the isomorphism. For cohomology we have the similar statement, but with \(f^*\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    [Proof of the corollary] \(X \simeq Y\) via \(f\) is saying that there exists \(g : Y \to X\), such that \(g \circ f \simeq \id_X\), and \(f\circ g\simeq \id_Y\). That is,
    \[g_* \circ f_* = \id \qqtext{and} f_* \circ g_* = \id\]
\end{proof}

We think of this as saying that (co)homology is ``insensitive to inessential deformations''.

\begin{example}
    Recall \(\{0\} \inj \R^n\) is a homotopy equivalence, and so
    \[\HH_*(\R^n) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & * = 0 \\
        0 & \text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}\]
\end{example}

The other key structural property relies on some homological algebra.

\begin{definition}
    [exact sequence]
    An \emph{exact sequence}\index{exact sequence} is a chain complex with \(H_*(C_*, \partial) = 0\). That is,
    \[\im(\partial : C_{n+1} \to C_n) = \ker(\partial : C_n \to C_{n-1})\]
    
    Similarly, a cochain complex is \emph{exact} if \(H^*(C^*, \partial) = 0\).

    If \(A, B, C\) are abelian groups, and we have
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMyxbMCwwLCJBIl0sWzEsMCwiQiJdLFsyLDAsIkMiXSxbMCwxLCJcXGFscGhhIl0sWzEsMiwiXFxiZXRhIl1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	A & B & C
	\arrow["\alpha", from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["\beta", from=1-2, to=1-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    Then this sequence is \emph{exact at \(B\)}\index{exact at} if \(\ker(\beta) = \im(\alpha)\).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    [short exact sequence]
    A \emph{short exact sequence}\index{short exact sequence} is an exact sequence of the form
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMSwwLCJBIl0sWzIsMCwiQiJdLFszLDAsIkMiXSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzQsMCwiXFxidWxsZXQiXSxbMCwxLCJcXGFscGhhIl0sWzEsMiwiXFxiZXRhIl0sWzMsMF0sWzIsNF1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & A & B & C & 0
	\arrow["\alpha", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["\beta", from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
which is exact at all places. That is, \(\alpha\) is injective, \(\beta\) is surjective, and \(\im(\alpha) = \ker(\beta)\).
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item If
        % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiQSJdLFsyLDAsIkIiXSxbMywwLCIwIl0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMiwiXFxhbHBoYSJdLFsyLDNdXQ==
        \[\begin{tikzcd}
            0 & A & B & 0
            \arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
            \arrow["\alpha", from=1-2, to=1-3]
            \arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
        \end{tikzcd}\]
        is exact, then \(\alpha\) is an isomorphism.
        \item If
        % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzIsMCwiRyJdLFszLDAsIlxcWi9uIl0sWzQsMCwiMCJdLFsxLDIsIlxcYWxwaGEiXSxbMiwzLCJcXGJldGEiXSxbMyw0XSxbMCwxXV0=
        \[\begin{tikzcd}

            \arrow["\alpha", from=1-2, to=1-3]
            \arrow["\beta", from=1-3, to=1-4]
            \arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]

\begin{theorem}
    [Mayer-Vietoris]
    \label{thm:mayer-vietoris}
    \index{Mayer-Vietoris}
    If \(X = A \cup B\), with \(A, B\) open subsets of \(X\). Then there exists \emph{Mayer-Vietoris boundary homomorphism}\index{Mayer-Vietoris!homomorphism}
    \[\partial_{MV} : \HH_{i+!}(X) \to \HH_i(A \cap B)\]
    for all \(i\), so that the sequence
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJcXEhIX3tpKzF9KFgpIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF9pKEEgXFxjYXAgQikiXSxbMywwLCJcXEhIX2koQSlcXG9wbHVzIFxcSEhfaShCKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhfaShYKSJdLFswLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbNSwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzQsMF0sWzAsMSwiXFxwYXJ0aWFsX3tNVn0iXSxbMSwyLCJpX3tBKn1cXG9wbHVzIGlfe0IqfSJdLFsyLDMsImpfe0EqfS1qX3tCXip9Il1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH_{i+1}(X)} & {\HH_i(A \cap B)} & {\HH_i(A)\oplus \HH_i(B)} & {\HH_i(X)} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["{\partial_{MV}}", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{i_{A*}\oplus i_{B*}}", from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow["{j_{A*}-j_{B^*}}", from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    is exact, where
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJBXFxjYXAgQiJdLFsxLDAsIkEiXSxbMCwxLCJCIl0sWzEsMSwiWCJdLFswLDIsImlfQiIsMl0sWzAsMSwiaV9BIl0sWzEsMywial9BIl0sWzIsMywial9CIiwyXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{A\cap B} & A \\
	B & X
	\arrow["{i_B}"', from=1-1, to=2-1]
	\arrow["{i_A}", from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["{j_A}", from=1-2, to=2-2]
	\arrow["{j_B}"', from=2-1, to=2-2]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    Similarly, we have a homomorphism
    \[\partial_{MV}^* : H^i(A \cap B) \to H^{i+1}(X)\]
    which makes the sequence
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNyxbMSwwLCJIXntpLTF9KEEgXFxjYXAgQikiXSxbMiwwLCJIXmkoWCkiXSxbMywwLCJIXmkoQSlcXG9wbHVzIEheaShCKSJdLFs0LDAsIkheaShBIFxcY2FwIEIpIl0sWzUsMCwiSF57aS0xfShYKSJdLFswLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbNiwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzAsMSwiXFxwYXJ0aWFsX3tNVn1eKiJdLFszLDQsIlxccGFydGlhbF97TVZ9XioiXSxbMSwyLCJqX0FeKiBcXG9wbHVzIC1qX0JeKiJdLFsyLDMsImlfQV4qICtpX0JeKiJdLFs1LDBdLFs0LDZdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH^{i-1}(A \cap B)} & {\HH^i(X)} & {\HH^i(A)\oplus \HH^i(B)} & {\HH^i(A \cap B)} & {\HH^{i-1}(X)} & \cdots
	\arrow["{\partial_{MV}^*}", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{\partial_{MV}^*}", from=1-5, to=1-6]
	\arrow["{j_A^* \oplus -j_B^*}", from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow["{i_A^* +i_B^*}", from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-6, to=1-7]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    exact.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item The Mayer-Vietoris maps are not induced by maps of spaces, but they are constructed algebraically. Suppose \(\sigma \in C_{i+1}(X)\) is a cycle, and suppose we can write \(\sigma = \sigma_A + \sigma_B\), where \(\sigma_A \in C_{i+1}(A)\) and \(\sigma_B \in C_{i+1}(B)\) are chains, and in general, not cycles. Since \(\partial \sigma = 0, \partial \sigma_A + \partial\sigma_B = 0\). We define
        \[\partial_{MV}(\sigma) = [\partial\sigma_A] \in \HH_i(A \cap B)\]
        Since \(\partial \sigma_A \in C_i(A)\), we must have that \(\partial \sigma_A = -\partial\sigma_B \in C_{i}(A \cap B)\). As \(\partial^2 = 0\), \(\partial\sigma_A\) is closed and so it represents a class in homology.

        This is (correct) intuition, but not a proof.
        \item The Mayer-Vietoris sequence is natural for maps of pairs. That is, if \(X = A \cup B, Y = C \cup D\), and \(f : X \to Y\) has \(f(A) \sse C\), \(f(B) \sse D\), then we have a map of long exact sequences
        % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTQsWzIsMCwiSF9pKEEgXFxjYXAgQikiXSxbMywwLCJIX2koQSkgXFxvcGx1cyBIX2koQikiXSxbNCwwLCJIX2koWCkiXSxbMiwxLCJIX2koQ1xcY2FwIEQpIl0sWzMsMSwiSF9pKEMpIFxcb3BsdXMgSF9pKEQpIl0sWzQsMSwiSF9pKFkpIl0sWzUsMCwiSF97aS0xfShBIFxcY2FwIEIpIl0sWzUsMSwiSF97aS0xfShDXFxjYXAgRCkiXSxbMSwwLCJIX3tpKzF9KFgpIl0sWzEsMSwiSF97aSsxfShZKSJdLFswLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMCwxLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzYsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFs2LDEsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMTAsOF0sWzExLDldLFs4LDBdLFs5LDNdLFszLDRdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFs0LDVdLFsyLDZdLFs1LDddLFs2LDEyXSxbNywxM10sWzgsOSwiZl8qIiwxXSxbMCwzLCJmXyoiLDFdLFsxLDQsImZfKiBcXG9wbHVzIGZfKiIsMV0sWzIsNSwiZl8qIiwxXSxbNiw3LCJmXyoiLDFdXQ==
        \[\begin{tikzcd}
            \cdots & {\HH_{i+1}(X)} & {\HH_i(A \cap B)} & {\HH_i(A) \oplus \HH_i(B)} & {\HH_i(X)} & {\HH_{i-1}(A \cap B)} & \cdots \\
            \cdots & {\HH_{i+1}(Y)} & {\HH_i(C\cap D)} & {\HH_i(C) \oplus \HH_i(D)} & {\HH_i(Y)} & {\HH_{i-1}(C\cap D)} & \cdots
            \arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
            \arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
            \arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
            \arrow[from=2-2, to=2-3]
            \arrow[from=2-3, to=2-4]
            \arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
            \arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
            \arrow[from=2-4, to=2-5]
            \arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
            \arrow[from=2-5, to=2-6]
            \arrow[from=1-6, to=1-7]
            \arrow[from=2-6, to=2-7]
            \arrow["{f_*}"{description}, from=1-2, to=2-2]
            \arrow["{f_*}"{description}, from=1-3, to=2-3]
            \arrow["{f_* \oplus f_*}"{description}, from=1-4, to=2-4]
            \arrow["{f_*}"{description}, from=1-5, to=2-5]
            \arrow["{f_*}"{description}, from=1-6, to=2-6]
        \end{tikzcd}\]
        where all the squares commute.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Examples of Homology calculations}

We will prove these later when we use them.

\begin{example}
    \[\HH_*(S^1) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & * = 0, 1 \\
        0 & \text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}\]
\end{example}

\begin{proof}
    
    Here, \(S^1 = A \cup B\), where \(A, B \simeq \{*\}\) and \(A \cap B \simeq \{p, q\}\).
    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/10_13 (1).jpg}
    \end{center}
    
    Using this, we know \(\HH_*(A), \HH_*(B), \HH_*(A \cap B)\). For \(i \ge 2\), we have
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMSwwLCJcXHVuZGVyYnJhY2V7SF9pKEEpXFxvcGx1cyBIX2koQil9X3s9MH0iXSxbMiwwLCJIX2koWCkiXSxbMywwLCJcXHVuZGVyYnJhY2V7SF97aS0xfShBIFxcY2FwIEIpfV97PTB9Il0sWzAsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFs0LDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMywwXSxbMiw0XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\underbrace{\HH_i(A)\oplus \HH_i(B)}_{=0}} & {\HH_i(X)} & {\underbrace{\HH_{i-1}(A \cap B)}_{=0}} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    and so \(\HH_i(X) = 0\) for all \(i \ge 2\). For lowe degrees, we have
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNyxbMCwwLCJcXHVuZGVyYnJhY2V7SF8xKEEgXFxjYXAgQil9X3s9MH0iXSxbMSwwLCJcXHVuZGVyYnJhY2V7SF8xKEEpXFxvcGx1cyBIXzEoQil9X3s9MH0iXSxbMiwwLCJIXzEoWCkiXSxbMCwyLCJcXHVuZGVyYnJhY2V7SF8wKEEgXFxjYXAgQil9X3tcXGlubmVye3B9XFxvcGx1c1xcaW5uZXJ7cX19Il0sWzEsMiwiXFx1bmRlcmJyYWNle0hfMChBKVxcb3BsdXMgSF8wKEIpfV97PVxcWiBcXG9wbHVzIFxcWn0iXSxbMiwyLCJcXHVuZGVyYnJhY2V7SF8wKFgpfV97PVxcWn0iXSxbMywyLCIwIl0sWzUsNl0sWzQsNV0sWzMsNCwiXFxhbHBoYSIsMl0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMl0sWzIsMywiXFxwYXJ0aWFsX3tNVn0iXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\underbrace{\HH_1(A \cap B)}_{=0}} & {\underbrace{\HH_1(A)\oplus \HH_1(B)}_{=0}} & {\HH_1(X)} \\
	\\
	{\underbrace{\HH_0(A \cap B)}_{\inner{p}\oplus\inner{q}}} & {\underbrace{\HH_0(A)\oplus \HH_0(B)}_{=\Z \oplus \Z}} & {\underbrace{\HH_0(X)}_{=\Z}} & 0
	\arrow[from=3-3, to=3-4]
	\arrow[from=3-2, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\alpha"', from=3-1, to=3-2]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{\partial_{MV}}", from=1-3, to=3-1]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where \(\alpha(n, m) = (n+m, n+m)\). Moreover, we use that \(S^1\) is path connected, and \(\HH_0\) is generated by any point. Therefore, \(\HH_1(X)\) sits in an exact sequence
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxISF8xKFgpIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxaXjIiXSxbMywwLCJcXFpeMiJdLFs0LDAsIlxcWiJdLFs1LDAsIjAiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyLCJcXHBhcnRpYWxfe01WfSJdLFsyLDMsIlxcYWxwaGEiXSxbMyw0LCJcXGJldGEiXSxbNCw1XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {\HH_1(X)} & {\Z^2} & {\Z^2} & \Z & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["{\partial_{MV}}", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["\alpha", from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow["\beta", from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    and so,
    \[\HH_1(X) \simeq \ker(\alpha) = \inner{(1, -1)} = \Z(p - q)\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    \[\HH^*(S^n) = \HH_*(S^n) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & * = 0, n \\
        0 &\text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}\]
\end{example}

\lec

\begin{proof}

\[\begin{tikzcd}

If \(i > 0\), we have

This gives us almost everything inductively. We can assume \(n \ge 2\). At the bottom of the sequence, we have
