\begin{corollary}
    \(\R^n\) is homeomorphic to \(\R^m\) if and only if \(m = n\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose \(f : \R^n \to \R^m\) is a homeomorphism. This gives us a homeomorphism
    \[f : \R^n \setminus 0 \to \R^m \setminus \{f(0)\}\]
    But \(\R^n\setminus \{*\}\) is homotopy equivalent to \(\S^{n-1}\). Thus \(f\) induces an isomorphism in homology
    \[\HH_i(S^{n-1}) = \HH_i(S^{m-1})\]
    But by our homology computations, this implies that \(n = m\).
\end{proof}

Note in the below we assume \(n > 0\).

\begin{definition}
    [degree]
    Suppose \(f : S^n \to S^n\) is a continuous map, then \(f\) induces a map
    \[f_* : \HH_n(S^n) \to \HH_n(S^n)\]
    which is multiplication by some \(d \in \Z\). We call \(\deg(f) = d\) the \emph{degree}\index{degree!of a map \(S^n \to S^n\)} of \(f\).
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    Since \(\HH_n(S^n) = \Z\) which is free, therefore \(f_*\) is determined by \(f_*(1)\). Moreover, we will need to use the \emph{same} identification of \(\HH_n(S^n) = \Z\), and so \(\deg(f)\) is well defined. Note that if we use different isomorphisms, then \(\deg(f)\) is only defined up to a sign.
\end{remark}

Note that 
\begin{itemize}
    \item if \(f \simeq g\), then \(\deg(f) = \deg(g)\),
    \item \(\deg(\id) = 1\).
    \item \(\deg(\text{const}) = 0\), since the constant map factors through \(\{*\}\), and so the induced map on homology factors through \(\HH^n(*) = 0\).
\end{itemize}

\begin{lemma}
    If \(A \in O(n+1)\), then \(A\) acts on \(S^n\), and we have
    \[\deg(A) = \det(A) \in \{\pm1\}\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    The group \(O(n+1)\) has two path components. By homotopy invariance of degree, any \(A\) is homotopic to \(\id_{S^n}\), or a reflection in an equatorial hyperplane \(H\).

    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_16 lec (1).jpg}
    \end{center}

    The reflection preserves \(A, B, A \cap B\), and \(H\) intersects \(A \cap B = S^{n-1}\) at two points. The Mayer-Vietoris sequence gives
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsOCxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiSF9uKFNebikiXSxbMiwwLCJIX3tuLTF9KFNee24tMX0pIl0sWzMsMCwiMCJdLFswLDEsIjAiXSxbMSwxLCJIX24oU157bn0pIl0sWzIsMSwiSF97bi0xfShTXntuLTF9KSJdLFszLDEsIjAiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyLCJcXGNvbmciXSxbNSw2LCJcXGNvbmciLDJdLFs0LDVdLFsxLDUsIkFfKiIsMl0sWzIsNiwiQV8qIl0sWzYsN10sWzIsM11d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {\HH_n(S^n)} & {\HH_{n-1}(S^{n-1})} & 0 \\
	0 & {\HH_n(S^{n})} & {\HH_{n-1}(S^{n-1})} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["\cong", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["\cong"', from=2-2, to=2-3]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
	\arrow["{A_*}"', from=1-2, to=2-2]
	\arrow["{A_*}", from=1-3, to=2-3]
	\arrow[from=2-3, to=2-4]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    The two vertical maps are the same. Therefore it suffices to consider the result on \(S^1\). 
    
    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_16 lec (2).jpg}
    \end{center}

    Recall our computation of \(H_1(S^1)\) has
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiSF8xKFNeMSkiXSxbMiwwLCJIXzAocCBcXHNxY3VwIHEpIl0sWzMsMCwiSF8wKEEpXFxvcGx1cyBIXzAoQikiXSxbNCwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMl0sWzIsMywiXFxhbHBoYSJdLFszLDRdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {\HH_1(S^1)} & {\HH_0(p \sqcup q)} & {\HH_0(A)\oplus \HH_0(B)} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["\alpha", from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where \(\alpha(u, v) = (u + v, u + v)\). Hence \(H_1(S^1)\) is generated by \((1, -1)\), which we can think of as \(p - q\). But in this case, reflection in \(H\) swaps \(p\) and \(q\), and so it acts on \(H^1(S^1)\) by multiplication by \(-1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    The antipodal map \(a_n : S^n \to S^n\) has degree \((-1)^{n+1}\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    \(a_n\) is orthogonal. Or equivalently, it is a composition of \(n+1\) reflections, and we have that
    \[\deg(fg) = \deg(f)\deg(g)\]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    If \(f : S^n \to S^n\) has no fixed point, then \(f\) is homotopic to the antipodal map.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    In fact, we will see that \(f(x) \ne g(x)\) for all \(x \in S^n\), then \(f \simeq a_n \circ g\). Taking \(g = \id\) gives the result. Consider the map

    \[x \mapsto \frac{tf(x) - (1-t)g(x)}{\norm{tf(x) - (1-t)g(x)}}\]
    for \(0 \le t \le 1\). Note that the denominator never vanishes. This then defines a homotopy from \(f\) to \(-g\).
\end{proof}

Using this, the degree of any map without fixed points is \((-1)^{n+1}\). Another corollary is that if \(f(x) \ne -x\) for all \(x \in S^n\), then \(f \simeq a \circ a = \id\).

\begin{definition}
    [vector field on \(\S^n\)]
    A \emph{vector field on \(S^n\)}\index{vector field on \(S^n\)} is a map \(v : S^n \to \R^{n+1}\), such that for all \(x \in S^n\),
    \[\inner{x, v(x)} = 0\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    [Hairy ball theorem] \(S^n\) has a nowhere vanishing vector field if and only if \(n\) is odd.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    If \(n = 2k-1\),
    \[v(x_1, y_1, \dots, x_k, y_k) = (-y_1, x_2, \dots, -y_k, x_k)\]
    works.

    \lec

    Now suppose a nowhere vanishing vector field \(v : S^n \to \R^{n+1}\) exists. By normalising, i.e. considering
    \[\frac{v(x)}{\norm{v(x)}}\]
    we can consider \(v : S^n \to S^n \sse \R^{n+1}\). Now consider the family
    \[v_t(x) = \cos(t)x + \sin(t)v(x)\]
    for \(0 \le t \le \pi\), which has unit length as we assumed \(v(x)\) and \(x\) are orthogonal. This family has \(v_0 = \id\), and \(v_\pi = a_n\). Thus, we have that
    \[1 = \deg(\id) = \deg(a_n) = (-1)^{n+1}\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    [Klein bottle]

    Recall the Klein bottle is the result if we glue two M\"obius strips together along their common boundary \(S^1\). That is, we define \(K\) by the gluing pattern

    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_18 (1).jpg}
    \end{center}

    In this case, we can write \(K = A \cup B\), where \(A, B\) are M\"obius bands, i.e. \(A \simeq S^1 \simeq B\). Moreover, \(A \cap B \simeq S^1\) as well. The interesting part of the Mayer-Vietoris sequence for homology is as follows:
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNyxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxISF8yKEspIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF8xKEEgXFxjYXAgQikiXSxbMywwLCJcXEhIXzEoQSlcXG9wbHVzXFxISF8xKEIpIl0sWzQsMCwiXFxISF8xKEspIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxISF8wKEEgXFxjYXAgQikiXSxbNiwwLCJcXEhIXzAoQSkgXFxvcGx1cyBcXEhIXzAoQikiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzLCJcXHBzaSJdLFszLDRdLFs0LDVdLFs1LDYsIlxcYWxwaGEiXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {\HH_2(K)} & {\HH_1(A \cap B)} & {\HH_1(A)\oplus\HH_1(B)} & {\HH_1(K)} & {\HH_0(A \cap B)} & {\HH_0(A) \oplus \HH_0(B)}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["\psi", from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
	\arrow["\alpha", from=1-6, to=1-7]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    Recall \(\alpha(1) = (1, 1)\), and so \(\alpha\) is injective, and the sequence becomes
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxISF8yKEspIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF8xKEEgXFxjYXAgQikiXSxbMywwLCJcXEhIXzEoQSlcXG9wbHVzXFxISF8xKEIpIl0sWzQsMCwiXFxISF8xKEspIl0sWzUsMCwiMCJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDMsIlxccHNpIl0sWzMsNF0sWzQsNV1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {\HH_2(K)} & {\HH_1(A \cap B)} & {\HH_1(A)\oplus\HH_1(B)} & {\HH_1(K)} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["\psi", from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    By exactness, \(\HH_1(K) \cong (\Z \oplus \Z)/\im(\psi) = \coker(\psi)\) and \(\HH_2(K) \cong \ker(\psi)\).

    \begin{claim}
        \(\psi(1) = (2, 2)\), and so \(\HH_2(K) = 0\), and \(\HH_1(K) = \Z \oplus \Z/2\).
    \end{claim}

    \begin{proof}
        [Proof of claim]

        We will use the decomposition of \(K\) into two M\"obius bands.

        \begin{center}
            \includegraphics[scale=0.15]{diagrams/2023_10_18 (2).jpg}
        \end{center}

        In this case, \(\HH_1(A \cap B) = \Z\inner{v + w}\), and \(\HH_1(A) = \Z\inner{\sigma_1 + \sigma_2}\). At chain level, there exists a collection of \(2\)-simplices in \(A\), with boundary \(c - (\sigma_1 + \sigma_2)\). Therefore, \(v \mapsto \sigma_1 + \sigma_2\) and \(w \mapsto \sigma_1 + \sigma_2\). With this, we have that (by symmetry),
        \[\psi(1) = (2, 2)\]
    \end{proof}
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item In cohomology, \(\HH^2(K) = \Z/2\) and \(\HH^1(K) = \Z\). That is, the homology and the cohomology are not the same.
        \item We defined \(C_i(X) = \left\{\sum_{\text{finite}} a_j \sigma_j \mid a_j \in \Z, \sigma_j \text{ is an }i\text{-simplex}\right\}\). But we can also define
        \[C_i(X; G) = \left\{\sum_{\text{finite}} a_j \sigma_j \ \big\vert\ a_j \in G, \sigma_j \text{ is an }i\text{-simplex}\right\}\]
        for any abelian group \(G\). \(\partial\) is defined as before, and we get a chain complex \(C_*(X; G)\) and homology \(\HH_*(X;G)\). If we instead computed
        \[\HH_*(K; \Z/2) = \begin{cases}
            \Z/2 & * = 0 \\
            \Z/2 \oplus \Z/2 & * = 1 \\
            \Z/2 & * = 2
        \end{cases}\]
        since \(\psi = 0\) if we used \(\Z/2\) coefficients.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{Interlude on relative homology}
Some of the questions on sheet 1 concern \emph{relative homology}\index{relative homology}. If \(A \sse X\) is a subspace, then any simplex \(\sigma : \Delta^1 \to A\) is a simplex in \(X\). Moreover, if \(\sigma\) is a simplex in \(A\), so are all of its boundary faces. Thus, the natural map induced by the inclusion map,
\[C_i(A) \inj C_i(X)\]
defines a chain map. Using this, \(C_*(A)\) is a \emph{subcomplex} of \(C_*(X)\). In particular, we can define the \emph{quotient complex},
\[C_i(X, A) = \frac{C_i(X)}{C_i(A)}\]
and this is a chain complex, with \(\partial\) induced by the boundary operator on \(C_i(X)\). Another way of thinking about \(C_i(X, A)\) is that it is the free abelian group on \(i\)-simplices in \(X\) not wholly contained in \(A\). The homology of \(C_*(X, A)\), denoted \(\HH_*(X, A)\) is called \emph{relative homology}.

We have a long exact sequence
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMCwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxISF9pKEEpIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF9pKFgpIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxISF9pKFgsQSkiXSxbNCwwLCJcXEhIX3tpLTF9KEEpIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDQsIlxccGFydGlhbCIsMl0sWzQsNV1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH_i(A)} & {\HH_i(X)} & {\HH_i(X,A)} & {\HH_{i-1}(A)} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow["\partial"', from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
\end{tikzcd}\]

If \(A\) and \(X\) are `well behaved', then \(\HH_i(X, A) \cong \HH_i(X/A)\) for \(i > 0\), where \(X/A\) is the quotient space where we quotient \(A\) into a point.

\subsection{Homotopy invariance}

Recall if \(f, g : X \to Y\) are homotopic, we would like to show that the induced maps on homology and cohomology are the same. Recall also that the maps are induced by (co)chain maps on (co)chain complexes.

\begin{definition}
    [chain homotopic]
    Let \(C_*, D_*\) be chain complexes. Let \(f_*, g_* : C_* \to D_*\) be chain maps. We say that they are \emph{chain homotopic}\index{chain homotopic} if there exists maps
    \[P_n : C_n \to D_{n+1}\]
    for all \(n\), such that
    \[P_{n-1}\circ \partial + \partial \circ P_n = f_n - g_n\]
    for all \(n\).
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMCwwLCJDX3tuKzF9Il0sWzIsMCwiQ19uIl0sWzQsMCwiQ197bi0xfSJdLFsyLDIsIkRfbiJdLFswLDIsIkRfe24rMX0iXSxbNCwyLCJEX3tuLTF9Il0sWzEsNCwiUF9uIiwyLHsiY29sb3VyIjpbMjQwLDYwLDYwXX0sWzI0MCw2MCw2MCwxXV0sWzIsMywiUF97bi0xfSIsMix7ImNvbG91ciI6WzI0MCw2MCw2MF19LFsyNDAsNjAsNjAsMV1dLFsxLDIsIlxccGFydGlhbCJdLFswLDEsIlxccGFydGlhbCJdLFs0LDMsIlxccGFydGlhbCIsMl0sWzMsNSwiXFxwYXJ0aWFsIiwyXSxbMSwzLCJmX24gLSBnX24iLDFdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{C_{n+1}} && {C_n} && {C_{n-1}} \\
	\\
	{D_{n+1}} && {D_n} && {D_{n-1}}
	\arrow["{P_n}"', color={rgb,255:red,92;green,92;blue,214}, from=1-3, to=3-1]
	\arrow["{P_{n-1}}"', color={rgb,255:red,92;green,92;blue,214}, from=1-5, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\partial", from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow["\partial", from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow["\partial"', from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\partial"', from=3-3, to=3-5]
	\arrow["{f_n - g_n}"{description}, from=1-3, to=3-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    Suppose \(f_*, g_* : C_* \to D_*\) are chain homotopic. Then they induce the same map on homology.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Let \(\alpha \in C_i\) be a cycle, that is, \(\partial \alpha = 0\). By definition, \(f_*[\alpha] = [f(\alpha)]\). Consider
    \[f_i(\alpha) - g_i(\alpha) = (f_i - g_i)(\alpha) = (\partial P + P\partial)(\alpha) = \partial(P\alpha)\]
    But this is in the image of \(\partial\), and so the corresponding homology classes are the same.
\end{proof}

\lec

\underline{Exercise:} Chain homotopy is an equivalence relation on chain maps \(C_* \to D_*\).

\begin{theorem}
    [homotopy invariance of homology]
    \index{homotopy invariance!of (co)homology!proof}
    If \(f, g : X \to Y\) are homotopic, then the induced maps on homology are the same.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Let \(i_j : X \inj X \times \{j\}\) be the natural map, \(F : X \times I \to Y\) the homotopy between \(f, g\). But then
    \[f_* = (F \circ i_0)_* = F_* \circ i_{0*}\]

    Therefore, it suffices to show that \(i_{0*}\) and \(i_{1*}\) are chain homotopic.

    Thus, we want
    \[P : C_n(X)  \to C_{n+1}(X \times [0, 1])\]
    such that \(\partial P + P \partial = i_{0*} - i_{1*}\). \(P\) is a \emph{prism operator}\index{prism operator} from a universal way of decomposing \(\Delta^n \times [0, 1]\) into a  finite collection of \(n+1\)-simplices.

    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.15]{diagrams/2023_10_20 (3).jpg}
    \end{center}

    Consider the ordered collections\([v_0, \dots, v_i, w_i, \dots, w_n]\), where \([v_0,\dots, v_n] = \Delta^n \times \{0\}\) and \([w_0, \dots, w_n] = \Delta^n \times \{1\}\). We define \(P\) by
    \[P(\sigma) = \sum_{i=0}^n (-1)^i(\sigma \times I)\vert_{[v_0, \dots, v_i, w_i, \dots, w_n]}\]

    \begin{claim}
        \(\partial P + P \partial = i_{i*} - i_{0*}\). That is,
        \[\partial P = i_{1*} - i_{0*} - P\partial\]
        i.e. ``boundary of prism is the top and bottom faces, as well as the prism of the boundary''.
    \end{claim}

    \begin{proof}
        \begin{align*}
            \partial P(\sigma) = \sum_{j \le i}(-1)^i(-1)^j(\sigma \times I)\vert_{[v_0, \dots, \widehat{v_j}, \dots, v_i, w_i, \dots, w_n]} + \sum_{j \ge i}(-1)^i(-1)^{j+1}(\sigma \times I)\vert_{[v_0, \dots, v_i, w_i, \dots, \widehat{w_j}, \dots, w_n]}
        \end{align*}
        The terms when \(j = i\) cancel, except for
        \[(\sigma \times I) \vert_{[w_0, \dots, w_n]} - (\sigma \times I)\vert_{[v_0, \dots, v_n]}\]
        which are the top and bottom faces. The terms with \(i > j\) and \(i < j\) comprise, up to a sign, \(P(\partial \sigma)\). Just compute (in a dark room with some gin).
    \end{proof}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    If \(C^*, D^*\) are cochain complexes, cochain maps \(f^*, g^*\) are cochain homotopic if we have \(P^i : C^i \to D^{i-1}\), such that
    \[\partial P + P\partial = f^* - g^*\]
    It is easy to check that in this case, \(f^*\) and \(g^*\) induce the saem map \(\HH^*(C^*) \to \HH^*(D^*)\).

    With this, our prism operator \(P : C_n(X) \to C_{n+1}(X \times [0, 1])\) has a dual
    \begin{align*}
        P^* : C^{n+1}(X \times [0, 1]) &\to C^n(X) \\
        P^*(f) &= f \circ P
    \end{align*}

    Dualising everything, the relation \(\partial P + P\partial = i_{1*} - i_{0*}\) becomes
    \[\partial P^* + P^*\partial = i_1^* - i_0^*\]
    and thus homotopic maps induce the same map on cohomology.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Snake lemma}

\begin{definition}
    A \emph{short exact sequence of chain complexes}\index{short exact sequence!of chain complexes}
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiQV8qIl0sWzIsMCwiQl8qIl0sWzMsMCwiQ18qIl0sWzQsMCwiMCJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDRdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {A_*} & {B_*} & {C_*} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    is a diagram
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMjEsWzEsMiwiQV9uIl0sWzEsMSwiQV97bisxfSJdLFsxLDMsIkFfe24tMX0iXSxbMSw0LCJcXHZkb3RzIl0sWzEsMCwiXFx2ZG90cyJdLFsyLDAsIlxcdmRvdHMiXSxbMiwxLCJCX3tuKzF9Il0sWzIsMiwiQl9uIl0sWzIsMywiQl97bi0xfSJdLFsyLDQsIlxcdmRvdHMiXSxbMywxLCJDX3tuKzF9Il0sWzMsMCwiXFx2ZG90cyJdLFszLDIsIkNfbiJdLFszLDMsIkNfe24tMX0iXSxbMyw0LCJcXHZkb3RzIl0sWzAsMSwiMCJdLFswLDIsIjAiXSxbMCwzLCIwIl0sWzQsMSwiMCJdLFs0LDIsIjAiXSxbNCwzLCIwIl0sWzIsM10sWzQsMV0sWzgsOV0sWzUsNl0sWzEzLDE0XSxbMTIsMTNdLFsxMCwxMl0sWzExLDEwXSxbMSwwXSxbMCwyXSxbNiw3XSxbNyw4XSxbMSw2LCJcXGFscGhhIiwxXSxbNiwxMCwiXFxiZXRhIiwxXSxbMCw3LCJcXGFscGhhIiwxXSxbNywxMiwiXFxiZXRhIiwxXSxbMiw4LCJcXGFscGhhIiwxXSxbOCwxMywiXFxiZXRhIiwxXSxbMTUsMV0sWzE2LDBdLFsxNywyXSxbMTMsMjBdLFsxMiwxOV0sWzEwLDE4XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	& \vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\
	0 & {A_{n+1}} & {B_{n+1}} & {C_{n+1}} & 0 \\
	0 & {A_n} & {B_n} & {C_n} & 0 \\
	0 & {A_{n-1}} & {B_{n-1}} & {C_{n-1}} & 0 \\
	& \vdots & \vdots & \vdots
	\arrow[from=4-2, to=5-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=2-2]
	\arrow[from=4-3, to=5-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=2-3]
	\arrow[from=4-4, to=5-4]
	\arrow[from=3-4, to=4-4]
	\arrow[from=2-4, to=3-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=2-4]
	\arrow[from=2-2, to=3-2]
	\arrow[from=3-2, to=4-2]
	\arrow[from=2-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow[from=3-3, to=4-3]
	\arrow["\alpha"{description}, from=2-2, to=2-3]
	\arrow["\beta"{description}, from=2-3, to=2-4]
	\arrow["\alpha"{description}, from=3-2, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\beta"{description}, from=3-3, to=3-4]
	\arrow["\alpha"{description}, from=4-2, to=4-3]
	\arrow["\beta"{description}, from=4-3, to=4-4]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
	\arrow[from=3-1, to=3-2]
	\arrow[from=4-1, to=4-2]
	\arrow[from=4-4, to=4-5]
	\arrow[from=3-4, to=3-5]
	\arrow[from=2-4, to=2-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    such that the squares commute (i.e. \(\alpha\), \(\beta\) are chain maps) and the rows are exact.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    [Snake lemma]
    \index{snake lemma}
    Suppose we have a SES \(0 \to A_* \to B_* \to C_* \to 0\) of chain complexes, then we have a long exact sequence of homology
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNyxbMCwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxISF97bisxfShDXyopIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF9uKEFfKikiXSxbMywwLCJcXEhIX24oQl8qKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhfbihDXyopIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxISF97bi0xfShBXyopIl0sWzYsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDIsIlxcZGVsdGEiXSxbMiwzLCJcXGFscGhhXyoiXSxbMyw0LCJcXGJldGFfKiJdLFs0LDUsIlxcZGVsdGEiXSxbNSw2XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH_{n+1}(C_*)} & {\HH_n(A_*)} & {\HH_n(B_*)} & {\HH_n(C_*)} & {\HH_{n-1}(A_*)} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["\delta", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{\alpha_*}", from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow["{\beta_*}", from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow["\delta", from=1-5, to=1-6]
	\arrow[from=1-6, to=1-7]
\end{tikzcd}\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    We will construct \(\delta\), but leave all other details. Suppose \(c_n \in C_n\) is a cycle, representing a homology class \([c_n] \in H_n(C^*)\). Horizontal exactness means \(c_n = \beta(b_n)\) for some \(b_n \in B_n\). But
    \[\beta(\partial b_n) = \partial\beta(b_n) = \partial c_n = 0\]
    Hence \(\partial b_n \in \ker(\beta) = \im(\alpha)\), by horizontal exactness. Thus, we have \(a_{n-1} \in A_{n-1}\) such that \(\alpha(a_{n-1}) = \partial b_n\). In this case,
    \[\alpha(\partial a_{n-1}) = \partial \alpha(a_{n-1}) = \partial^2 b_n = 0\]
    But \(\alpha\) is injective, and so \(\partial a_{n-1} = 0\), and this represents a cycle.

    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsOSxbMCwwLCJBX24iXSxbMiwwLCJCX24iXSxbNCwwLCJDX24iXSxbMCwyLCJBX3tuLTF9Il0sWzIsMiwiQl97bi0xfSJdLFs0LDIsIkNfe24tMX0iXSxbMCw0LCJBX3tuLTJ9Il0sWzIsNCwiQl97bi0yfSJdLFs0LDQsIkNfe24tMn0iXSxbMSwyLCJcXGJldGEiLDAseyJzdHlsZSI6eyJoZWFkIjp7Im5hbWUiOiJlcGkifX19XSxbMSw0LCJcXHBhcnRpYWwiLDJdLFszLDQsIlxcYWxwaGEiLDAseyJzdHlsZSI6eyJ0YWlsIjp7Im5hbWUiOiJob29rIiwic2lkZSI6InRvcCJ9fX1dLFs2LDcsIlxcYWxwaGEiLDAseyJzdHlsZSI6eyJ0YWlsIjp7Im5hbWUiOiJob29rIiwic2lkZSI6InRvcCJ9fX1dLFs0LDcsIlxccGFydGlhbCJdLFszLDYsIlxccGFydGlhbCIsMl0sWzIsNSwiXFxwYXJ0aWFsIl0sWzQsNSwiXFxiZXRhIiwwLHsic3R5bGUiOnsiaGVhZCI6eyJuYW1lIjoiZXBpIn19fV1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{A_n} && {B_n} && {C_n} \\
	\\
	{A_{n-1}} && {B_{n-1}} && {C_{n-1}} \\
	\\
	{A_{n-2}} && {B_{n-2}} && {C_{n-2}}
	\arrow["\beta", two heads, from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow["\partial"', from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\alpha", hook, from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\alpha", hook, from=5-1, to=5-3]
	\arrow["\partial", from=3-3, to=5-3]
	\arrow["\partial"', from=3-1, to=5-1]
	\arrow["\partial", from=1-5, to=3-5]
	\arrow["\beta", two heads, from=3-3, to=3-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    We define \(\delta([c_n]) = [a_{n-1}]\).

    To complete the proof:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Check that \(\delta\) is independent of the choices of \(c_n, b_n, a_{n-1}\).
        \item \(\delta\) is a homomorphism.
        \item Check that the sequence is exact.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    Recall if \(G\) is an abelian group, we introducted \(C_*(X; G)\). If we started with an SES of abelian groups
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiR18xIl0sWzIsMCwiR18yIl0sWzMsMCwiR18zIl0sWzQsMCwiMCJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDRdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {G_1} & {G_2} & {G_3} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    then there is an associated SES of chain complexes
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiQ18qKFgsIEdfMSkiXSxbMiwwLCJDXyooWCwgR18yKSJdLFszLDAsIkNfKihYLCBHXzMpIl0sWzQsMCwiMCJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDRdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {C_*(X; G_1)} & {C_*(X; G_2)} & {C_*(X; G_3)} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    The homomorphism
    \[\HH_n(X; G_3) \to \HH_{n-1}(X; G_1)\]
    are often called \emph{Bockstein homomorphisms}\index{Bockstein homomorphism}.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    If \(A \sse X\) is a subspace, then we defined relative homology, using the SES
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiQ18qKEEpIl0sWzIsMCwiQ18qKFgpIl0sWzMsMCwiQ18qKFgsQSkiXSxbNCwwLCIwIl0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMl0sWzIsM10sWzMsNF1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {C_*(A)} & {C_*(X)} & {C_*(X,A)} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    The associated long exact sequence includes a boundary map
    \[\delta : \HH_n(X, A) \to \HH_{n-1}(A)\]
    and we have a LES of the pair \((X, A)\).
\end{example}

\subsection{Excision}

\begin{theorem}
    [Excision] \index{excision}\label{thm:excision} Let \(X\) be a topological space, \(A \sse X\) a subspace, \(Z \sse X\) a subspace with \(\Cl(Z) \sse \Int(A)\). Then the inclusion
    \[i : (X\setminus \Z, A \setminus Z) \to (X, A)\]
    induces an isomorphism on relative homology
    \[i_* : \HH_n(X \setminus Z, A \setminus Z) \to \HH_n(X, A)\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}
    [five lemma] \index{five lemma} Suppose we have a diagram
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTAsWzAsMCwiQSJdLFsxLDAsIkIiXSxbMiwwLCJDIl0sWzMsMCwiRCJdLFs0LDAsIkUiXSxbMCwxLCJBJyJdLFsxLDEsIkInIl0sWzIsMSwiQyciXSxbMywxLCJEJyJdLFs0LDEsIkUnIl0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMl0sWzIsM10sWzMsNF0sWzQsOSwiXFxlcHNpbG9uIiwxXSxbMyw4LCJcXGRlbHRhIiwxXSxbMiw3LCJcXGdhbW1hIiwxXSxbMSw2LCJcXGJldGEiLDFdLFswLDUsIlxcYWxwaGEiLDFdLFs1LDZdLFs2LDddLFs3LDhdLFs4LDldXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	A & B & C & D & E \\
	{A'} & {B'} & {C'} & {D'} & {E'}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow["\epsilon"{description}, from=1-5, to=2-5]
	\arrow["\delta"{description}, from=1-4, to=2-4]
	\arrow["\gamma"{description}, from=1-3, to=2-3]
	\arrow["\beta"{description}, from=1-2, to=2-2]
	\arrow["\alpha"{description}, from=1-1, to=2-1]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
	\arrow[from=2-2, to=2-3]
	\arrow[from=2-3, to=2-4]
	\arrow[from=2-4, to=2-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where the rows are exact, \(\alpha,\beta, \delta,\epsilon\) are isomorphisms, then so is \(\delta\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{corollary}
    If \(f : (X, A) \to (Y, B)\) is a map of pairs, and the maps \(f_* : \HH_*(X) \to |HH_*(Y)\) and \(f_* : \HH_*(A) \to \HH_*(B)\) are isomorphisms, then so are the induced maps on relative homology
    \[f_* : \HH_*(X, A) \to \HH_*(Y, B)\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    Follows from the five lemma and the LES of relative homology. 
\end{proof}

\lec

\subsection{Relative homology}

\begin{definition}
    [Reduced homology] If \(X\) is a topological space, \(x_0 \in X\) is a base point, then we define the \emph{reduced homology of \(X\)}\index{reduced homology, \(\tilde \HH_*(X)\)} as
    \[\tilde \HH_*(X) = \HH_*(X, x_0)\]
    It is easy to see that \(\tilde \HH_i(X) = \HH_i(X)\) for \(i > 0\), and \(\tilde\HH_i(X) \oplus \Z = \HH_i(X)\). 
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    [good pair]
    A pair \((X, A)\) is \emph{good}\index{good pair} if \(A \sse X\) is closed, and \(A\) a neighbourhood deformation retract. That is, there exists an open neighbourhood \(A \sse U \sse X\) of \(A\), and a homotopy \(H : U \times [0, 1] \to U\), with
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \(H(u, 0) = u\),
        \item \(H(u, 1) \in A\) for all \(u \in U\),
        \item \(H(a, t) = a\) for all \(a \in A, t \in [0, 1]\).
    \end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    One example would be a loop on \(\Sigma_2\), where we can choose an annular neighbourhood.

    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_23 (1).jpg}
    \end{center}

    On the other hand, consider

    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_23 (2).jpg}
    \end{center}

    There is no neighbourhood retract fixing \(A\).
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}
    If \((X, A)\) is a good pair, the natural map \((X, A) \to (X/A, A/A = \{\bullet\})\), induces isomorphism
    \[\HH_n(X, A) \to \HH_n(X/A, A/A) = \tilde \HH_*(X/A)\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Let \(A \sse U \sse X\) be as in the definition of a good pair, and note that \(\HH_*(A) = \HH_*(U)\), and as such, we have an isomorphism on relative homology
    \[\HH_*(X, A) \cong \HH_*(X, U)\]
    Since the homotopy \(H : U \times [0, 1] \to U\) is fixed on \(A\), it induces a homotopy on \(U/A\), and so
    \[\frac{A}{A} = \{\bullet\} \inj \frac{U}{A}\]
    is also a neighbourhood deformation retract. With this,
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMCwwLCJIXyooWCwgQSkiXSxbMSwwLCJIXyooWCxVKSJdLFsyLDAsIkhfKihYL0EsIFUvQSkiXSxbMCwyLCJIXyooWC9BLCBcXGJ1bGxldCkiXSxbMSwyLCJIXyooWC9BLCBVL0EpIl0sWzIsMiwiSF8qKChYL0EpL1xcYnVsbGV0LCAoVS9BKS9cXGJ1bGxldCkiXSxbMCwzXSxbMiw1LCJcXHNpbVxcdGV4dHtob21lb21vcnBoaXNtIG9mIHBhaXJzfSJdLFswLDEsIlxcY29uZyIsMV0sWzIsMSwiXFxzaW0iLDFdLFs1LDQsIlxcc2ltIiwxXSxbMyw0LCJcXGNvbmciLDFdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{H_*(X, A)} & {H_*(X,U)} & {H_*(X/A, U/A)} \\
	\\
	{H_*(X/A, \bullet)} & {H_*(X/A, U/A)} & {H_*((X/A)/\bullet, (U/A)/\bullet)}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow["{\sim\text{homeomorphism of pairs}}", from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\cong"{description}, from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["\sim"{description}, from=1-3, to=1-2]
	\arrow["\sim"{description}, from=3-3, to=3-2]
	\arrow["\cong"{description}, from=3-1, to=3-2]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    and so the left map is an isomorphism. Note \(\sim\) is an isomorphism by excision, and \(\cong\) is an isomorphism by the above, from the homotopy.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item \(\HH_j(D^n, \partial D^n) = \tilde \HH_j(D^n/\partial D^n) = \tilde \HH_j(S^n) = \begin{cases}
            \Z & j = n \\
            0 & \text{otherwise}
        \end{cases}\)
        \item \(\HH_j(S^2, S^1_{\text{eq}}) = \tilde \HH_j(S^2 \vee S^2) = \begin{cases}
            \Z\oplus \Z & j = 2 \\
            0 & \text{otherwise}
        \end{cases}\)
        Since we can think of \(S^2/S^1\) as \(S^2 \vee S^2\) via
        \begin{center}
            \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_23 (4).jpg}
        \end{center}
        \item A \emph{manifold} of dimension \(n\) is a Hausdorff topological space which is locally homeomorphic to \(\R^n\). If \(M^n\) is a manifold, \(x \in M\), then
        \[\HH_j(M, M \setminus x) \underbrace{=}_{\text{by excision}} \HH_j(\R^n, \R^n \setminus 0) \underbrace{=}_{\text{by homotopy invariance}} \HH_j(D^n, \partial D^n) = \Z \text{ when j = n}\]
        For excision, we choose an open set \(U\) which is homeomorphic to a ball, and remove the complement of \(U\).
        \begin{center}
            \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_23 (4).jpg}
        \end{center}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\subsection{Small simplices theorem}

Both the Mayer-Vietoris property and excision will follow from the \emph{small simplices theorem}.

\begin{definition}
    [chain complex adapted to a cover]
    Let \(X\) be a topological space,
    \[\mcU = \left\{U_\alpha\right\}_{\alpha \in I}\]
    be such that \(\{\Int(U_\alpha)\}_{\alpha \in I}\) is an open cover of \(X\). Let
    \[C_j^\mcU(X) = \left\{\sum_\text{finite} a_j \sigma_i \ \big\vert\ a_i \in \Z, \sigma_i : \Delta^i \to X, \im(\sigma_i) \sse U_{\alpha(\sigma_i)} \text{ for some }\alpha(\sigma_i) \in I\right\}\]
    This defines a subcomplex of \(C_*(X)\) with the usual boundary map.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    [small simplices] \index{small simplices theorem} The inclusion \(C_*^\mcU(X) \inj C_*(X)\) induces an isomorphism on homology.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
    If \(X, Y\) are spaces with covers \(\mcU, \mcV\), and if \(f : X \to Y\) sends each \(U_\alpha\) to some \(V_{f(\alpha)}\), then \(f\) induces a map
    \[f_* : C_j^\mcU(X) \to C_*^\mcV(Y)\] 
    But \((X, \mcU) \mapsto C_*^\mcU(X)\) is \emph{not} functorial for arbitrary maps.
\end{remark}

We will use small simplices to prove Mayer-Vietoris and excision.

\begin{proof}
    [Proof of Mayer-Vietoris, \cref{thm:mayer-vietoris}]
    \index{Mayer-Vietoris!proof}
    Let \(\mcU = \{A, B\}\), where \(A, B \sse X\) open. Then we have an SES of chain complexes
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiQ18qKEEgXFxjYXAgQikiXSxbMiwwLCJDXyooQSlcXG9wbHVzIENfKihCKSJdLFszLDAsIkNfKl5cXG1jVShYKSJdLFs0LDAsIjAiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXSxbMyw0XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {C_*(A \cap B)} & {C_*(A)\oplus C_*(B)} & {C_*^\mcU(X)} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    The last map is surjective because we have taken the subgroup \(C_*^\mcU(X)\). By the Snake lemma and the small simplices theorem, we get the Mayer-Vietoris sequence.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item The boundary map is what we wrote down in the special case earlier.
        \item The naturality of Mayer-Vietoris is the naturality of the map
        \[C_*^\mcU(X) \to C_*^\mcV(Y)\]
        where \(\mcU = \{A, B\}, \mcV = \{C, D\}\) and \(f(A) \sse C, f(B) \sse D\).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
    [Proof of excision, \cref{thm:excision}]

    \index{excision!proof}
    Recall we have \(Z \sse A \sse X\), \(\Cl(Z) \sse \Int(A)\). Let \(B = X \setminus Z\), \(\mcU = \{A, B\}\). Then \(\Int(A) \cup \Int(B) = X\), and so we can apply the small simplices theorem.

    Consider
    \[\frac{C_n^{\mcU}(X)}{C_n(A)} = \frac{C_n(B)}{C_n(A \cap B)} = \text{free ab. group of simplices in }B\text{ not wholly contained in }A\]
    and so we have the short exact sequences
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTAsWzAsMCwiMCJdLFsxLDAsIkNfKihBKSJdLFsyLDAsIkNfKl5cXG1jVShYKSJdLFszLDAsIkNfKl57XFxtY1V9KFgpL0NfKihBKSJdLFs0LDAsIjAiXSxbMCwxLCIwIl0sWzEsMSwiQ18qKEEpIl0sWzIsMSwiQ18qKEEpIl0sWzMsMSwiQ18qKFgpL0NfKihBKSJdLFs0LDEsIjAiXSxbMSw2LCIiLDEseyJzdHlsZSI6eyJ0YWlsIjp7Im5hbWUiOiJob29rIiwic2lkZSI6InRvcCJ9fX1dLFsyLDcsIiIsMSx7InN0eWxlIjp7InRhaWwiOnsibmFtZSI6Imhvb2siLCJzaWRlIjoidG9wIn19fV0sWzMsOCwiIiwxLHsic3R5bGUiOnsidGFpbCI6eyJuYW1lIjoiaG9vayIsInNpZGUiOiJ0b3AifX19XSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXSxbMyw0XSxbNSw2XSxbNiw3XSxbNyw4XSxbOCw5XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {C_*(A)} & {C_*^\mcU(X)} & {C_*^{\mcU}(X)/C_*(A)} & 0 \\
	0 & {C_*(A)} & {C_*(A)} & {C_*(X)/C_*(A)} & 0
	\arrow[hook, from=1-2, to=2-2]
	\arrow[hook, from=1-3, to=2-3]
	\arrow[hook, from=1-4, to=2-4]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
	\arrow[from=2-2, to=2-3]
	\arrow[from=2-3, to=2-4]
	\arrow[from=2-4, to=2-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where the vertical maps are induced by the inclusion, and all squares commute. Therefore, we have long exact sequences in homology and natural maps between them,
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTQsWzEsMCwiSF9pKEEpIl0sWzIsMCwiSF9pKENfKl5cXG1jVShYKSkiXSxbMywwLCJIX2koQ18qXlxcbWNVKFgpL0NfKihBKSJdLFs0LDAsIkhfe2ktMX0oQSkiXSxbMSwxLCJIX2koQSkiXSxbMiwxLCJIX2koWCkiXSxbMywxLCJIX2koWCwgQSkiXSxbNCwxLCJIX3tpLTF9KEEpIl0sWzUsMCwiSF97aS0xfShDXypeXFxtY1UoWCkpIl0sWzUsMSwiSF97aS0xfShYKSJdLFs2LDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbNiwxLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzAsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFswLDEsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMyw4XSxbNyw5XSxbOCwxMF0sWzksMTFdLFs4LDksIlxcc2ltIiwxXSxbMyw3LCI9IiwxXSxbMTIsMF0sWzEzLDRdLFswLDQsIj0iLDFdLFswLDFdLFs0LDVdLFsxLDUsIlxcc2ltIiwxXSxbMSwyXSxbNSw2XSxbMiw2XSxbNiw3XSxbMiwzXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH_i(A)} & {\HH_i(C_*^\mcU(X))} & {\HH_i(C_*^\mcU(X)/C_*(A)} & {\HH_{i-1}(A)} & {\HH_{i-1}(C_*^\mcU(X))} & \cdots \\
	\cdots & {\HH_i(A)} & {\HH_i(X)} & {\HH_i(X, A)} & {\HH_{i-1}(A)} & {\HH_{i-1}(X)} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
	\arrow[from=2-5, to=2-6]
	\arrow[from=1-6, to=1-7]
	\arrow[from=2-6, to=2-7]
	\arrow["\sim"{description}, from=1-6, to=2-6]
	\arrow["{=}"{description}, from=1-5, to=2-5]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
	\arrow["{=}"{description}, from=1-2, to=2-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=2-2, to=2-3]
	\arrow["\sim"{description}, from=1-3, to=2-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=2-3, to=2-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=2-4]
	\arrow[from=2-4, to=2-5]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where \(=\) are identity maps, \(\sim\) is an isomorphism by the small simplices theorem, and so the middle map is an isomorphism by the five lemma. But we have that
    \[\HH_i(X, A) = \HH_i(C_*(X)/C_*(A)) = \HH_i(C_*(B)/C_*(A \cap B)) = \HH_i(B, A \cap B) = \HH_*(X \setminus Z, A \setminus Z)\]
\end{proof}

Small simplices uses \emph{barycentric subdivision}\index{barycentric subdivision}.

\lec

If \(\Delta^n\) is the standard simplex, let \(b_n = \frac{1}{n+1}(1, \dots, 1)\) be its \emph{barycentre}\index{barycentre}. Take an \(i\)-simplex \(\sigma : \Delta^i \to \Delta^n\), we define
\begin{align*}
    \Cone_i^{\Delta^n}(\sigma) : \Delta^{i+1} &\to \Delta^n \\
    (t_0, \dots, t_{i+1}) &\mapsto t_0 b_n + (1-t_0)\sigma\left(\frac{(t_1, \dots, t_{i+1})}{1-t_0}\right)
\end{align*}
We can view \(\Cone_i^{\Delta^n} : C_i(\Delta^n) \to C_{i+1}(\Delta^n)\) by extending linearly.

\underline{Exercise:}
\[\partial(\Cone_i^{\Delta^n}(\sigma)) = \begin{cases}
    \sigma - \Cone_{i-1}^{\Delta^n}(\partial\sigma) & i > 0 \\
    \sigma - \epsilon(\sigma) b_n & i = 0
\end{cases}\]
where
\begin{align*}
    \epsilon : C_0(\Delta^n) &\to \Z \\
    \sum n_i \sigma_i &\mapsto \sum n_i
\end{align*}
If we define \(c_\bullet : C_*(\Delta^n) \to C_*(\Delta^n)\) by
\[c_\bullet(\sigma) = \begin{cases}
    \epsilon(\sigma)b_n & * = 0 \\
    0 & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases}\]
Then
\[\partial \Cone^{\Delta^n} + \Cone^{\Delta^n}\partial = \id - c_\bullet\]

Our aim is to introduce a barycentric subdivision operation \(\varphi : C_*(X) \to C_*(X)\), which for each simplex divides the boundary and cones off to the barycentre.

\begin{definition}
    [natural]
    A collection of chain maps \(\varphi^X : C_*(X) \to C_*(X)\) is \emph{natural}\index{natural} if for \(f : X \to Y\), \(f_* \circ \varphi^X = \varphi^Y \circ f_*\).
\end{definition}

If \(\sigma : \Delta^n \to X\) is an \(n\)-simplex, and \(i_n : \Delta^n \to \Delta^n\) denotes the inclusion map, then \(\sigma = \sigma \circ i_n\), and so
\[\varphi^X(\sigma)  = \varphi^X_n(\sigma_* i_n) = \sigma_*(\varphi_n^{\Delta^n}(\sigma))\]
Thus, if we can subdivide the \(n\)-simplex, then we can use naturality to extend \(\varphi^X\) for all \(X\).

The formula is
\[\varphi^X_n(\sigma) = \sigma_*\left(\Cone_{n-1}^{\Delta^n}\left(\varphi_{n-1}^{\Delta^n}(\partial i_n)\right)\right)\]

\begin{lemma}
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item If \(\sigma = [v_0, \dots, v_n] \sse \R^{n+1}\) is an \(n\)-simplex, for example any \(n\)-simplex in a subdivision of \(\Delta^N\) for \(N \ge n\), then for any simplex \(\tau\) in its barycentric subdivision,
        \[\diam(\tau) \le \frac{n}{n+1}\diam(\sigma)\]
        \item If \(\sigma \in C_n^{\mcU}(X)\), then \(\varphi_n^X(\sigma) \in C_n^\mcU(X)\).
        \item If \(\sigma \in C_n(X)\), there exists \(k > 0\) such that \((\varphi_n^X)^k(\sigma) \in C_n^\mcU(X)\).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    (i) is just Euclidean geometry, (ii) is obvious. For (iii), we use the fact that \(\sigma\) is a finite sum of simplices, and so it suffices to prove this for a single simplex, since we can just take the maximum value of \(k\). Then
    \[\left\{\sigma^{-1}(\Int(U_{\alpha}))\right\}_{\alpha \in A}\]
    is an open cover of \(\Delta^n\), which is a compact metric space, and so it has a Lebesgue number \(\epsilon\). That is, every open \(\epsilon\)-ball in \(\Delta^n\) lies in \(\sigma^{-1}(\Int(U_\alpha))\) for some \(\alpha\). But we can choose \(k\) such that
    \[\left(\frac{n}{n+1}\right)^k < \epsilon\]
    and so we are done.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    There is a natural (with respect to maps of spaces) chain homotopy
    \[P_X^* : C_*(X) \to C_{*+1}(X)\]
    with
    \[\partial P_n^X + P_{n-1}^X\partial = \varphi_n^* - \id_{C_n(X)}\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    As in homotopy invariance, we construct a prism \(\Delta^n \times [0, 1]\) by gluing \(\Delta^n\) to its barycentric subdivision. We can subdivide this into \(n+1\)-simplices. See Hatcher for a formula.

    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_25 13_29 (1).jpg}
    \end{center}
    
    Let \(U : \HH_n(C_*^\mcU(X)) \to \HH_n(C_*(X))\) denote the induced map. Choose \([\sigma] \in \HH_n(X)\), and choose \(k\) such that \((\varphi_n^X)^k(c) \in C_n^{\mcU}(X)\). Since \(\varphi_n^X\) is homotopic to \(\id\), so is \((\varphi_n^X)^k\), so there exists \(F\) such that
    \[\partial F + F\partial = (\varphi^*)^k - \id\]
    Thus, \((\varphi^X)^k(c) = c + \partial(\text{stuff})\), and so \(U\) is surjective.

    On the other hand, if \(U([c]) = 0\), then there exists \(z \in C_{n+1}(X)\) with \(\partial z = c\). There exists \(k\) such that
    \[(\varphi_{n+1}^X)^k(z) \in C_{n+1}^\mcU(X) \qqtext{and} (\varphi_{n+1}^X)^k(z) - z = (\partial F + F\partial)(c)\]
    Then
    \[c = \partial z = \partial(\varphi^X)^k(z) - \partial F(\partial z) \in C_{n+1}^{\mcU}\]
    and so \([c] = 0\) in \(\HH_n(C_*^\mcU(X))\) already.
\end{proof}

\section{Cellular homology}

Singular homology is most effective on `nice' spaces. One example would be \emph{cell complexes} or CW-complexes. We will introduce a more manageable chain complex, for computing the homology or cohomology of a cell complex.

\subsection{Definitions}

\begin{definition}
    [cell complex] A \emph{cell complex}\index{cell complex} is a topological space \(X\) obtained inductively as follows:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \(X_0\) is a discrete set,
        \item 
        \[X_k = X_{k-1} \cup \bigcup_{i \in I_k}D_i^k\]
        attached via maps \(\varphi^k_i : \partial D_i^k = S^{k-1} \to X\). More formally, we can write this as a quotient of
        \[X_{k-1} \sqcup \bigsqcup_{i \in I_k}D_i^k\]
        \item \(X = \bigcup_k X_k\), where we note that \(X_{k-1} \sse X_k\), equipped with the \emph{weak topology}\index{weak topology}. That is, \(U \sse X\) is open if and only if \(U \cap X_k\) is open in \(X_k\) for all \(k\).
    \end{itemize}

    We call the \(D^k_i\) the \emph{\(k\)-cells of \(X\)}\index{cells of a cell complex@\(k\)-cells of a cell complex}, the \(\varphi^k_i\) the \emph{attaching maps}\index{attaching maps}, \(X_k\) the \emph{\(k\)-skeleton of \(X\)}\index{skeleton of a cell complex@\(k\)-skeleton of a cell complex}.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    [spheres]
    \(S^n = \{*\} \cup \{\text{open disc}\}\), i.e. \(X_0 = \{*\}\) and a unique \(n\)-cell.

    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_25 13_29 (2).jpg}
    \end{center}

    On the other hand, we can also construct \(S^2\) as 2 \(0\)-cells, 2 \(1\)-cells and 2 \(2\)-cells.

    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_25 13_29 (3).jpg}
    \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    [torus]
    The standard gluing diagram of \(T^2\) gives a cell structure with 1 \(0\)-cells, 2 \(1\)-cells and 1 \(2\)-cell. 
    
    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_25 13_29 (4).jpg}
    \end{center}

    More generally, the surface \(\Sigma_g\) of genus \(g\) has \(1\) \(0\)-cell, \(2g\) \(1\)-cells and 1 \(2\)-cells.

    \begin{center}
        \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_10_25 13_29 (5).jpg}
    \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    If \(X, Y\) are cell complexes, \(x_0 \in X_0\), \(y_0 \in Y_0\), then
    \[X \vee Y = \frac{X \sqcup Y}{x_0 = y_0}\]
    is naturally a cell complex.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
    If \(X\) is a cell complex, then \(X\) is a disjoint union of the open cells \(\Int(D^k_i)\).
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
    [finite dimensional, finite cell complex]
    If \(X = X_n\) for some \(n\), we say \(X\) is \emph{finite dimensional}\index{finite dimensional cell complex}. If \(X = X_n\) and for all \(k \le n\), \(I_k\) is finite (i.e. \(X\) has finitely many cells), we say that \(X\) is an \emph{finite cell complex}\index{finite cell complex}.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    A finite cell complex is compact.
\end{remark}

\lec

\begin{definition}
    [subcomplex]
    A \emph{subcomplex of \(X\)}\index{subcomplex} is a closed subspace of \(X\) which is a union of cells of \(X\).
\end{definition}

\subsection{Homology of cell complexes}

\begin{lemma}
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item \(A \sse X\) is open (resp. closed) if and only if its preimage in any cell is open (resp. closed), through the composition
        \[\varphi_\alpha : D_\alpha \inj X_{n+1}\sqcup \bigsqcup_{\beta} D_\beta \surj X_n \inj X\]
        We call \(\varphi_\alpha\) the \emph{characteristic map of the cell \(D_\alpha\)}\index{characteristic map}. Note its restriction to the boundary is the attaching map.
        \item Cell complexes are Hausdorff and locally contractible. In particular, connected and path connected are equivalent.
        \item If \(Z \sse X\) is compact, then \(Z \sse X_N\) for some \(N\).
        \item If \(A \sse X\) is a subcomplex, then the pair \((X, A)\) is good.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Exercises, or see Hatcher.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    If \(A \sse X\) is a subcomplex, then \(\HH_*(X, A) \cong \widetilde H_*(X/A)\). In particular,
    \[\HH_i(X_k, X_{k-1}) = \begin{cases}
        \Z^{\oplus I_k} & i = k \\
        0 & \text{ otherwise }
    \end{cases}\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    \[\frac{X_k}{X_{k-1}} = \bigvee_{I_k}S^k\]
    Result follows by Mayer-Vietoris, we note that
    \[\left(\bigsqcup_{\alpha \in I_k}S_\alpha^k, \bigsqcup_{\alpha \in I_k} \{x_\alpha\}\right)\]
    is a good pair, and the quotient is the wedge sum. Thus, the reduced homology of the wedge sum is given by the relative homology
    \[\HH_*\left(\bigsqcup S^k, \bigsqcup \{x_k\}\right) = \bigoplus \HH_*(S^k, x_k)\]
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    Let \(X\) be a cell complex.
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item \(\HH_k(X_n) = 0\) for all \(k > n\),
        \item The inclusion \(X_n \inj X\) induces an isomorphism of \(\HH_k(X_n) \cong \HH_k(X)\) for \(k < n\).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    For (i), consider the long exact sequence given by \((X_n, X_{n-1})\),
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJcXEhIX3trKzF9KFhfbiwgWF97bi0xfSkiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIX2soWF97bi0xfSkiXSxbMywwLCJcXEhIX2soWF9uKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhfayhYX24sIFhfe24tMX0pIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFswLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbNSwwXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXSxbMyw0XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH_{k+1}(X_n, X_{n-1})} & {\HH_k(X_{n-1})} & {\HH_k(X_n)} & {\HH_k(X_n, X_{n-1})} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    if \(k > n\), then \(\HH_k(X_n, X_{n-1}) = \HH_{k+1}(X_n, X_{n-1})\) as it is a wedge of spheres, and so
    \[\HH_k(X_{n-1}) \cong \HH_k(X_n)\]
    But we can iterate this, since \(k > n > n - 1\), and so
    \[\HH_k(X_n) \cong \HH_k(X_{n-1}) \cong \cdots \cong \HH_k(X_0) = 0\]
    since \(X_0\) is a discrete set.

    For (ii), consider the same sequence
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJcXEhIX3trKzF9KFhfbiwgWF97bi0xfSkiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIX2soWF97bi0xfSkiXSxbMywwLCJcXEhIX2soWF9uKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhfayhYX24sIFhfe24tMX0pIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFswLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbNSwwXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXSxbMyw0XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH_{k+1}(X_n, X_{n-1})} & {\HH_k(X_{n-1})} & {\HH_k(X_n)} & {\HH_k(X_n, X_{n-1})} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    as above, with \(k < n - 1\). In this case, \(k + 1 < n\) and \(k < n\), and so the same logic shows
    \[\HH_k(X_{n-1}) \cong \HH_k(X_n) \cong \HH_k(X_N)\]
    for all \(N \ge n\). If \(X\) is finite dimensional, then we are done. In general, if \(\alpha \in \HH_k(X)\), then it is represented by a finite collection of \(k\)-simplices. But this is a compact space, and so it lies within \(X_N\) for some \(N\), and so \(\alpha\) lies in the image of the map \(\HH_k(X_N) \to \HH_k(X)\). Conversely, if we have \(\alpha \in \HH_k(X)\) bounding a \(k+1\)-chain, then that union of simplices lies in \(X_{N'}\) for some \(N' \ge N\), and so \(\alpha = 0\) in \(\HH_k(X_{N'})\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    If \(X\) is a finite cell complex of dimension \(N\), then \(\HH_j(X) = 0\) for all \(j > N\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{definition}
    [cellular chain complex]
    We define the \emph{cellular chain complex}\index{cellular chain complex} \(C_*^\cell(X)\) of a cell complex \(X\) (with its cell structure) via
    \[C_k^\cell(X) := \HH_*(X_k, X_{k-1}) = \text{free abelian group on \(k\)-cells}\]
    with the differential defined as:
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwxLCJcXEhIX3trKzF9KFhfe2srMX0sWF9rKSJdLFsyLDEsIlxcSEhfayhYX2ssIFhfe2stMX0pIl0sWzQsMSwiXFxISF97ay0xfShYX3trLTF9LCBYX3trLTJ9KSJdLFsxLDAsIlxcSEhfayhYX2spIl0sWzMsMiwiXFxISF97ay0xfShYX3trLTF9KSJdLFswLDMsIiIsMix7ImNvbG91ciI6WzAsNjAsNjBdfV0sWzMsMSwiIiwyLHsiY29sb3VyIjpbMzAsNjAsNjBdfV0sWzEsNCwiIiwyLHsiY29sb3VyIjpbMzAsNjAsNjBdfV0sWzQsMiwiIiwyLHsiY29sb3VyIjpbMCw2MCw2MF19XSxbMCwxLCJcXHBhcnRpYWxeXFxjZWxsX3trKzF9IiwyXSxbMSwyLCJcXHBhcnRpYWxeXFxjZWxsX2siLDJdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	& {\HH_k(X_k)} \\
	{\HH_{k+1}(X_{k+1},X_k)} && {\HH_k(X_k, X_{k-1})} && {\HH_{k-1}(X_{k-1}, X_{k-2})} \\
	&&& {\HH_{k-1}(X_{k-1})}
	\arrow[color={rgb,255:red,214;green,92;blue,92}, from=2-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[color={rgb,255:red,214;green,153;blue,92}, from=1-2, to=2-3]
	\arrow[color={rgb,255:red,214;green,153;blue,92}, from=2-3, to=3-4]
	\arrow[color={rgb,255:red,214;green,92;blue,92}, from=3-4, to=2-5]
	\arrow["{\partial^\cell_{k+1}}"', from=2-1, to=2-3]
	\arrow["{\partial^\cell_k}"', from=2-3, to=2-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where the diagonal maps are defined using the long exact sequence of a pair. We will write \(\HH_*^\cell(X)\) for the homology of the chain complex \(C_*^\cell(X)\). 
\end{definition}

\begin{claim}
    \(\partial_k^\cell \circ \partial_{k+1}^\cell = 0\). 
\end{claim}

\begin{proof}
    The composition \(\partial_k^\cell \circ \partial_{k+1}^\cell\) includes two consecutive maps (the orange ones in the above diagram) in the long exact sequence of the pair \((X_k, X_{k-1})\), which compose to zero. As such, \(\partial_k^\cell \circ \partial_{k+1}^\cell = 0\).
\end{proof}

Note \(C_*^\cell(X)\) depends on the choice of cell structure on \(X\).

\begin{proposition}
    \index{isomorphism!of cellular and singular cohomology}
    \(\HH_*(X) \cong \HH_*^\cell(X)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Consider the diagram
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsOSxbMCwzLCJcXEhIX3trKzF9KFhfe2srMX0sWF9rKSJdLFsyLDMsIlxcSEhfayhYX2ssIFhfe2stMX0pIl0sWzQsMywiXFxISF97ay0xfShYX3trLTF9LCBYX3trLTJ9KSJdLFsxLDIsIlxcSEhfayhYX2spIl0sWzMsNCwiXFxISF97ay0xfShYX3trLTF9KSJdLFswLDEsIjA9XFxISF9rKFhfe2stMX0pIl0sWzIsNSwiMD1cXEhIX3trLTF9KFhfe2stMn0pIl0sWzIsMSwiXFxISF9rKFhfe2srMX0pPVxcSEhfayhYKSJdLFszLDAsIjAiXSxbMCwzLCJcXHBhcnRpYWxfe2srMX0iXSxbMywxLCJpX2siXSxbMSw0LCJcXHBhcnRpYWxfayIsMl0sWzQsMiwiaV97ay0xfSIsMl0sWzAsMSwiXFxwYXJ0aWFsXlxcY2VsbF97aysxfSIsMl0sWzEsMiwiXFxwYXJ0aWFsXlxcY2VsbF9rIiwyXSxbNiw0XSxbNSwzXSxbMyw3XSxbNyw4XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	&&& 0 \\
	{0=\HH_k(X_{k-1})} && {\HH_k(X_{k+1})=\HH_k(X)} \\
	& {\HH_k(X_k)} \\
	{\HH_{k+1}(X_{k+1},X_k)} && {\HH_k(X_k, X_{k-1})} && {\HH_{k-1}(X_{k-1}, X_{k-2})} \\
	&&& {\HH_{k-1}(X_{k-1})} \\
	&& {0=\HH_{k-1}(X_{k-2})}
	\arrow["{\partial_{k+1}}", from=4-1, to=3-2]
	\arrow["{i_k}", from=3-2, to=4-3]
	\arrow["{\partial_k}"', from=4-3, to=5-4]
	\arrow["{i_{k-1}}"', from=5-4, to=4-5]
	\arrow["{\partial^\cell_{k+1}}"', from=4-1, to=4-3]
	\arrow["{\partial^\cell_k}"', from=4-3, to=4-5]
	\arrow[from=6-3, to=5-4]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=3-2]
	\arrow[from=3-2, to=2-3]
	\arrow[from=2-3, to=1-4]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    From this, we have that
    \[\HH_k(X) = \frac{\HH_k(X_k)}{\im(\partial_{k+1})} = \frac{i_k(\HH_k(X_k))}{\im(i_k \circ \partial_{k+1})} = \frac{\ker(\partial_k)}{\im(\partial_{k+1}^\cell)} = \frac{\ker(i_{k-1} \circ \partial_k)}{\im(\partial_{k+1}^\cell)} = \frac{\ker(\partial_k^\cell)}{\im(\partial_{k+1}^\cell)} = \HH_k^\cell(X)\]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    Let \(X\) be a finite cell complex. Then
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item \(\HH_k(X)\) is a finitely generated abelian group of rank at most \(n_k = \abs{I_k}\),
        \item if \(\HH_k(X) \ne 0\), every cell structure must contain \(k\)-cells.
        \item if \(X\) admist a cell structure with cells in only even dimension, then \(\HH_*(X) \cong C_*^\cell(X)\).
        \item if \(F\) is a field, then \(\HH_*(X;F)\) is a finite dimensional \(F\)-vector space.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

For (iii), we saw that \(\C\P^n\) satisfies the requirements on examples sheet 1, and the same is true for the Grassmannian \(\Gr(k, \C^n)\) of \(k\)-dimensional linear subspaces of \(\C^n\) (where \(\Gr(1, \C^n) = \C\P^{n-1}\)), and various other spaces in complex algebraic geometry.

The same sort of argument as for \(\C\P^n\) shows that \(\R\P^n\) has a single cell in each degree \(0 \le i \le n\), that is, the cellular chain complex is
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNyxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFs0LDAsIlxcWiJdLFs1LDAsIlxcWiJdLFs2LDAsIjAiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXSxbMyw0XSxbNCw1XSxbNSw2XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & \Z & \Z & \cdots & \Z & \Z & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
	\arrow[from=1-6, to=1-7]

But we need new tools to compute the cellular boundary.

\lec

That is, if \(e_\alpha\) is a \(k\)-cell, then
\[\partial_k^\cell(e_\alpha) = \sum_{\beta}d_{\alpha\beta}e_\beta\]
of \(k-1\)-cells. We would like to find the \(d_{\alpha\beta} \in \Z\).

\begin{lemma}
    \(d_{\alpha\beta}\) is the degree of the following map on spheres:
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCJTXntrLTF9X1xcYWxwaGEiXSxbMiwwLCJYX3trLTF9Il0sWzQsMCwiWF97ay0xfS9YX3trLTJ9Il0sWzYsMCwiXFxiaWd2ZWVfe1xcYmV0YX1TXntrLTF9X1xcYmV0YSJdLFs4LDAsIlNee2stMX1fXFxiZXRhIl0sWzAsMSwiXFx2YXJwaGlfXFxhbHBoYSJdLFsxLDIsIiIsMCx7InN0eWxlIjp7ImhlYWQiOnsibmFtZSI6ImVwaSJ9fX1dLFsyLDMsIlxcc2ltIl0sWzMsNCwiXFx0ZXh0e3Byb2p9IiwwLHsic3R5bGUiOnsiaGVhZCI6eyJuYW1lIjoiZXBpIn19fV1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{S^{k-1}_\alpha} && {X_{k-1}} && {X_{k-1}/X_{k-2}} && {\bigvee_{\beta}S^{k-1}_\beta} && {S^{k-1}_\beta}
	\arrow["{\varphi_\alpha}", from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow[two heads, from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow["\sim", from=1-5, to=1-7]
	\arrow["{\text{proj}}", two heads, from=1-7, to=1-9]
\end{tikzcd}\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{remark}
    For this to be well defined (and not just defined up to a sign), we need to fix isomorphisms \(\HH_{k-1}(S^{k-1}) \cong \Z\).
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
    Consider

\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH_k(D_\alpha^k, \partial D_\alpha^k)} && {\HH_{k-1}(\partial D_\alpha^k)} && {\HH_{k-1}(S_\beta^{k-1})} \\
	\\
	{\HH_k(X_k, X_{k-1})} && {\HH_{k-1}(X_{k-1})} \\
	\\
	&& {\HH_{k-1}(X_{k-1}, X_{k-2})} && {\tilde H_{k-1}(X_{k-1}/X_{k-2})}
	\arrow["{\varphi_\alpha}", from=1-1, to=3-1]

This is useful if we can compute the degree of maps between spheres.

Suppose \(f : S^n \to S^n\) and \(y \in S^n\) has finitely many preimages
\[f^{-1}(y) = \{x_1, \dots, x_m\}\]
In this case, we can choose pairwise disjoint discs \(U_i\) containing \(x_i\), and a disc \(V\) containing \(y\) such that \(f(U_i) \sse V\). Then \(f\) defines a map
\[(U_i, x_i) \to (V, y)\]
This then defines a map
\[\Z \cong \HH_n(U_i, U_i \setminus x) \to \HH_n(V, V \setminus y) = \Z\]
Moreover, recall that the isomorphisms above are from excision, and so the maps above are between the \emph{same} copy of \(\Z\). With this, we have a \emph{local degree of \(f\) at \(x\)}\index{local degree}. We will write
\[\deg_{x_i}(f) \text{ or }\deg_f(x_i) \in \Z\]
for this.

\begin{lemma}
    Under the assumption that such a \(y\) exists,
    \[\deg(f) = \sum_{i=1}^m \deg_{x_i}(f)\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    We have the diagram
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNyxbMCwwLCJcXEhIX24oU15uKSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcSEhfbihTXm4pIl0sWzAsMiwiXFxISF9uKFNebiwgU15uIFxcc2V0bWludXNcXHt4XzEsIFxcZG90cywgeF9uXFx9KSJdLFsyLDIsIlxcSEhfbihTXm4sIFNebiBcXHNldG1pbnVzXFx7eVxcfSkiXSxbMCw0LCJcXEhIX24oXFxiaWdzcWN1cF9pIFVfaSwgXFxiaWdzcWN1cF9pIChVX2kgXFxzZXRtaW51c1xce3hfaVxcfSkpIl0sWzIsNCwiXFxISF9uKFYsIFYgXFxzZXRtaW51cyBcXHt5XFx9KSJdLFswLDYsIlxcYmlnb3BsdXNfe2k9MX1ebSBcXEhIX24oVV9pLCBVX2kgXFxzZXRtaW51cyB4X2kpIl0sWzAsMSwiXFxkZWcoZikiXSxbMCwyXSxbMiwzLCJmXyoiLDJdLFsxLDNdLFs0LDIsIlxcY29uZyJdLFs0LDIsIlxcdGV4dHtleGNpc2lvbn0iLDJdLFs1LDMsIlxcY29uZyIsMl0sWzUsMywiXFx0ZXh0e2V4Y2lzaW9ufSJdLFs0LDVdLFs0LDYsIlxcY29uZyIsMl0sWzYsNSwiXFxiaWdvcGx1cyBcXGRlZ197eF9pfShmKSIsMl1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH_n(S^n)} && {\HH_n(S^n)} \\
	\\
	{\HH_n(S^n, S^n \setminus\{x_1, \dots, x_n\})} && {\HH_n(S^n, S^n \setminus\{y\})} \\
	\\
	{\HH_n(\bigsqcup_i U_i, \bigsqcup_i (U_i \setminus\{x_i\}))} && {\HH_n(V, V \setminus \{y\})} \\
	\\
	{\bigoplus_{i=1}^m \HH_n(U_i, U_i \setminus x_i)}
	\arrow["{\deg(f)}", from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow["{f_*}"', from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\cong", from=5-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow["{\text{excision}}"', from=5-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow["\cong"', from=5-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["{\text{excision}}", from=5-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow[from=5-1, to=5-3]
	\arrow["\cong"', from=5-1, to=7-1]
	\arrow["{\bigoplus \deg_{x_i}(f)}"', from=7-1, to=5-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    and the result follows from the fact that the diagram commutes.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    By the same argument as for \(\C\P^n\), \(\R\P^n\) has a cell structure of the form
    \[e^n \cup \R\P^{n-1}\]
    That is, there is an \(i\)-cell for \(0 \le i \le n\). The attaching map \(\partial e^n = S^{n-1} \to \R\P^{n-1}\) is the canonical \(2 : 1\) map. With this, the cellular complex is:
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsOCxbMSwwLCJcXFoiXSxbMiwwLCJcXFoiXSxbMywwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzYsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzAsMCwiMCJdLFs3LDAsIjAiXSxbNCwwLCJcXFoiXSxbNSwwXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMyw0LCIwIiwyXSxbNCw2XSxbMiw3XSxbNywzXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & \Z & \Z & \cdots & \Z & \Z & \Z & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow["0"', from=1-6, to=1-7]
	\arrow[from=1-7, to=1-8]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    Consider
    \[\partial_k^\cell : C_k^\cell \to C_{k-1}^\cell\]
    This is induced by
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMyxbMCwwLCJcXHBhcnRpYWwgRF5rID0gU157ay0xfSJdLFsxLDAsIlxcUlxcUF57ay0xfSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcUlxcUF57ay0xfS9cXFJcXFBee2stMn0gPSBTXntrLTF9Il0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMl1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\partial D^k = S^{k-1}} & {\R\P^{k-1}} & {\R\P^{k-1}/\R\P^{k-2} = S^{k-1}}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    Let \(\eta\) be the composition. At a general point \(p\) of the image \(S^{k-1}\), \(p\) has two preimages under \(\eta\). Moreover, near each of the preimages, \(\eta\) is a homeomorphism. Fix \(V \sse \R\P^{k-1}\) an open disc, under the map \(S^{K-1} \to \R\P^{k-1}\), \(V\) has two (disjoint) preimages, \(U_1, U_2\), with \(\eta\vert_{U_2} = \eta\vert_{U_1} \circ \text{(antipodal)}\), and so
    \[\deg_{x_1}(f) = (-1)^k\deg_{x_2}(f)\]
    With this, \(\partial_k^\cell\) is multiplication by \(1 + (-1)^k\) (possibly up to a sign). The complex then becomes
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsOCxbNiwwLCJcXFoiXSxbNywwLCIwIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzQsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFsxLDAsIlxcWiJdLFswLDAsIlxcWiJdLFswLDFdLFszLDIsIjIiLDJdLFsyLDAsIjAiLDJdLFs0LDMsIjAiLDJdLFs1LDRdLFs3LDZdLFs2LDVdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\Z & \Z & \cdots & \Z & \Z & \Z & \Z & 0
	\arrow[from=1-7, to=1-8]
	\arrow["2"', from=1-5, to=1-6]
	\arrow["0"', from=1-6, to=1-7]
	\arrow["0"', from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    and so the homology is
    \begin{align*}
        \HH_*(\R\P^n) = \begin{cases}
            \Z & * = 0 \\
            \Z/2 & 0 < * < n, * \text{ odd} \\
            \Z & * = n, n \text{ odd} \\
            0 & \text{otherwise}
        \end{cases}
    \end{align*}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    [Exercise] If \(p(z)\) is a complex polynomial, then \(p\) extends to a continuous map \(\hat p : \C_\infty \to \C_\infty\) of degree \(\deg(p)\), and if \(x \in \C\) is a root of \(p\), the local degree \(\deg_x(\hat p)\) is the multiplicity of the root.

    c.f. the fundamental theorem of algebra.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
    If \(f : S^n \to S^n\) is a \emph{smooth map}, then \(f^{-1}(y)\) is finite if \(y\) is a \emph{regular value}, and by Sard's theorem the set of critical values has measure zero, and as such, the set of regular values is dense.

    Moreover, every continuous map \(S^n \to S^n\) is homotopic to a smooth map.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Digression on cohomology}

Set \(C^*_\cell(X) = \Hom(C_*^\cell(X), \Z)\), and \(\partial^*_\cell\) for the adjoint of \(\partial_*^\cell\). Consider

But on examples sheet 2, we show that the diagram below
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMCwwLCJcXEhIXmkoWF9pLCBYX3tpLTF9KSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcSEheaShYX2kpIl0sWzQsMCwiXFxISF57aSsxfShYX3tpKzF9LCBYX2kpIl0sWzAsMiwiXFxIb20oXFxISF9pKFhfaSwgWF97aS0xfSksIFxcWikiXSxbMiwyLCJcXEhvbShcXEhIX2koWF9pKSwgXFxaKSJdLFs0LDIsIlxcSG9tKFxcSEhfe2krMX0oWF97aSsxfSwgWF9pKSwgXFxaKSJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFswLDMsIlxcY29uZyIsMl0sWzMsNF0sWzQsNV0sWzIsNSwiXFxjb25nIl0sWzEsNF0sWzMsNSwiXFx0ZXh0e2Fkam9pbnQgdG8gfVxccGFydGlhbF9rXlxcY2VsbCIsMix7ImN1cnZlIjozfV1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH^i(X_i, X_{i-1})} && {\HH^i(X_i)} && {\HH^{i+1}(X_{i+1}, X_i)} \\
	\\
	{\Hom(\HH_i(X_i, X_{i-1}), \Z)} && {\Hom(\HH_i(X_i), \Z)} && {\Hom(\HH_{i+1}(X_{i+1}, X_i), \Z)}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow["\cong"', from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow[from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow[from=3-3, to=3-5]
	\arrow["\cong", from=1-5, to=3-5]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["{\text{adjoint to }\partial_k^\cell}"', curve={height=18pt}, from=3-1, to=3-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
commutes. Thus, we could have defined
\[C^i_\cell(X) = \HH^i(X_i, X_{i-1})\]
and the boundary operators will have given the same cochain complex. Note however in the diagram above, the middle map need not be an isomorphism.

\begin{proposition}
    Let \(X\) be a finite cell complex. Then we have a (non-canonical) isomorphism
    \[\HH^i(X) \cong \frac{\HH_i(X)}{\Tor(\HH_i(X))} \oplus \Tor(\HH_{i-1}(X))\]
    where for an abelian group \(A\), \(\Tor(A)\) is the subgroup of torsion elements.
\end{proposition}

\lec

\begin{proof}
    This is pure algebra. Let \(C_*\) be a chain complex, such that the chain groups \(C_i\) are finitely generated and free. Let \(C^*\) be the corresponding cochain complex, and then corresponding relation holds. That is,
    \[\HH^j(X) \cong \frac{\HH_j(X)}{\Tor(\HH_j(X))} \oplus \Tor(\HH_{j-1}(X))\]
    and so, all we are using is that for a finite cell complex, \(C_*^\cell\) is finitely generated and free,

    Break the chain complex into a sequence of short exact seqeuences
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiWl9uIl0sWzIsMCwiQ19uIl0sWzMsMCwiQl97bi0xfSJdLFs0LDAsIjAiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXSxbMyw0XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {Z_n} & {C_n} & {B_{n-1}} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    and
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiQl9uIl0sWzIsMCwiWl9uIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxISF9uKENfKikiXSxbNCwwLCIwIl0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMl0sWzIsM10sWzMsNF1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {B_n} & {Z_n} & {\HH_n(C_*)} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where \(Z_n = \ker(\partial : C_n \to C_{n-1})\), \(B_{n-1} = \im(\partial : C_n \to C_{n-1})\). In the top sequence, all of the groups are free. So we can split the top sequence. That is, there exists \(\alpha_n : B_{n-1} \to C_n\) such that \(\partial_n \alpha_n = \id\). Then we have a (non-canonical) isomorphism
    \[C_n \cong Z_n \oplus B_{n-1}\]
    With this, the chain complex looks like
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMSwwLCJaX3tuKzF9XFxvcGx1cyBCX24iXSxbMiwwLCJaX25cXG9wbHVzIEJfe24tMX0iXSxbMywwLCJcXFpfe24tMX1cXG9wbHVzIEJfe24tMn0iXSxbMCwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzQsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFszLDBdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDRdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {Z_{n+1}\oplus B_n} & {Z_n\oplus B_{n-1}} & {\Z_{n-1}\oplus B_{n-2}} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    In terms of this decomposition, \(\partial(B_n) \sse Z_n\). That is, \(C_*\) breaks down as a direct sum of length two complexes.

    We can simplify this further. By choosing helpful bases for \(B_n\) and \(Z_{n-1}\) using the Smith normal form, which says that using a \(\Z\)-change of basis, we can write
    \[\partial_n = \begin{pmatrix}
        d_1 \\
        & \ddots \\
        & & d_k \\
        & & & 0 \\
        & & & & \ddots \\
        & & & & & 0
    \end{pmatrix}\]
    where \(d_1 \mid d_2, d_2 \mid d_3\) and so on. Then \(C_*\) is broken into a direct sum of two kinds of complex:
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzMsMCwiMCJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDIsImQiXSxbMiwzXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & \Z & \Z & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["d", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    for \(d \ne 0\), and
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzMsMCwiMCJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDIsIjAiXSxbMiwzXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & \Z & \Z & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["0", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    For these two very simple complexes, the claim in the proposition is clear.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    For abelian groups \(H, G\), let \index{Ext@\(\Ext^1\)}

	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
