\begin{theorem}
    [universal coefficient] There are exact sequences (for \emph{any} topological space \(X\)),
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxFeHReMShcXEhIX3tuLTF9KFgpLEcpIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF5uKFg7IEcpIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxIb20oXFxISF9uKFgpO0cpIl0sWzQsMCwiMCJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDRdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {\Ext^1(\HH_{n-1}(X),G)} & {\HH^n(X; G)} & {\Hom(\HH_n(X);G)} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    which are (non-canonically) split.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Omitted.
\end{proof}

Recall if \(X\) is a finite cell complex, then \(\HH_i(X)\) is a finitely generated abelian group, and \(\HH_i(X; F)\) is a finite dimensional \(F\)-vector space.

\begin{definition}
    [Euler characteristic] If \(X\) is a finite cell complex, we define its \emph{Euler characteristic}\index{Euler characteristic}
    \[\chi(X) = \sum_{i\ge0}(-1)^i\rank_\Z(\HH_i(X))\]
    and more generally, if \(F\) is a field,
    \[\chi(X, F) = \sum_i(-1)^i \dim(\HH_i(X; F))\]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    If \(X\) is a finite cell complex, then
    \[\chi(X) = \sum_i (-1)^i N_i\]
    where \(X\) has \(N_i\) \(i\)-cells in its cell structure.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Recall our short exact sequences 
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiWl9uIl0sWzIsMCwiQ19uIl0sWzMsMCwiQl97bi0xfSJdLFs0LDAsIjAiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXSxbMyw0XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {Z_n} & {C_n} & {B_{n-1}} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    and
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiQl9uIl0sWzIsMCwiWl9uIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxISF9uKFgpIl0sWzQsMCwiMCJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDRdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {B_n} & {Z_n} & {\HH_n(X)} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where \(C_* = C_*^\cell(X)\). Say \(N_n = \rank(C_n), z_n = \rank(Z_n)\) and \(b_n = \rank(B_n)\). Then
    \[N_n = z_n + b_{n-1} \qqtext{ and }\rank(\HH_n(X)) = z_n - b_n\]
    Substituting,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_k (-1)^k \rank_\Z(\HH_k(X)) &= \sum_{k\ge 0}(-1)^k(z_k - b_k) \\
        &= \sum_{k\ ge 0}\left(z_k - \left(N_{k+1} - z_{k+1}\right)\right) \\
        &= \sum_{k \ge 0}(-1)^{k+1}N_{k+1} + z_0 \\
        &= \sum_{k\ge 0}(-1)^kN_k
    \end{align*}
    since \(z_0 = N_0\).
\end{proof}

The same computation shows that \(\chi(X; F) = \sum(-1)^k N_k\), and so the Euler characteristic is independent of our choice of field.

\begin{example}
    \(\chi(S^4) = 2, \chi(\C\P^3) = 3\) and so \(S^4\) is not homotopy equivalent to \(\C\P^3\). 
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    If \(X\) is a union of two subcomplexes \(A, B\), then
    \[\chi(X) = \chi(A) + \chi(B) - \chi(A \cap B)\]
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    If \(X, Y\) are finite cell complexes, then \(X \times Y\) admits a cell structure, such that the open cells in \(X \times Y\) are products of open cells in \(X\) and open cells in \(Y\). Then
    \[\chi(X \times Y) = \chi(X)\chi(Y)\]
\end{example}

\subsection{Generalised homology theories}

\begin{definition}
    [generalised homology theory]
    A \emph{generalised homology theory}\index{generalised homology theory} is an assignment
    \[(X, A) \mapsto h_*(X, A) = \bigoplus_{i \in \Z}h_i(X, A)\]
    of a graded abelian group to a pair \((X, A)\), where \(X\) is a topological space and \(A \sse X\) a subspace. This needs to satisfy:

    \begin{enumerate}
        \item (functoriality) A map \(f : (X, A) \to (Y, B)\) induces \(f_* : h_*(X, A) \to h_*(Y, B)\), with \(\id_* = \id\) and \((f \circ g)_* = f_* \circ g_*\),
        \item (homotopy invariance) if \(f \simeq g\) as maps of pairs, \(f_* = g_*\),
        \item (long exact sequence) writing \(h_i(X) = h_i(X, \emptyset)\), we have a long exact sequence
        % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJoX2koQSkiXSxbMiwwLCJoX2koWCkiXSxbMywwLCJoX2koWCxBKSJdLFs0LDAsImhfe2ktMX0oQSkiXSxbNSwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzAsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFs1LDBdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDRdXQ==
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \cdots & {h_i(A)} & {h_i(X)} & {h_i(X,A)} & {h_{i-1}(A)} & \cdots
        \arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
        \arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
        \arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
        \arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
        \arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
        which is natural. The maps \(h_i(A) \to h_i(X)\) and \(h_i(X) \to h_i(X, A)\) are induced by inclusion.
        \item (excision) If \(\Cl(Z) \sse \Int(A)\), then
        \[h_i(X \setminus Z, A \setminus Z) \cong h_*(X, A)\]
        via the inclusion map.
        \item (unions)
        \[\bigoplus_\alpha h_*(X_\alpha) \cong h_*\left(\bigsqcup_\alpha X_\alpha\right)\]
        via the sum of the inclusion maps.
    \end{enumerate}
    These are called the \emph{Eilenberg-Steenrod axioms}\index{Eilenberg-Steenrod axioms}, and \(h_*(\{\text{pt}\})\) are called the \emph{coefficients}\index{coefficients} of the theory.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    We can build examples of the form
    \[(X, A) \mapsto \HH_*(X, A) \otimes_\Z R\]
    where \(R\) is an abelian group. A `meta-theorem' says that interesting generalised homology theories are not from chain complexes.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}
    If \(h_*, k_*\) are generalised homology theories on the set of pairs \((X, A)\) of a cell complex and a subcomplex, and if \(\Phi : h_* \to k_*\) is a natural transformation, then \(\Phi\) being an isomorphism on a point implies it is an isomorphism on all such pairs.
\end{proposition}

\lec

\begin{proof}
    [Outline proof where \(X\) is finite dimensional]
    We induct on \(\dim(X)\). If \(X = X_0\), then \(X\) is a discrete set and the unions axiom implies the result.

    Suppose inductively \(\Phi : h_*(X, A) \to k_*(X, A)\) is an isomorphism whenever \(\dim(X) \le n-1\). Let \(X\) be an \(n\)-dimensional cell complex. In this case, we have long exact sequences
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTAsWzAsMCwiaF97aSsxfShYLCBYX3tuLTF9KSJdLFsyLDAsImhfaShYX3tuLTF9KSJdLFs0LDAsImhfaShYKSJdLFs2LDAsImhfaShYLCBYX3tuLTF9KSJdLFs4LDAsImhfe2ktMX0oWF97bi0xfSkiXSxbMCwyLCJrX3tpKzF9KFgsIFhfe24tMX0pIl0sWzIsMiwia19pKFhfe24tMX0pIl0sWzQsMiwia19pKFgpIl0sWzYsMiwia19pKFgsIFhfe24tMX0pIl0sWzgsMiwia197aS0xfShYX3tuLTF9KSJdLFswLDUsIlxcUGhpIiwxXSxbMSw2LCJcXFBoaSIsMl0sWzIsNywiXFxQaGkiLDFdLFszLDgsIlxcUGhpIiwxXSxbNCw5LCJcXFBoaSIsMl0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMl0sWzIsM10sWzMsNF0sWzgsOV0sWzcsOF0sWzYsN10sWzUsNl0sWzEsNiwiXFxjb25nIl0sWzQsOSwiXFxjb25nIl1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{h_{i+1}(X, X_{n-1})} && {h_i(X_{n-1})} && {h_i(X)} && {h_i(X, X_{n-1})} && {h_{i-1}(X_{n-1})} \\
	\\
	{k_{i+1}(X, X_{n-1})} && {k_i(X_{n-1})} && {k_i(X)} && {k_i(X, X_{n-1})} && {k_{i-1}(X_{n-1})}
	\arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow["\Phi"', from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-5, to=3-5]
	\arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-7, to=3-7]
	\arrow["\Phi"', from=1-9, to=3-9]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-7]
	\arrow[from=1-7, to=1-9]
	\arrow[from=3-7, to=3-9]
	\arrow[from=3-5, to=3-7]
	\arrow[from=3-3, to=3-5]
	\arrow[from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\cong", from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\cong", from=1-9, to=3-9]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    By the five lemma, if \(\Phi\) is an isomorphism on \(h_i(X, X_{n-1})\) for all \(i\), then it is an isomorphism on \(h_i(X)\). But by excision,
    \[h_*(X, X_{n-1}) = h_*(X_n, X_{n-1}) \cong h_*\left(\bigsqcup_\alpha D_\alpha^n, \bigsqcup_\alpha \partial D_\alpha^n\right)\]
    Here, we are using the fact that \(X_{n-1}\) has a neighbourhood \(N_\epsilon(X_{n-1}) \sse X_n\) for all \(\epsilon\) sufficiently small, whcih are constructed cell by cell, and retract onto the boundary on each cell. By the unions axiom,
    \[h_*\left(\bigsqcup_\alpha D_\alpha^n, \bigsqcup_\alpha \partial D_\alpha^n\right) = \bigoplus_\alpha h_*(D_\alpha^n, \partial D_\alpha^n)\]
    Now consider the LES of the pair
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTAsWzAsMCwiaF9pKFxccGFydGlhbCBEXm4pIl0sWzIsMCwiaF9pKERebikiXSxbNCwwLCJoX2koRF5uLCBcXHBhcnRpYWwgRF5uKSJdLFs2LDAsImhfe2ktMX0oXFxwYXJ0aWFsIERebikiXSxbOCwwLCJoX3tpLTF9KERebikiXSxbMCwyLCJrX2koXFxwYXJ0aWFsIERebikiXSxbMiwyLCJrX2koRF5uKSJdLFs0LDIsImtfaShEXm4sIFxccGFydGlhbCBEXm4pIl0sWzYsMiwia197aS0xfShcXHBhcnRpYWwgRF5uKSJdLFs4LDIsImtfe2ktMX0oRF5uKSJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDRdLFs1LDZdLFs2LDddLFs3LDhdLFs4LDldLFs0LDksIlxcUGhpIiwxXSxbMyw4LCJcXFBoaSIsMV0sWzAsNSwiXFxQaGkiLDFdLFsxLDYsIlxcUGhpIiwxXSxbMiw3LCJcXFBoaSIsMV1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{h_i(\partial D^n)} && {h_i(D^n)} && {h_i(D^n, \partial D^n)} && {h_{i-1}(\partial D^n)} && {h_{i-1}(D^n)} \\
	\\
	{k_i(\partial D^n)} && {k_i(D^n)} && {k_i(D^n, \partial D^n)} && {k_{i-1}(\partial D^n)} && {k_{i-1}(D^n)}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-7]
	\arrow[from=1-7, to=1-9]
	\arrow[from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow[from=3-3, to=3-5]
	\arrow[from=3-5, to=3-7]
	\arrow[from=3-7, to=3-9]
	\arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-9, to=3-9]
	\arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-7, to=3-7]
	\arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-5, to=3-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    Using the fact that \(\partial D^n\) is \((n-1)\)-dimensional, and \(D^n\) is contractible (and using homotopy invariance), by the five lemma the middle map is an isomorphism. Hence by induction, we have that
    \[\Phi_{(X, \emptyset)} : h_*(X) \to k_*(X)\]
    is an isomorphism when \(\dim(X) = n\). Now the same argument with the LES of the pair and the five lemma shows that \(\Phi_{(X, A)}\) is an isomorphism if \(X\) is a cell complex and \(A\) is a subcomplex.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    The result is true for infinite dimensional cell pairs, but it uses the ``telescope construction'', see Hatcher.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
    There is a corresponding notion of a generalised cohomology theory. We replace the covariant functor \(h_*\) with a contravariant functor \(h^*\), reverse the long exact sequence, and replace the direct sum with the direct product in the unions axiom.

    The analogue of the previous proposition holds with the same proof.
\end{remark}

\section{Cohomology}

\subsection{Cup product}

\begin{definition}
    [cup product]
    Let \(X\) be any topological space, \(\phi \in C^k(X), \psi \in C^\ell(X)\), then their \emph{cup product}\index{cup product} \(\phi \smile \psi \in C^{k+\ell}(X)\) is 
    \[(\phi \smile \psi)(\sigma : [v_0, \dots, v_{k+\ell}] \to X) = \phi\left(\sigma\vert_{[v_0, \dots, v_k]}\right)\psi\left(\sigma\vert_{[v_k, \dots, v_{k+\ell}]}\right)\]
\end{definition}

\begin{notation}
    We will write \(\phi \cdot \psi = \phi \smile \psi\).
\end{notation}

\begin{lemma}
    If \(\partial^* : C^*(X) \to C^{*+!}(X)\) is the boundary operator, then
    \[\partial^*(\phi \cdot \psi) = (\partial^*\phi)\cdot \psi + (-1)^k\phi \cdot \partial^*\psi\] 
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Let \([v_0, \dots, v_{k+\ell+1}]\) be a \((k+\ell + 1)\)-simples of \(X\). Then
    \begin{align*}
        (\partial^*\phi)\psi([v_0, \dots, v_{k+\ell+1}]) = \sum_{i=0}^{k+1}(-1)^i\phi([v_0, \dots, \widehat{v_i}, \dots, v_{k+1}])\psi([v_{k+1}, \dots, v_{k+\ell + 1}])
    \end{align*}
    On the other hand,
    \begin{align*}
        (-1)^k\phi(\partial^*)([v_0, \dots, v_{k+\ell+1}]) = \phi([v_0, \dots, v_k])\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell+1}(-1)^i\psi([v_k, \dots, \widehat{v_i}, \dots, v_{k+\ell+1}])
    \end{align*}
    where the indexing on the right hand side absorbed the sign \((-1)^k\).

    In the two expressions, there is only one term which appears twice, which are
    \[(-1)^{k+1}\phi([v_0, \dots, v_k])\psi([v_{k+1}, \dots, v_{k+\ell + 1}]) + (-1)^k\phi([v_0, \dots, v_k])\psi([v_{k+1}, \dots, v_{k+\ell + 1}]) = 0\]
    and cancel. The remaining terms give
    \[(\phi\psi)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k+\ell + 1}(-1)^i[v_0, \dots, \widehat{v_i}, \dots, v_{k+\ell+1}]\right) = (\phi\psi)(\partial\sigma) = \partial^*(\phi\psi)(\sigma)\]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    Cup product descends to cohomology, and so it induces a map
    \[\HH^k(X) \times \HH^\ell(X) \to \HH^{k+\ell}(X)\]
    which makes \(\HH^*(X)\) into a graded unital ring.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    Let \(\phi \in C^k(X)\), \(\psi \in C^\ell(X)\) be cocycles, The lemma gives that \(\partial^*(\phi\psi) = 0\), and so \([\phi\psi]\) represents a cohomology class in \(\HH^{k+\ell}(X)\). Next, we need to show that this is independent of choices.

    A general representative of \([\phi]\) is \(\phi + \partial^*\alpha\), then \(\phi\psi\) becomes
    \[(\phi + \partial^*\alpha)\psi = \phi\psi + (\partial^*\alpha)\psi = \phi\psi + \partial^*(\alpha\psi)\]
    With this, the element
    \[[\phi][\psi] = [\phi\psi]\]
    is well defined.

    Recall we have an element \(1 \in C^0(X)\), given by \(1(p) = 1\) for all \(p \in X\). With this,
    \[\partial^* 1(\sigma) = 1(v_0) - 1(v_1) = 1 - 1 = 0\]
    and so \(\partial^*1 = 0\). With this, we have an associated element \(1 = [1] \in \HH^0(X)\). It is easy to see that \(1\psi = \psi\) and \(\psi1=\psi\), and so this is a unit.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    [on coefficients] Recall for any abelian group \(G\), we have \(C_j(X; G)\) for chains with coefficients in \(G\), and a corresponding cochain group 
    \[C^j(X; G) = \Hom_\Z(C_j(X; \Z), G)\]
    If \(G\) is a commutative (not necessarily unital) ring, then we can define cup product on \(C^*(X; G)\), which induces a cup product on \(\HH^*(X; G)\), making it into a graded (not necessarily unital) ring. If \(G\) is a unital ring, then \(\HH^*(X; G)\) is a unital ring.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
    [Properties of the cup product]
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item (associativity) \((\phi \cdot \psi)\cdot\tau = \phi\cdot(\psi\cdot\tau)\),
        \item if \(f : X \to Y\) is a continuous map, then the induced map \(f^* : \HH^*(Y) \to \HH^*(X)\) is a ring homomorphism. In fact, this is already true at the cochain level.
        \item The \emph{cross product}\index{cross product} is
        \begin{align*}
            \HH^i(Y) \times \HH^j(Z) &\to \HH^{i+j}(Y \times Z) \\
            (\phi, \psi) &\mapsto \pr_Y^*\phi \cdot \pr_Z^*\psi
        \end{align*}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\lec

\begin{example}
    If \(X = \{*\}\), then
    \[\HH^*(X) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & * = 0 \\
        0 & \text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}\]
    In this case, \(\HH^*(X) = \Z\) has its usual ring structure.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    Now consider \(X = S^n\), where \(n > 1\). Then
    \[\HH^*(S^n) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & * = 0, n \\
        0 & \text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}\]
    If \(x\) is a generator of \(\HH^n(S^n)\), then \(x \cdot x \in \HH^{2n}{S^n} = 0\), and so
    \[\HH^*(S^n) = \frac{\Z[x]}{\inner{x^2}}\]
    where \(\abs{x} = n\).
\end{example}

\begin{notation}
    We write \(\abs{x} = k\) for the \emph{degree}\index{degree!of a cohomoloogy class} of \(x \in \HH^k(X)\). 
\end{notation}

\begin{example}
    If \(X, Y\) are cell complexes, \(x_0 \in X, y_0 \in Y\), then we have maps
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMyxbMiwwLCJYIFxcdmVlIFkiXSxbMCwxLCJYIl0sWzQsMSwiWSJdLFswLDEsInBfWCIsMCx7ImN1cnZlIjotMX1dLFswLDIsInBfWSIsMix7ImN1cnZlIjoxfV0sWzEsMCwiaV9YIiwwLHsiY3VydmUiOi0xfV0sWzIsMCwiaV9ZIiwyLHsiY3VydmUiOjF9XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	&& {X \vee Y} \\
	X &&&& Y
	\arrow["{p_X}", curve={height=-6pt}, from=1-3, to=2-1]
	\arrow["{p_Y}"', curve={height=6pt}, from=1-3, to=2-5]
	\arrow["{i_X}", curve={height=-6pt}, from=2-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{i_Y}"', curve={height=6pt}, from=2-5, to=1-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    where \(p_X\) is given by collapsing \(Y\) and \(i_X\) is the inclusion map. This gives us ring homomorphisms
    \[p_X^* : \HH^*(X) \to \HH^*(X \vee Y)\]
    Since \(x_0, y_0\) are neighbourhood deformation retracts, we have an open vover of \(X \vee Y\) and using Mayer-Vietoris, we have that
    \[\widetilde \HH^*(X) \oplus \widetilde \HH^*(Y) \to \widetilde \HH^*(X \vee Y)\]
    is an isomorphism of abelian groups. Hence we know \(\HH^*(X \vee Y)\) in terms of \(\HH^*(X)\) and \(\HH^*(Y)\). With this, if \(\alpha \in \HH^i(X), \beta \in \HH^j(Y)\), with \(i, j > 0\), then \(\alpha \cdot \beta = 0\).

    More simply,
    \[\HH^*(X \sqcup Y) = \HH^*(X) \oplus \HH^*(Y)\]
    as rings.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{prop:graded-commutative}
    \(\HH^*(X)\) is a \emph{graded commutative ring}, that is, if \(\phi \in \HH^k(X), \psi \in \HH^\ell(X)\), then
    \[\phi \cdot \psi = (-1)^{k\ell}\psi\cdot\phi\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
    If \(R\) is a commutative ring, then \(\HH^*(X;R)\) is a graded-commutative ring. Moreover, unlike associativity, this is \emph{not} true at the cochain level.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
    Suppose \(X\) has
    \[\HH^*(X) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & * = 0, 3, 6 \\
        0 & \text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}\]
    For degree reasons, the only possible non-trivial cup product is
    \[\HH^3(X) \times \HH^3(X) \to \HH^6(X)\]
    But if \(\theta \in \HH^3(X)\) is a generator, then by graded commutativity,
    \[\theta \cdot \theta = -\theta \cdot \theta\]
    and so \(\theta \cdot \theta = 0\) since \(\HH^6(X)\) is torsion free.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
    [K\"unneth] 
    \label{thm:kunneth}
    Let \(Y\) be a space be such that \(\HH^i(Y)\) is free and finitely generated for all \(i\). Then the cross product
    \[\HH^k(X) \times \HH^\ell(Y) \to \HH^{k + \ell}(X \times Y)\]
    induces an isomorphism of \emph{graded rings}
    \[\bigoplus_{k + \ell = n}\HH^k(X) \otimes \HH^\ell(Y) \to \HH^{k+\ell}(X \times Y)\]
    whenever \(X\) is a finite cell complex.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
    In general, cross product induces a homomorphism
    \[\HH^*(X; R) \otimes_R \HH^*(Y; R) \to \HH^*(X \times Y; R)\]
    of abelian groups, and if we declare the left hand side is a (graded) ring via
    \[(a \otimes b)(c \otimes d) = (-1)^{\abs{b}\abs{c}}(ac) \otimes (bd)\]
    and so the map from the cross product is a ring homomorphism. In the case of the K\"unneth formula, we then obtain an isomorphism of rings.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
    Recall
    \[\HH^*(S^1) = \frac{\Z[x]}{x^2}\]
    with \(\abs{x} =1\). Equivalently, this is \(\Lambda(x)\), exterior algebra on one generator (i.e \(\Lambda^*\Z\)).
    
    Recall additively,
    \[\HH^*(T^2) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & * = 0, 2 \\
        \Z^2 & * = 1 \\
        0 & \text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}\]
    In terms of the K\"unneth formula,
    \begin{align*}
        \HH^0(T^2) &= \HH^0(S^1) \otimes \HH^0(S^1) \\
        \HH^1(T^2) &= \HH^0(S^1) \otimes \HH^1(S^1) \oplus \HH^1(S^1) \otimes \HH^0(S^1) \\
        \HH^2(T^2) &= \HH^1(S^1) \otimes \HH^1(S^1)
    \end{align*}
    if \(x_i \in \HH^1(S^1)\) is a generator for the \(i\)-th factor, then the map \(\HH^1(T^2) \times \HH^1(T^2) \to \HH^2(T^2)\) will be
    \[(1 \otimes x_2)(x_1 \otimes 1) = -x_2x_1\]
    This is isomorphic to the exterior algebra \(\Lambda(x_1, x_2)\). Iteratotively,
    \[\HH^*(T^n) = \Lambda(x_1, \dots, x_n)\]
    where \(\HH^1(\T^n) \cong \Z^n\) has generators \(x_1, \dots, x_n\).
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    By Mayer-Vietoris, we know that
    \[\HH^*(\Sigma_g) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & * = 0, 2 \\
        \Z^{2g} & * = 1
    \end{cases}\]
    Let \(1 \in \HH^0(\Sigma_g)\) and \(u \in \HH^2(\Sigma_g)\) be generators.
    \begin{claim}
        \[\HH^*(\Sigma_g) = \frac{\Z\inner{x_1, y_1, \dots, x_g, y_g}}{\inner{x_ix_j = y_iy_j = 0, x_iy_j = \delta_{ij}u}}\]
        (and with the relations from skew-commutativity).
    \end{claim}

    \begin{proof}
        By collapsing the purple subspace
        \begin{center}
            \includegraphics[scale=0.1]{diagrams/2023_11_06 12_43 Office Lens (2).jpg}
        \end{center}
        we have a projection map \(\pi : \Sigma_g \to \bigvee_{i=1}^g T_i^2\). We also have a natural inclusion map \(p : \bigsqcup_i T_i^2 \to \bigvee_{i=1}^g T_i^2\).

        The maps \(\pi^*\) and \(p^*\) are ring homomorphisms on cohomology, and they define isomorphism on \(\HH^1\). In particular,
        \[\HH^i(\bigsqcup_i T^2) \cong \bigoplus_{i=1}^n \Lambda(x_i, y_i)\]
        where \(\abs{x_i} = \abs{y_i} = 1\), and generate \(\HH^1(T^2_i)\). Since \(p^*, \pi^*\) are ring homomorphisms on \(\HH^1\), we can use these to define classes in \(\HH^1(\Sigma_g)\).

        On \(\HH^2\), we have
        % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMyxbMCwwLCJcXFo9IFxcSEheaShcXFNpZ21hX2cpIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF4yKFxcYmlndmVlX3tpPTF9XmcgVF9pXjIpID0gXFxaXmciXSxbMiwyLCJcXEhIXjIoXFxiaWdzcWN1cF9pIFRfaV4yKSA9IFxcWl5nIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxwaV4qIiwyXSxbMSwyLCJwXioiXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\Z= \HH^i(\Sigma_g)} && {\HH^2(\bigvee_{i=1}^g T_i^2) = \Z^g} \\
	\\
	&& {\HH^2(\bigsqcup_i T_i^2) = \Z^g}
	\arrow["{\pi^*}"', from=1-3, to=1-1]
	\arrow["{p^*}", from=1-3, to=3-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
        and if \(u_i = x_iy_i \in \HH^2(T^2_i)\) are generators, we need to show that the map \(\Z^g \to \Z\) sends \(u_i\) to \(u\). To see this, we need to consider the map
        \[\HH^2(T^2) \to \HH^2(\Sigma_g)\]
        when \(\Sigma_g \to T^2\) is the map to one factor. Recall from out computation of degree of maps between spheres that if we have a point with finite preimage, we can express
        \[\deg(f) = \sum_i \deg_{x_i}(f)\]
        as a sum of the local degree. The same argument works in this case, and so the degree is well defined.

        Thus, up to changing the sign of \(y_j\), we get the result we want, since the degree of the map \(\Sigma_g \to T^2\) is 1.
    \end{proof}
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}
    Let \(f : S^n \to T^n\) be any map, and \(n > 1\). Then \(f\) has degree zero, where \(\deg(f) : \HH^n(T^n) \to \HH^n(S^n)\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    \(f\) induces a ring homomorphism from
    \[\HH^*(T^n) = \Lambda(x_1, \dots, x_n)\]
    to
    \[\HH^*(S^n) = \frac{\Z[u]}{u^2}\]
    Since \(n > 1\), \(\HH^1(S^n) = 0\), and so \(f^*(x_i) = 0\). Hence
    \[f^*(x_1\cdots x_n) = f^*(x_1)\cdots f^*(x_n) = 0\]
\end{proof}

\lec

\begin{proof}
    [Proof of \cref{thm:kunneth}]

    Recall that
    \[C^*(X, A) = \left\{f \in C^*(X) \mid C_*(A) \sse \ker(f)\right\}\]
    If \(\varphi \in C^k(X, A), \psi \in C^\ell(X)\), then for \(\sigma : \Delta{k+\ell} \to A\) a simplex in \(A\),
    \[\varphi \cdot \psi(\sigma) = \varphi(\text{front of }\sigma)\psi(\text{back of }sigma)\]
    and so \(\varphi \cdot \psi \in C^{k+\ell}(X, A)\). From this, we have a relative cup product
    \[\HH^k(X, A) \times \HH^\ell(X) \to \HH^{k+\ell}(X, A)\]
    In particular, \(\HH^*(X, A)\) is a graded ring, but it is typically not unital. We also have a relative cross product,
    \[C^k(X, A) \otimes C^\ell(Y) \to C^{k + \ell}(X \times Y, A \times Y)\]
    and this induces a map on cohomoloogy
    \[\HH^k(X, A) \otimes \HH^\ell(Y) \to \HH^{k + \ell}(X \times Y, A \times Y)\]

    Now consider the associations for fixed \(Y\), with cellular pairs \((X, A)\),
    \begin{align*}
        h^*(X, A) &:= \HH^*(X, A) \otimes \HH^*(Y) \\
        k^*(X, A) &:= \HH^*(X \times Y, A \times Y)
    \end{align*}
    and the relative cup product defines a map \(\Phi : h^*(X, A) \to k^*(X, A)\). If \((X, A) = (\pt, \emptyset)\), then
    \[\Phi : h^*(\pt) = \Z \otimes \HH^*(Y) \cong \HH^*(\pt \times Y) = \HH^*(Y)\]
    By our discussion of generalised cohomology theories, if \(\Phi\) is a natural transformation, and if \(h^*, k^*\) are generalised cohomology theories, then \(\Phi\) will be an isomorphism for all cellular pairs \((X, A)\).

    \textbf{\(h^*, k^*\) are generalised cohomology theories:} For \(k^*\), all the axioms follow by our known properties of singular cohomology. For \(h^*\), naturality, homotopy invariance and excision are immediate. The long exact sequence and unions axioms hold as we are assuming that \(\HH^i(Y)\) is finitely generated and free. That is, if \(M\) is finitely generated and free, then
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item the functor \(\T_M(N) = M \otimes N\) is exact (i.e. it preserves exact sequences),
        \item \(M \otimes \prod_\alpha N_\alpha = \prod_\alpha(M \otimes N_\alpha)\),
    \end{enumerate}

    \textbf{\(\Phi\) is a natural transformation}

    We know the cup product and cross products are natural for maps of spaces. So naturality, homotopy invariance and excision axioms are fine. Consider 
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXEhIXmsoQSlcXG90aW1lc1xcSEheXFxlbGwoWSkiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIXntrKzF9KFgsIEEpXFxvdGltZXMgXFxISF5cXGVsbChZKSJdLFswLDIsIlxcSEhee2srXFxlbGx9KEEgXFx0aW1lcyBZKSJdLFsyLDIsIlxcSEhee2srXFxlbGwrMX0oWCBcXHRpbWVzIFksIEEgXFx0aW1lcyBZKSJdLFsxLDMsIlxcUGhpIiwxXSxbMCwyLCJcXFBoaSIsMV0sWzAsMSwiXFxwYXJ0aWFsX1xcbGVzXFxvdGltZXNcXGlkIiwxXSxbMiwzLCJcXHBhcnRpYWxfXFxsZXMiLDFdXQ==
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        {\HH^k(A)\otimes\HH^\ell(Y)} && {\HH^{k+1}(X, A)\otimes \HH^\ell(Y)} \\
        \\
        {\HH^{k+\ell}(A \times Y)} && {\HH^{k+\ell+1}(X \times Y, A \times Y)}
        \arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-3, to=3-3]
        \arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-1, to=3-1]
        \arrow["{\partial_\les\otimes\id}"{description}, from=1-1, to=1-3]
        \arrow["{\partial_\les}"{description}, from=3-1, to=3-3]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    We need the square to commute and then \(\Phi\) will be compatible with the long exact sequences.

    Recall given \(\phi \in C^k(A)\) with \(\partial^*\phi = 0\), we extend it to \(\widehat \phi \in C^k(X)\), and set
    \[\partial_\les[\phi] = \partial^*[\widehat\phi]\]
    Thus, if \(\phi \in C^\ell(Y)\) is any cocyle, then \(\widehat\phi \times \psi\) is an extension of \(\phi \times \psi\) from \(A \times Y\) to \(X \times Y\). Hence the square commutes.
\end{proof}

% I will defer the details to you equipped with Hatcher and a vat of gin.

\begin{proof}
    [Sketch proof of \cref{prop:graded-commutative}]

    Let \(\epsilon_n = (-1)^{n(n+1)/2}\), and
    \begin{align*}
        \rho : C_n(X) &\to C_n(X) \\
        [v_0, \dots, v_n] &\mapsto \epsilon_n[v_0, \dots, v_n]
    \end{align*}

    \begin{claim}
        \(\rho\) is a chain map, which is chain homotopic to the identity.
    \end{claim}

    Given the claim,
    \begin{align*}
        (\rho^*\phi)\cdot(\rho^*(\psi))([v_0, \dots, v_{k+\ell}]) &= \phi(\epsilon_k[v_k, \dots, v_n])\psi(\epsilon_\ell[v_{k+\ell}, \dots, v_k])
    \end{align*}
    whereas
    \[\rho^*(\psi\cdot\phi) = \epsilon_{k+\ell}\psi([v_{k+\ell}, \dots, v_k])\phi([v_k, \dots, v_0])\]
    and we also have that
    \[\epsilon_k\epsilon_\ell = (-1)^{k\ell}\epsilon_{k+\ell}\]
    and \(\rho\) being chain homotopic to \(\id\) shows that \(\rho^* = \id\) on \(\HH^*\), and the result then follows.

    To see that \(\rho\) is a chain map, we can just compute. We would like the diagram to commute
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJDX24oWCkiXSxbMCwyLCJDX24oWCkiXSxbMiwwLCJDX3tuLTF9KFgpIl0sWzIsMiwiQ197bi0xfShYKSJdLFsxLDAsIlxccmhvIiwxXSxbMywyLCJcXHJobyIsMV0sWzAsMiwiXFxwYXJ0aWFsIiwxXSxbMSwzLCJcXHBhcnRpYWwiLDFdXQ==
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        {C_n(X)} && {C_{n-1}(X)} \\
        \\
        {C_n(X)} && {C_{n-1}(X)}
        \arrow["\rho"{description}, from=3-1, to=1-1]
        \arrow["\rho"{description}, from=3-3, to=1-3]
        \arrow["\partial"{description}, from=1-1, to=1-3]
        \arrow["\partial"{description}, from=3-1, to=3-3]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    But
    \[\partial(\rho\sigma) = \epsilon_n \sum (-1)^i \sigma\vert_{[v_n, \dots, \widehat{v_{n-i}}, \dots, v_0]}\]
    and
    \[\rho(\partial\sigma) = \epsilon_{n-1}\sum (-1)^{n-i} \sigma\vert_{[v_n, \dots, \widehat{v_{n-i}}, \dots, v_0]}\]
    Since \(\epsilon_n = (-1)^n \epsilon_{n-1}\), we are done.

    For the homotopy, we will need a \emph{twisted prism operator}. We want \(P : C_n(X) \to C_{n+1}(X)\) such that
    \[\partial P + P\partial = \rho - \id\]

    If \(\pi : \Delta^n \times [0, 1]^n \to \Delta^n\) is the projection map, define
    \[P\sigma = \sum_i (-1)^i\epsilon_{n-i}\pi([v_0, \dots, v_i, w_n, \dots, w_i])\]
\end{proof}

\lec

\subsection{Projective space}

We will go through an extended example. Recall \(\C\P^n\) has a cell structure with one cell in each even dimension \(0, 2, \dots, 2n\). In particular, \(\partial^*_\cell = 0\). Hence \(\HH^*(\C\P^n) \cong C^*_\cell(\C\P^n)\).

As a ring, we will show that
\[\HH^*(\C\P^n) = \frac{\Z[x]}{x^{n+1}}\]
where \(\abs{x} = 2\). Thus, \(x^i \ne 0\) and generates \(\HH^i(\C\P^n) \cong \Z\) for \(0 \le i \le n\).

\begin{lemma}
    There is a natural map
    \[\pi : \underbrace{\C\P^1 \times \cdots \times \C\P^1}_{n\text{ copies}} \to \C\P^n\]
    which is invariant under permutation, and so it induces a homeomorphism
    \[\overline\pi : \frac{(\C\P^1)^n}{S_n} \cong \C\P^n\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    For \((a: b) \in \C\P^1\), we associate the linear homogeneous polynomial \((bx - ay)\) which vanishes at \((a : b)\). If we have \((a_1 : b_1), \dots, (a_n, b_n) \in \P^1\), consider
    \[\prod_{i=1}^n (b_i x - a_i y) = \alpha_0 x^n + \alpha_1 x^{n-1}y + \cdots + \alpha_{n-1}x y^{n-1} + \alpha_n y^n\]
    and define
    \[\pi((a_1:b_1), \dots, (a_n: b_n)) = (\alpha_0 : \dots : \alpha_n)\]

    Clearly
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item \(\pi\) is continuous,
        \item it descends to a map \((\C\P^1)^n/S_n \to \C\P^n\),
        \item from the fundamental theorem of algebra, the map is surjective (consider roots),
        \item the induced map \(\overline\pi\) is a bijection, as a polynomial, up to scaling, is determined by its roots. So \(\overline\pi\) is a homeomorphism by the topological inverse function theorem.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

We've seen \(\HH^{2n}(\C\P^n) = \Z\), and that
\[\HH^*(\C\P^1) = \frac{\Z[x]}{x^2}\]
where \(\abs{x} = 2\). Since \(\HH^*(\C\P^1)\) is finitely generated and free in each degree, and so we can use K\"unneth to get that
\begin{align*}
    \HH^*(\C\P^1 \times \cdots \times \C\P^1) &\cong \bigotimes_{i=1}^n \HH^i(\C\P^n) \\
    &= \frac{\Z[u_1, \dots, u_n]}{\inner{u_1^2, \dots, u_n^2}}
\end{align*}
where \(\abs{u_i} = 2\). In particular,
\[\HH^{2n}((\C\P^1)^n) \cong \Z\]
generated by \(u_1\cdots u_n\). Hence it makes sense to compute the degree of \(\pi\). That is, what is the induced map
\[\pi^* : \HH^*(\C\P^n) \to \HH^*((\C\P^1)^n)\]
Choose a generic point \(q = (\alpha_0 : \dots : \alpha_n)\) representing a polynomial with distinct roots, then naturally
\[\pi^{-1}(q) = \left\{p_\sigma \mid \sigma \in S_n\right\}\]
is a finite set of \(n!\) elements. By our considerations of local degree, we can fix a small disc \(q \sse V\), \(V\) homeomorphic to \(\R^{2n}\), with \(q\) not intersecting the locus of polynomials with repeated roots, such that
\[\pi^{-1}(V) = \bigsqcup_{\sigma \in S_n}U_\sigma\]
where \(\pi : U_\sigma \to V\) is a homeomorphism. That is, away from the locus of polynomials with repeated roots, the \(S_n\) action is free. With this, by local degree computations,
\[\deg(\sigma) = \sum_{\sigma \in S_n}\deg_{p_\sigma}(\pi)\]
where each \(\deg_{p_\sigma}(\pi) = \pm 1\) since \(\pi\) is a local homeomorphism. But the maps
\[\pi_\sigma : U_\sigma \to V \qqtext{and} \pi_\tau : U_\tau \to V\]
differ by the homeomorphism of \((\C\P^1)^n\) given by the elmeent \(\sigma\tau^{-1} \in S_n\).

But if we fix an isomorphism \(\HH^2(\P^1) \cong \Z\), where \(u_i = 1\), then the \(S_n\) action on \((\C\P^1)^n\) induces an action on \(\HH^2((\C\P^1)^n) = \Z u_1 \oplus \cdots \oplus \Z u_n\), by permuting the \(u_i\) (for example, thinking about cellular maps). Hence the action preserves \(u_1\cdots u_n \in \HH^{2n}((\C\P^1)^n)\). Thus, all of the local degrees are the same. Hence (up to a sign) \(\deg(\pi) = n!\). Now consider the pullback map
\[\HH^*(\C\P^n) \to \HH^*((\C\P^1)^n) = \frac{\Z[u_1, \dots, u_n]}{\inner{u_1^2, \dots, u_n^2}}\]
Let \(x\) be a generator of \(\HH^2(\C\P^n)\). In fact, from the cell structure of \(\C\P^n\), the \(2\)-skeleton is a copy of \(\C\P^1\). The inclusion \(\C\P^1 \to \C\P^n\) induces an isomorphism
\[\HH^2(\C\P^n) \to \HH^2(\C\P^1)\]
Now consider
\[\C\P^1 \times \text{ some generic, i.e. pairwise distinct points } \sse (\C\P^1)^n\]
The image under \(\pi\) is a line in \(\C\P^n\). Hence we can choose \(x\) so that it restricts to \(u_1\). By symmetry (i.e. \(S_n\)-equivariance), \(\pi^*(x) = u_1 + \cdots + u_n\).

In this case, \(\pi^*(x^n) = \deg(\pi)u_1\cdots u_n\). But then the right hand side is non-zero, and so \(x^n \ne 0\). In fact, \((u_1 + \cdots + u_n)^n = n!u_1\cdots u_n\). Hence \(x^i \ne 0\) for all \(1 \le i \le n\). Moreover, \(x^i\) is the generator of \(\HH^{2i}(\C\P^n)\)\footnote{To see this, note that the inclusion \(\C\P^{n-1} \inj \C\P^n\) induces isomorphisms
\[\HH^{i}(\C\P^{n-1}) \cong \HH^i(\C\P^n)\]
for \(0 \le i \le 2n-2\), and so if we show that \(x^n\) generates \(\HH^{2n}(\C\P^n)\), then under the above isomorphism, \(x^n\) also generates \(\HH^{2n}(\C\P^{n+k})\) for any \(k \ge 0\). The result then follows by induction.}.

\begin{corollary}
    A map \(f : \C\P^2 \to \C\P^2\) cannot have degree \(-1\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    \(\deg(f)\) is defined by \(f^*(x^2) = (\deg f)x^2\) where \(\abs{x} = 2\), but \(f^*(x) = \lambda x\), for some \(\lambda \in \Z\), and so \(f^*(x^2) = \lambda^2 x^2\), and \(\lambda^2 \ne -1\).
\end{proof}

In fact, we see that the degree of \(f\) has to be a square.

A consequence of our computations is that for \(M = (\C\P^1)^n, \C\P^n\), and a non-zero class \(\alpha \in \HH^*(M)\), there exists \(\beta \in \HH^*(M)\) such that \(\abs{\alpha\cdot\beta} = \dim(M)\)\footnote{Dimension of \(M\) as a real manifold.}. In fact, this is a general fact of the cohomology classes of compact oriented manifolds. This is called Poincar\'e duality, and it is our next goal.  But we would like to understand the cohomology of a manifold.

Locally, \(M\) is a disc, and the cohomology of a disc is not very interesting.

\subsection{Cohomology with compact support}

Let \(X\) be any space, \(K_1, K_2 \sse X\) be compact subsets. If \(K_1 \sse K_2\), then \(X \setminus K_1 \supseteq X \setminus K_2\), and so we have an inclusion of pairs \((X, X\setminus K_2) \sse (X, X \setminus K_1)\). This defines a pullback map on cohomology
\[\HH^*(X, X\setminus K_1) \to \HH^*(X, X \setminus K_2)\]

\begin{definition}
    [cohomology with compact support]
    The \emph{cohomology of \(X\) with compact supports}\index{cohomology with compact support} is \(\HH^*_\ct(X)\), given by
    \[\dirlim_{K \sse X \text{ compact}}\HH^*(X, X \setminus K)\]
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{Crash course on direct limits}

Let \(A\) be a poset, such that for any \(a, b \in A\), there exists \(c \in A\) with \(a \le c, b \le c\).

A \emph{direct limit of abelian groups on \(A\)}\index{direct limit of abelian groups}: Given the data
\begin{itemize}
    \item abelian groups \(\{G_a\}_{a \in A}\),
    \item a homomorphism \(\rho_{ab} : G_a \to G_b\) if \(a \le b\), such that
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item \(\rho_{aa} = \id_{G_a}\),
        \item \(\rho_{bc}\rho_{ab} = \rho_{ac}\) if \(a \le b \le c\).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{itemize}
The direct limit is
\[\dirlim_{a \in A} G_a = \frac{\bigoplus_{a \in A}G_a}{\inner{x - \rho_{ab}(x)}}\]
If \(x \in G_a, y \in G_b\), choose \(c\) with \(a \le c, b \le c\), then \(x \sim \rho_{ac}(x) \in G_c, y \sim \rho_{bc}(x) \in G_c\), and so we can set
\[[x] + [y] = [\rho_{ac}(x) + \rho_{bc}(y)]\]
This makes \(\dirlim_a G_a\) into an abelian group.

If \(\Gamma \sse A\) is \emph{cofinal}\index{cofinal}, that is, for all \(a \in A\), there exists \(\gamma \in \Gamma\), with \(a \le \gamma\), then
\[\dirlim_{a \in A} G_a = \dirlim_{\gamma \in \Gamma} G_\gamma\]

\lec

\begin{example}
    Let \(A = \N\) with its usual order, \(G_a = \Z/p^a\) for a fixed prime \(p\). The maps are
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\Z}{p^a} &\to \frac{\Z}{p^{a+1}} \\
        x &\mapsto px
    \end{align*}
    The direct limit is
    \[\dirlim_A G_a = \Z(p^\infty) = \left\{z \in S^1 \mid z \text{ is a }p^n\text{ root of unity for some }n \in \N\right\}\]
    The result is called the \emph{Pr\"ufer group}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    Let \(A = \N\) again, with \(n \le m \iff m \mid n\), and groups \(G_a = \Z\), and \(\rho_{ab}\) is multiplication by \(b/a\). In this case,
    \[\dirlim_A G_a = \Q\]
    Note that the elements \((n!)\) form a cofinal family, and so we have that the limit is
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMSwwLCJcXFoiXSxbMiwwLCJcXFoiXSxbMywwLCJcXFoiXSxbNCwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzAsMCwiXFxaIl0sWzAsMSwiXFxjZG90IDIiXSxbMSwyLCJcXGNkb3QgMyJdLFsyLDNdLFs0LDAsIlxcY2RvdCAxIl1d
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \Z & \Z & \Z & \Z & \cdots
        \arrow["{\cdot 2}", from=1-2, to=1-3]
        \arrow["{\cdot 3}", from=1-3, to=1-4]
        \arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
        \arrow["{\cdot 1}", from=1-1, to=1-2]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    Equivalently,
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXFoiXSxbMSwwLCJcXGZyYWN7MX17MiF9XFxaIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxmcmFjezF9ezMhfVxcWiJdLFszLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMCwxLCJcXGlkIl0sWzEsMiwiXFxpZCJdLFsyLDMsIlxcaWQiXV0=
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \Z & {\frac{1}{2!}\Z} & {\frac{1}{3!}\Z} & \cdots
        \arrow["\id", from=1-1, to=1-2]
        \arrow["\id", from=1-2, to=1-3]
        \arrow["\id", from=1-3, to=1-4]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    and so the limit is
    \[\bigcup_{n \in \N}\frac{1}{n!}\Z = \Q\]
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    If \(X\) is a compact set, then the poset \(\mcK\) of compact subsets of \(X\) ordered by inclusion has a final element, namely \(X\), and so
    \[\HH^*_\ct(X) = \dirlim_\mcK \HH^*(X, X \setminus K) = \HH^*(X, X \setminus X) = \HH^*(X)\]
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    \[\HH^*_\ct(\R^n) = \begin{cases}
        \Z & * = n \\
        0 &\text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}\]
    By Heine-Borel, every compact \(K \sse \R^n\) lies in \(\overline{B}(0, N)\) for some \(N\), and so
    \[\dirlim_K \HH^*(\R^n, \R^n \setminus K) = \dirlim_N \HH^*(\R^n, \R^n\setminus \overline{B}(0, 1))\]
    But via the long exact sequence of the pair, and homotopy invariance,
    \[\HH^*(\R^n, \R^{n} \setminus \overline{B}(0, N)) \to \HH^{i-1}(S^{n-1})\]
    Moreover, this is compatible with the inclusion \(\overline{B}(0, N) \sse \overline{B}(0, N+1)\), and so the direct limit is just
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXFoiXSxbMSwwLCJcXFoiXSxbMiwwLCJcXFoiXSxbMywwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzAsMSwiXFxpZCJdLFsxLDIsIlxcaWQiXSxbMiwzXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\Z & \Z & \Z & \cdots
	\arrow["\id", from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["\id", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    which is \(\Z\), where the only non-zero degree is when \(* = n\).
\end{example}

Note that
\[\HH^*_\ct(\{\pt\}) = \begin{cases}
    \Z & * = 0 \\
    0 & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases} \qquad \HH^*_\ct(\R^n) = \begin{cases}
    \Z & * = n \\
    0 & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases}\]
and so it is \emph{not} homotopy invariant. Moreover, it is \emph{not} functorial either. On the other hand, if \(f : X \to Y\) is continuous, closed and proper, then it induces a map
\[\HH^*_\ct(Y) \to \HH^*_\ct(X)\]

On the other hand, if \(i : U \to X\) is the inclusion of an open set in a Hausdorff space \(X\), then we have an \emph{extension by zero}\index{extension by zero} map \(i_* : \HH^*_\ct(U) \to \HH^*_\ct(X)\), using the fact that if \(K \sse U\) is compact, then \(K \sse X\) is compact. This gives a map from compact sets on \(U\) to compact sets on \(X\), which gives an induced map on cohomology. In particular, we are interested in the case when \(X\) is a manifold and \(U \sse X\) is a disc.

\section{Cohomology of manifolds}

Recall in this course, an \(n\)-manifold is a Hausdorff space locally homeomorphic to \(\R^n\).

\subsection{Orientation}

\begin{definition}
    [local orientation] Let \(R\) be a unital commuative ring (most importantly \(\Z\) and \(\Z/2\)). A \emph{local \(R\)-orientation}\index{local orientation} for a manifold \(M\) at \(x \in M\) is a choice of generator
    \[\epsilon_x \in \HH_n(M, M \setminus x; R) \cong R\]
\end{definition}
Recall by excision, \(\HH_n(M, M \setminus x; R) \cong \HH_n(U, U \setminus x)\), where \(U\) is an open ball.

\begin{definition}
    [oriented] A manifold \(M\) is \emph{\(R\)-oriented}\index{oriented!manifold} if we have chosen local orientations \(\epsilon_x\) for all \(x \in M\), such that if \(\varphi : U \to \R^n\) is a chart on \(M\) in a preferred atlas, such that for all \(p_i \in U \sse M\),
    \[\HH_n(M, M \setminus p_i) \cong \HH_n(\R^n, \R^n \setminus \varphi(p_i))\]
    by excision above, and we can define a map of pairs \((\R^n, \R^n \setminus \varphi(p_1)) \to (\R^n, \R^n \setminus \varphi(p_2))\) by translation, which induces an isomorphism on the right hand side. This induces an isomorphism
    \[\psi : \HH_n(M, M \setminus p_1) \to \HH_n(M, M \setminus p_2)\]
    we require \(\psi(\epsilon_{p_1}) = \epsilon_{p_2}\).

    Diagramatically, \(\psi\) is given by
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMCwwLCJcXEhIX24oTSwgTSBcXHNldG1pbnVzIHApIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF9uKFUsIFUgXFxzZXRtaW51cyBwKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhfbihcXFJebiwgXFxSXm4gXFxzZXRtaW51cyBcXHZhcnBoaShwKSkiXSxbNCwxLCJcXEhIX24oXFxSXm4sIFxcUl5uXFxzZXRtaW51cyBcXHZhcnBoaShxKSkiXSxbMiwxLCJcXEhIX24oVSwgVSBcXHNldG1pbnVzIHApIl0sWzAsMSwiXFxISF9uKE0sIE1cXHNldG1pbnVzIHEpIl0sWzAsNSwiXFxwc2kiLDJdLFswLDEsIlxcdGV4dHtleGNpc2lvbn0iXSxbNSw0LCJcXHRleHR7ZXhjaXNpb259IiwyXSxbMSwyLCJcXHRleHR7Y2hhcnR9Il0sWzQsMywiXFx0ZXh0e2NoYXJ0fSIsMl0sWzIsMywiXFx0ZXh0e3RyYW5zbGF0aW9ufSJdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH_n(M, M \setminus p)} && {\HH_n(U, U \setminus p)} && {\HH_n(\R^n, \R^n \setminus \varphi(p))} \\
	{\HH_n(M, M\setminus q)} && {\HH_n(U, U \setminus p)} && {\HH_n(\R^n, \R^n\setminus \varphi(q))}
	\arrow["\psi"', from=1-1, to=2-1]
	\arrow["{\text{excision}}", from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{\text{excision}}"', from=2-1, to=2-3]
	\arrow["{\text{chart}}", from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow["{\text{chart}}"', from=2-3, to=2-5]
	\arrow["{\text{translation}}", from=1-5, to=2-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    If \(U, V \sse \R^n\) are open, \(f : U \to V\) is a homeomorphism is \emph{orientation preserving}\index{orientation preserving} if for all \(x \in U, f(x) \in V\),
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMCwwLCJcXEhIX24oXFxSXm4sIFxcUl5uXFxzZXRtaW51cyAwKSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcSEhfbihcXFJebiwgXFxSXm4gXFxzZXRtaW51cyB4KSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhfbihVLCBVXFxzZXRtaW51cyB4KSJdLFs0LDEsIlxcSEhfbihWLCBWXFxzZXRtaW51cyB4KSJdLFsyLDEsIlxcSEhfbihcXFJebiwgXFxSXm4gXFxzZXRtaW51cyB5KSJdLFswLDEsIlxcSEhfbihcXFJebiwgXFxSXm4gXFxzZXRtaW51cyAwKSJdLFswLDEsIlxcdGV4dHt0cmFuc2xhdGlvbn0iXSxbNSw0LCJcXHRleHR7dHJhbnNsYXRpb259IiwyXSxbMSwyLCJcXHRleHR7ZXhjaXNpb259Il0sWzQsMywiXFx0ZXh0e2V4Y2lzaW9ufSIsMl0sWzIsMywiZl8qIl0sWzAsNSwiXFxpZCIsMl1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH_n(\R^n, \R^n\setminus 0)} && {\HH_n(\R^n, \R^n \setminus x)} && {\HH_n(U, U\setminus x)} \\
	{\HH_n(\R^n, \R^n \setminus 0)} && {\HH_n(\R^n, \R^n \setminus y)} && {\HH_n(V, V\setminus x)}
	\arrow["{\text{translation}}", from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{\text{translation}}"', from=2-1, to=2-3]
	\arrow["{\text{excision}}", from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow["{\text{excision}}"', from=2-3, to=2-5]
	\arrow["{f_*}", from=1-5, to=2-5]
	\arrow["\id"', from=1-1, to=2-1]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    commutes. Then \(M\) is orientable if it admits an atlas \(\{U_\alpha, \varphi_\alpha\}\) of charts where the transition maps are orientation preserving.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
    If \(R = \Z/2\), then \(\Z/2\) has only one generator, and so all manifolds are \(\Z/2\) orientable.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
    [Poincar\'e duality] \index{Poincar\'e duality}Let \(R\) be an \(R\)-oriented manifold of dimension \(n\), then there is a distinguised isomorphism
    \[D : \HH^i_\ct(M; R) \to \HH_{n-i}(M; R)\]
    In particular, if \(M\) is compact, then
    \[D : \HH^i(M; R) \to \HH_{n-i}(M; R)\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
    By considering \(R = \Z\) and \(M\) being \(\R\P^2\) or the Klein bottle, we see that we need the orientability requirement.
\end{remark}

The isomorphism \(D\) is obtained from the \emph{cap product}.

\subsection{Cap product}

\begin{definition}
    [cap product]
    Let \(X\) be any topological space, the \emph{cap product}\index{cap product} is
    \begin{align*}
        \frown : C_k(X) \otimes C^\ell(X) &\to C_{k - \ell}(X) \\
        [v_0, \dots, v_k] \otimes \psi &\mapsto \psi([v_0, \dots, v_\ell])[v_\ell, \dots, v_k]
    \end{align*}
    for \(\ell \le k\), and \(0\) if \(\ell > k\).
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    For any space \(X\),
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item \(\partial(\sigma \frown \phi) = (-1)^\ell(\partial\sigma \frown \phi - \sigma \frown \partial^*\phi)\) for \(\sigma \in C_k(X), \phi \in C^\ell(X)\). Indeed, \(\frown\) induces a pairing (for \(\ell \le k\))
        \[\HH_k(X) \otimes \HH^\ell(X) \to \HH_{k - \ell}(X)\]
        \item Given a map \(f : X \to Y\),
        \[f_*(\alpha) \frown \psi = f_*(\alpha \frown f^*(\psi))\]
        for \(\alpha \in \HH_k(X), \psi \in \HH^\ell(Y)\).
        \item \[\psi(\sigma \frown \phi) = (\phi \smile \psi)(\sigma) \in \Z\]
        For \(\sigma \in C_{k+\ell}(X), \phi \in C^k(X), \psi \in C^\ell(X)\),
        \item for a pair \((X, A)\), there is a \emph{relative cap product}\index{relative cap product}
        \[C_k(X, A) \otimes C^\ell(X, A) \to C_{k - \ell}(X)\]
        which descends to coohomology.  
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\lec

\begin{proof}
    For (i),
    \begin{align*}
        \partial\sigma \frown \phi &= \sum_{i=0}^\ell\phi([v_0, \dots, \widehat{v_i}, \dots, v_{\ell + 1}])[v_{\ell + 1}, \dots, v_k] + \sum_{i=\ell + 1}^k(-1)^i\phi([v_0, \dots, v_\ell])[v_\ell, \dots, \widehat{v_i}, \dots, v_k] \\
        \sigma \frown \partial^*\phi &= \sum_{i=0}^{\ell + 1}(-1)^i\phi([v_0, \dots, \widehat{v_i}, \dots, v_{\ell + 1}])[v_{\ell + 1}, \dots, v_k] \\
        \partial(\sigma \frown \phi) &= \sum_{i=\ell}^k(-1)^{i + \ell}\phi([v_0, \dots, v_\ell])[v_\ell, \dots, \widehat{v_i}, \dots, v_k]
    \end{align*}
    Rearrange/compare terms to get the result. 
    
    For (ii), and (iii), they hold at chain lebel from definitions. Say at the level of (co)homology we have for (ii)

    and for (iii)

    \[\begin{tikzcd}

        \arrow[from=1-1, to=1-3]
