    Note that the horizontal maps in (iii) don't need to be isomorphism, but if we worked over a field, then they would be.

    \textit{Ivan lost (iv) somewhere...}
\end{proof}

We want to define the map \(D\) from the statment of Poincar\'e duality.

\begin{proposition}
    \label{prop:exists-orientation-on-cpct}
    Let \(M\) be an oriented \(n\)-manifold, and so we have \(\omega_x \in \HH_n(M, M \setminus x)\) which are coherent. Then for each \(K \sse M\) compact, there exists a unique \(\omega_K \in \HH_n(M, M \setminus K)\) such that the map on pairs
    \[(M, M \setminus K) \to (M, M \setminus x)\]
    sends \(\omega_K\) to \(\omega_x\) for all \(x \in K\). Note that \(\HH_i(M, M \setminus K) = 0\) for \(i > n\). 
\end{proposition}

Given the proposition, then \(M\) be oriented, \(K \sse L \sse M\) be compact. We can consider
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsOCxbMCwwLCJcXEhIX2koTSwgTSBcXHNldG1pbnVzIEwpIl0sWzAsMiwiXFxISF9pKE0sIE0gXFxzZXRtaW51cyBLKSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcSEheayhNLCBNIFxcc2V0bWludXMgTCkiXSxbMSwwLCJcXG90aW1lcyJdLFsyLDIsIlxcSEheayhNLCBNIFxcc2V0bWludXMgSykiXSxbMSwyLCJcXG90aW1lcyJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhfe2kta30oTSkiXSxbNCwyLCJcXEhIX3tpLWt9KE0pIl0sWzAsMV0sWzQsMl0sWzIsNiwiXFxmcm93biJdLFs0LDcsIlxcZnJvd24iLDJdLFs2LDcsIiIsMCx7Im9mZnNldCI6MSwic3R5bGUiOnsiaGVhZCI6eyJuYW1lIjoibm9uZSJ9fX1dLFs2LDcsIiIsMSx7Im9mZnNldCI6LTEsInN0eWxlIjp7ImhlYWQiOnsibmFtZSI6Im5vbmUifX19XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=tiny]
	{\HH_i(M, M \setminus L)} & \otimes & {\HH^k(M, M \setminus L)} && {\HH_{i-k}(M)} \\
	\\
	{\HH_i(M, M \setminus K)} & \otimes & {\HH^k(M, M \setminus K)} && {\HH_{i-k}(M)}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow[from=3-3, to=1-3]
	\arrow["\frown", from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow["\frown"', from=3-3, to=3-5]
	\arrow[shift right, no head, from=1-5, to=3-5]
	\arrow[shift left, no head, from=1-5, to=3-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
where the vertical maps are induced from the inclusion \(i\) (of pairs). Now
\[\omega_K \frown \phi = i_*\omega_L \frown \phi = \omega_L \frown i^*\phi\]
since by uniqueness, \(i_*\omega_L = \omega_K\).

The map \(\phi \mapsto \omega_k \frown \phi\) is compatible with the maps in the directed system definining \(\HH^*_\ct\) via invlusions \(K \inj L\). So there exists an induced map \(\HH^k_\ct(M) \to \HH_{n-k}(M)\).

Moreover, if \(M\) is compact, then we have \(\omega_M \in \HH_n(M)\), and \(D(\phi) = \omega_M \frown \phi\) is called the \emph{fundamental class}, denoted by \([M]\).

\begin{proof}
    [Proof of \cref{prop:exists-orientation-on-cpct}] We will prove this for more and more general classes of \(K\). We say that \(K \sse M\) compact is \emph{good} if it satisfies the conclusions of the proposition.

    \textbf{Step 1: If \(A, B, A \cap B\) are good, then so is \(A \cup B\)}. In this case, we have
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJcXEhIX3tuKzF9KE0sIE0gXFxzZXRtaW51cyBBIFxcY2FwIEIpIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF9uKE0sIE0gXFxzZXRtaW51cyBBIFxcY3VwIEIpIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxISF9uKE0sIE1cXHNldG1pbnVzIEEpXFxvcGx1c1xcSEhfbihNLCBNXFxzZXRtaW51cyBCKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhfbihNLCBNIFxcc2V0bWludXMgQSBcXGNhcCBCKSJdLFswLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbNSwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzQsMF0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMl0sWzIsM10sWzMsNV1d
    \[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=tiny]
        \cdots & {\HH_{n+1}(M, M \setminus A \cap B)} & {\HH_n(M, M \setminus A \cup B)} & {\HH_n(M, M\setminus A)\oplus\HH_n(M, M\setminus B)} & {\HH_n(M, M \setminus A \cap B)} & \cdots
        \arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
        \arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
        \arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
        \arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
        \arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    By uniqueness, \(\omega_A \mapsto \omega_{A \cap B}\) and \(\omega_B \mapsto \omega_{A \cap B}\) under relative inclusions. Hence the map
    \[\HH_n(M, M \setminus A) \oplus \HH_n(M, M \setminus B) \to \HH_n(M, M \setminus A \cap B)\]
    is zero. Hence we have a class \(\omega_{A \cup B}\) mapping to \(\omega_A\) and \(\omega_B\). By assumption, \(\HH_{n+1}(M, M\setminus A \cap B)\) is zero, and so \(\omega_{A \cup B}\) is unique. Moreover, for all \(x \in A \cup B\), by construction \(\omega_{A \cup B} \mapsto \omega_x\). Finally, \(\HH_i(M, M \setminus A \cup B) = 0\) for \(i > n\) by exactness of the Mayer-Vietoris sequence.

    \textbf{Step 2: If \(K \sse \R^n\) is convex, then \(K\) is good.} If so, then \(\HH_*(\R^n, \R^n \setminus K) = \HH_*(\R^n, \R^n \setminus 0)\). But this case is easy, since we can use translations. Using step 1, finite unions of convex sets is good.

    \textbf{Step 3: Every compact set \(K \sse \R^n\) is good.} If \(K \sse \R^n\) is compact, then \(K \sse B = \overline{B}(0, R)\) for some \(R > 0\). Define \(\omega_K\) to be the restriction of \(\omega_B\) to \(K\), via
    \[(\R^n, \R^n \setminus B) \to (\R^n, \R^n \setminus K)\]
    We will write \(\omega_K = \omega_B\vert_K\). Since \(\omega_B \mapsto \omega_x\) under restriction, so does \(\omega_K\). So what we need is uniqueness. That is, we need to know that no other element of \(\HH_n(\R^n, \R^n \setminus K)\) also satisfies the requirement that it pushes forward to \(\omega_x\) for all \(x \in K\).

    That is, we want to know if that if \(\lambda \in \HH_n(\R^n, \R^n \setminus K)\), with \(\lambda\vert_x = 0\) for all \(x \in K\), then \(\lambda = 0\). Suppose we have such a \(\lambda\). So \(\lambda\) is represented by a chain, and \(\partial\lambda\) is a finite union of simplices in \(\R^n \setminus K\). Thus there exists a finite union of balls \(B_j\) such that
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \(K \sse \widetilde K = \bigcup_j B_j\)
        \item \(\partial \lambda \cap \tilde K = \emptyset\)
    \end{itemize}
    That is,
    \[\lambda \in \im(\HH_n(\R^n, \R^n \setminus \widetilde K) \to \HH_n(\R^n, \R^n \setminus K))\]
    But \(\tilde K\) is a finite union of convex sets, so it is good. Hence \(\lambda = 0\) by the uniqueness for \(\tilde K\).

    \textbf{Step 4: If \(K \sse M\) is compact, it is good.} We can write \(K\) as a finite union of \(K_i\), where each \(K_i\) is a compact subset of the coordinate neighbourhoods. Each \(K_i\) is good, and so their union is good by step 1.
\end{proof}

\lec

\subsection{Consequences of Poincar\'e duality}

Take coefficients in a field \(F\). Recall that
\[\psi(\sigma \frown \phi) = (\phi \psi)(\sigma)\]
and we have an isomorphism
\[\HH^k(M; F) \to \Hom(\HH_k(X; F), F) = \HH_k(X; F)^*\]a
and so we get a pairing
\begin{align*}
    \HH^k_\ct(M; F) \otimes \HH^{n-k}(M; F) &\to F \\
    (\phi, \psi) &= \psi(D\phi)
\end{align*}
and Poincar\'e dueality says that this pairing is non-degenerate. In particular, if \(M\) is compact, then the map is
\begin{align*}
    \HH^k(M; F) \otimes \HH^{n-k}(M; F) &\to F \\
    (\phi, \psi) &\mapsto \inner{\phi \cdot \psi, [M]}
\end{align*}

\begin{corollary}
    Let \(M, N\) be oriented connected compact \(n\)-manifolds, and so \(\HH^n(M) \cong \HH_0(M) \cong \Z\), and the orientation gives us a preferred orientation. Thus, a map \(f : M \to N\) has a degree \(\deg(f) \in \Z\). If this degree is non-zero, then over a field, the pullback map
    \[f^* : \HH^*(N; F) \to \HH^*(M; F)\]
    is injective in each degree.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    If \(\alpha \in \HH^i(N; F)\) is a non-zero class, the non-degeneracy of cup product as a pairing implies there exists \(\beta \in \HH^{n-i}(N; F)\) such that \(\alpha\beta \ne 0 \in \HH^n(N; F)\). Since \(\deg(f) \ne 0\), \(f^* : \HH^n(N; F) \to \HH^n(M; F)\) is non-zero, and so it is an isomorphism. Hence \(f^*(\alpha\beta) \ne 0\). But then \(f^*(\alpha)f^*(\beta) \ne 0\), and so \(f^*(\alpha) \ne 0\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    Let \(M\) be a compact manifold of odd dimension \(n = 2k + 1\). Then the Euler characteristic of \(M\) is zero.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    First of all, note that in this case, \(\HH^i(M; \Z)\) is finitely generated for all \(i\), and non-zero only if \(i \le n\). Hence the alternating sum
    \[\chi(M) = \sum_i(-1)^i\rank(\HH^i(M; \Z))\]
    is well defined. Moreover, we saw that we could compute \(\chi(M)\) by working over a field \(F\). Suppose \(M\) is oriented. Then
    \[\chi(M) = \sum_{i=0}^{2k+1}(-1)^i\rank(\HH^i(M; F))\]
    Let \(b^i = \rank(\HH^i(M; F))\) be the Betti numbers. Then
    \[\chi(M) = b_0 - b_1 + \cdots + b_{2k} - b_{2k+1}\]
    But the non-degenerate pairing implies that \(b_i = b_{n - 1}\), and so \(b_0 = b_{2k+1}\) and so on. Hence \(\chi(M) = 0\). Now if we take \(F = \Z/2\), then \(M\) is \(F\)-oriented.
\end{proof}

An alternative proof is to use the existence of an oriented double cover.

\begin{definition}
    [manifold with boundary] A \emph{manifold with boundary}\index{manifold with boundary} is a Hausdorff space \(M\), locally homeomorphic to
    \[\R^n_{\ge 0} \left\{(x_1, \dots, x_n) \mid x_1 \ge 0\right\}\]
    We define the boundary \(\partial M\) of \(M\) to be the points \(x\) which under some chart, \(\phi(x) \in \{x_1 = 0\}\).
\end{definition}

\(\partial M\) is well defined by some point set topology. Now given a compact manifold \(M^n\), is there a compact manifold with boundary \(W^{n+1}\), with \(\partial W \cong M\)? If so, we say \(M\) is \emph{null-cobordant}\index{null-cobordant}.

\begin{lemma}
    If \(M = \partial W\) as above, then \(\chi(M)\) is even. In particular, \(\C\P^2\) is not the boundary of any compact \(5\)-manifold.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Without loss of generality, \(\dim(M)\) is even. Suppose \(M = \partial W\), and we form the \emph{double} \(Z\) of \(W\), which is two copies of \(W\) glued along their common boundary \(M\). That is,
    \[Z = W_{\text{left}} \cup_M W_{\text{right}}\]
    In this case, \(Z\) is a compact manifold without boundary, with \(\dim(Z)\) odd, and so \(\chi(Z) = 0\). 
    
    But we can compute \(\chi(Z)\) using Mayer-Vietors. \(Z = U \cup V\), with \(U, V\) homotopy equvialent to \(W\), and \(U \cap V\) homotopy equivalent to \(M\). Note we use the fact that a neighbourhood of \(\partial W \sse W\) is homeomorphic to \(\partial W\times \Ico{0, \epsilon}\). That is, we have a collar neighbourhood. Using this, we have an exact sequence
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJcXEhIXmkoWikiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIXmkoVylcXG9wbHVzIFxcSEheaShXKSJdLFszLDAsIlxcSEheaShNKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhee2krMX0oWikiXSxbMCwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFs0LDBdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDVdXQ==
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \cdots & {\HH^i(Z)} & {\HH^i(W)\oplus \HH^i(W)} & {\HH^i(M)} & {\HH^{i+1}(Z)} & \cdots
        \arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
        \arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
        \arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
        \arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
        \arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    We know all the groups in the above are finitely generated. But the Mayer-Vietoris sequence is a chain complex with homology zero, and so the Euler characteristic of the chain complex is zero. But taking the alternating sum of the ranks, we get
    \[\chi(Z) - 2\chi(W) + \chi(M)\]
    But \(\chi(Z) = 0\), and so \(\chi(M) = 2\chi(W)\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Poincar\'e duality}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:pd-a}
    Let \(X\) be a locally compact Hausdorff space. If \(X = U \cup V\), with \(U, V \sse X\) open, then there exists a Mayer-Vietoris type sequence
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJcXEhIXntpLTF9X1xcY3QoWCkiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIXmlfXFxjdChVIFxcY2FwIFYpIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxISF5pX1xcY3QoVSlcXG9wbHVzXFxISF5pX1xcY3QoVikiXSxbNCwwLCJcXEhIXmlfXFxjdChYKSJdLFs1LDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMCwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzUsMF0sWzAsMV0sWzEsMl0sWzIsM10sWzMsNF1d
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \cdots & {\HH^{i-1}_\ct(X)} & {\HH^i_\ct(U \cap V)} & {\HH^i_\ct(U)\oplus\HH^i_\ct(V)} & {\HH^i_\ct(X)} & \cdots
        \arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
        \arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
        \arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
        \arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
        \arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:pd-b}
    Suppose \(M\) is a oriented \(n\)-manifold. We will say \(U \sse M\) open is \emph{good} if Poincar\'e duality holds on \(U\). That is, \(D_U : \HH^k_\ct(U) \to \HH_{n-k}(U)\) is an isomorphism. Note in this case, \(U\) is also an oriented manifold.

    Suppose \(U, V, U \cap V\) are good. Then \(U \cup V\) is good.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Assume without loss of generality that \(M = U \cup V\). From \cref{lem:pd-a}, we have
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTAsWzAsMCwiXFxISF5rX1xcY3QoVSBcXGNhcCBWKSJdLFsxLDAsIlxcSEhea19cXGN0KFUpXFxvcGx1c1xcSEhea19cXGN0KFYpIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF5rX1xcY3QoTSkiXSxbMywwLCJcXEhIXntrKzF9X1xcY3QoVSBcXGNhcCBWKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhee2srMX1fXFxjdChVKVxcb3BsdXNcXEhIXntrKzF9KFYpIl0sWzAsMSwiXFxISF97bi1rfShVIFxcY2FwIFYpIl0sWzEsMSwiXFxISF97bi1rfShVKVxcb3BsdXNcXEhIX3tuLWt9KFYpIl0sWzIsMSwiXFxISF9rKE0pIl0sWzMsMSwiXFxISF97bi0oaysxKX0oVVxcY2FwIFYpIl0sWzQsMSwiXFxISF97bi0oaysxKX0oVSkgXFxvcGx1cyBcXEhIX3tuLShrKzEpfShVXFxjYXAgVikiXSxbMCw1LCJEIl0sWzEsNiwiRCJdLFsyLDcsIkQiXSxbMyw4LCJEIl0sWzQsOSwiRCJdLFswLDFdLFs1LDZdLFsxLDJdLFs2LDddLFsyLDNdLFs3LDhdLFszLDRdLFs4LDldXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=tiny]
	{\HH^k_\ct(U \cap V)} & {\HH^k_\ct(U)\oplus\HH^k_\ct(V)} & {\HH^k_\ct(M)} & {\HH^{k+1}_\ct(U \cap V)} & {\HH^{k+1}_\ct(U)\oplus\HH^{k+1}(V)} \\
	{\HH_{n-k}(U \cap V)} & {\HH_{n-k}(U)\oplus\HH_{n-k}(V)} & {\HH_k(M)} & {\HH_{n-(k+1)}(U\cap V)} & {\HH_{n-(k+1)}(U) \oplus \HH_{n-(k+1)}(U\cap V)}
	\arrow["D", from=1-1, to=2-1]
	\arrow["D", from=1-2, to=2-2]
	\arrow["D", from=1-3, to=2-3]
	\arrow["D", from=1-4, to=2-4]
	\arrow["D", from=1-5, to=2-5]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=2-2, to=2-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=2-3, to=2-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=2-4, to=2-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    The horizontal sequences are exact, and the vertical maps are isomorphisms except the centre one. We would like to apply the five lemma. The fact that the squares commute when the square does not involve the boundary maps is clear. For the square
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXEhIXmtfXFxjdChNKSJdLFsxLDAsIlxcSEhee2srMX1fXFxjdChVIFxcY2FwIFYpIl0sWzAsMSwiXFxISF9rKE0pIl0sWzEsMSwiXFxISF97bi0oaysxKX0oVVxcY2FwIFYpIl0sWzAsMiwiRCJdLFsxLDMsIkQiXSxbMCwxXSxbMiwzXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH^k_\ct(M)} & {\HH^{k+1}_\ct(U \cap V)} \\
	{\HH_k(M)} & {\HH_{n-(k+1)}(U\cap V)}
	\arrow["D", from=1-1, to=2-1]
	\arrow["D", from=1-2, to=2-2]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    it does not commute as is. Hpwever it does commute up to a sign, and we can use the five lemma in this case. We will omit the proof of this fact. See Hatcher/Spanier for details.

\lec

    Now suppose \(M = \bigcup_i U_i\), with \(U_1 \sse U_2 \sse \cdots\) are all good, then so is \(M\) since any compact subset is contained in some \(U_i\), and so
    \[\dirlim \HH^*\ct(U_i) \to \HH^*_\ct(M)\]
    is an isomorphism. On the other hand, any homology class is represented by a finite union of simplices, which has compact image, we have natural maps
    \[\HH_*(U_i) \to \HH_*(M)\]
    and taking the direct limit,
    \[\dirlim \HH_*(U_i) \to \HH_*(M)\]
    is also an isomorphism.

    Using this, and that any open subset of \(\R^n\) is a counable union of open balls, we see that any open subset of \(\R^n\) is good. Thus, if the manifold \(M\) is \emph{second countable}, then it is covered by countably many discs, and we are done.

    In general, use Zorn's lemma for the collection of all good open subsets of \(M\),
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}
    [Proof of \cref{lem:pd-a}]

    If \((X, Y) = (A \cup B, C \cup D)\) is a union of pairs, then we have a relative Mayer-Vietoris seqeuence
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXEhIXmkoWCwgWSkiXSxbMSwwLCJcXEhIXmkoQSwgQylcXG9wbHVzXFxISF5pKEIsIEQpIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF5pKEEgXFxjYXAgQiwgQyBcXGNhcCBEKSJdLFszLDAsIlxcSEhee2krMX0oWCwgWSkiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH^i(X, Y)} & {\HH^i(A, C)\oplus\HH^i(B, D)} & {\HH^i(A \cap B, C \cap D)} & {\HH^{i+1}(X, Y)}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    using the small-simplices theorem (see Sheet 4). If \(X = U \cup V\), \(K \sse U, L \sse V\) compact, set \(A = B = X\), \(C = X \setminus K, D = X \setminus Y\), \(Y = X \setminus (K \cap L)\), \(C \cap D = X \setminus (K \cup L)\).
    We then get an exact sequence
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXEhIXmkoWCwgWFxcc2V0bWludXMoSyBcXGNhcCBMKSkiXSxbMSwwLCJcXEhIXmkoWCwgWCBcXHNldG1pbnVzIEspXFxvcGx1c1xcSEheaShYLCBYIFxcc2V0bWludXMgTCkiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIXmkoWCwgWCBcXHNldG1pbnVzIChLIFxcY3VwIEwpKSJdLFszLDAsIlxcSEhee2krMX0oWCwgWCBcXHNldG1pbnVzIChLIFxcY2FwIEwgKSkiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH^i(X, X\setminus(K \cap L))} & {\HH^i(X, X \setminus K)\oplus\HH^i(X, X \setminus L)} & {\HH^i(X, X \setminus (K \cup L))} & {\HH^{i+1}(X, X \setminus (K \cap L ))}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    Excise \(X \setminus U \cap V, X \setminus U, X \setminus V\) from the first three terms, we get
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXEhIXmkoVSBcXGNhcCBWLCAoVSBcXGNhcCBWKVxcc2V0bWludXMoSyBcXGNhcCBMKSkiXSxbMSwwLCJcXEhIXmkoVSwgVSBcXHNldG1pbnVzIEspXFxvcGx1c1xcSEheaShWLCBWXFxzZXRtaW51cyBMKSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcSEheaShYLCBYIFxcc2V0bWludXMgKEsgXFxjdXAgTCkpIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxISF57aSsxfShVIFxcY2FwIFYsIChVIFxcY2FwIFYpXFxzZXRtaW51cyhLIFxcY2FwIEwpKSJdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=tiny]
	{\HH^i(U \cap V, (U \cap V)\setminus(K \cap L))} & {\HH^i(U, U \setminus K)\oplus\HH^i(V, V\setminus L)} & {\HH^i(X, X \setminus (K \cup L))} & {\HH^{i+1}(U \cap V, (U \cap V)\setminus(K \cap L))}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    But every compact subset \(Q \sse U \cap V\) is of the form \(K \cap L\), where \(K \sse U\) compact, \(L \sse V\) compact. For example, \(U = V = Q\).

    Thus, if we range over all \(K, L\), in the first three terms we get all possible compact subsets. Since \(X\) is locally compact, any compact subset of \(X\) is contained in \(K \cup L\), for some compact \(K \sse U, L \sse V\). Thus, the compact subsets of the form \(K \cup L\) form a cofinal family. Taking the direct limit over \(K \sse U, L \sse V\) compact, and using that the direct limit of exact sequences is exact, we get the Mayer-Vietoris seqeunce for \(\HH^*_\ct\).
\end{proof}

\section{Vector bundles}

\begin{definition}
    [vector bundle] Let \(B\) be a topological space. A \emph{vector bundle of rank \(d\)}\index{vector bundle} \(E \to B\) is:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item A family of \(d\)-dimensional vector spaces \(\{E_b\}_{b \in B}\),
        \item with a topology on \(E = \bigsqcup E_b\), such that
        \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
            \item the natural projection \(p : E \to B\) is continuous
            \item and locally trivial. That is, for all \(b \in B\), there exists a neighbourhood \(U\) of \(b\), and a \emph{local trivialisation}\index{local trivialisation}, which is a homeomorphism \(\psi\) making the diagram
            % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMyxbMCwwLCJwXnstMX0oVSkgPSBFXFx2ZXJ0X1UiXSxbMiwwLCJVIFxcdGltZXMgXFxSXmQiXSxbMSwyLCJVIl0sWzAsMiwicCIsMl0sWzAsMSwiXFxwc2kiXSxbMSwyLCJcXHByXzEiXV0=
            \[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=tiny]
                {p^{-1}(U) = E\vert_U} && {U \times \R^d} \\
                \\
                & U
                \arrow["p"', from=1-1, to=3-2]
                \arrow["\psi", from=1-1, to=1-3]
                \arrow["{\pr_1}", from=1-3, to=3-2]
            \end{tikzcd}\]
            commute, and such that the map
            \[\psi : E_b \to \{b\} \times \R^d\]
            is a linear isomorphism for all \(y \in U\).
            \item The subspace topology on \(E_b\) from \(E\) is the same as the topology on \(E_b\) as a Euclidean space.
        \end{enumerate}
    \end{itemize}

    We call \(E\) the total space, \(B\) the base space, \(E_b\) the fibres.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    [section] A map \(s : B \to E\) is called a \emph{section}\index{section} of \(E\) if \(p \circ s = \id_B\).
\end{definition}

The map
\begin{align*}
    B &\to E \\
    b &\mapsto 0 \in E_b
\end{align*}
is called the \emph{zero section}.

\begin{example}
    The \emph{trivial vector bundle of rank \(d\)}\index{trivial vector bundle} is \(E = B \times \R^d\) with the product topology and the projection map.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
    [pullback] If \(p : E \to X\) is a vector bundle, \(f : Y \to X\) is any map, we define the \emph{pullback}\index{pullback} as \(f^*E \to Y\), with
    \[f^*E = \left\{(e, y) \in E \times Y \mid p(e) = f(y)\right\}\]
    This has a natural projection map to \(Y\). Then \(f^*E_y = E_{f(y)}\).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    [Whitney sum] Let \(p : E \to X, q : F \to X\) be vector bundles, then we define their \emph{Whitney sum}\index{Whitney sum} has
    \[E \oplus F = \left\{(x, y) \in E \times F \mid p(e) = q(f)\right\}\]
    This has a natural map to \(X \times X\), which lands in the diagonal, that we identify with \(X\).
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    Both of these operations have
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item it takes trivial bundles to trivial bundles,
        \item they commute with retriction to open subsets \(U \sse B\). In other words,
        \[f^*(E\vert_U) = (f^*E)\vert_{f^{-1}U} \qqtext{ and }(E \oplus F)\vert_U = E\vert_U \oplus F \vert_U\]
    \end{enumerate}
    and so they are locally trivial.
\end{remark}

Other operations include tensor product, dual, exterior powers etc.

If \(p : E \to X\) is a vector bundle, we say \(F \sse E\) is a \emph{subbundle}\index{subbundle} if for all \(x \in X\), \(F_x = p^{-1}(E_x)\) is a linear subspace, and we have an open neighbourhood of \(U\) with a trivalisation of \(F\) making
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJFXFx2ZXJ0X1UiXSxbMCwyLCJGXFx2ZXJ0X1UiXSxbMiwwLCJVIFxcdGltZXMgXFxSXmQiXSxbMiwyLCJVIFxcdGltZXMgXFxSXmsiXSxbMSwwLCIiLDAseyJzdHlsZSI6eyJ0YWlsIjp7Im5hbWUiOiJob29rIiwic2lkZSI6InRvcCJ9fX1dLFswLDIsIlxccHNpIl0sWzMsMiwiIiwyLHsic3R5bGUiOnsidGFpbCI6eyJuYW1lIjoiaG9vayIsInNpZGUiOiJ0b3AifX19XSxbMSwzLCJcXHdpZGV0aWxkZVxccHNpIiwyXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{E\vert_U} && {U \times \R^d} \\
	\\
	{F\vert_U} && {U \times \R^k}
	\arrow[hook, from=3-1, to=1-1]
	\arrow["\psi", from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow[hook, from=3-3, to=1-3]
	\arrow["\widetilde\psi"', from=3-1, to=3-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
commute.

If \(F \sse E\) is a subbundle, then we can define \(E/F \to X\), with fibre \(E_x/F_x\).

\begin{definition}
    [isomorphism] We say that vector bundles \(E \to X, F \to X\) are \emph{isomorphic}\index{isomorphism!of vector bundles} if there exists homeomorphisms \(\alpha : E \to F, g : X \to X\), making the diagram
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJFIl0sWzIsMCwiRiJdLFswLDIsIlgiXSxbMiwyLCJYIl0sWzIsMywiZyIsMl0sWzAsMl0sWzEsM10sWzAsMSwiXFxhbHBoYSJdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	E && F \\
	\\
	X && X
	\arrow["g"', from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\alpha", from=1-1, to=1-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    commute, such that \(\alpha : E_x \to F_{g(x)}\) is a linear isomorphism.
\end{definition}

The most important case is when \(g = \id\).

\lec

Define
\[\Gr(k, \R^n) = \left\{k\text{-dimensional linear subspaces in }\R^n\right\} = \frac{O(n)}{O(k) \times O(n - k)}\]
for the \emph{Grassmannian}\index{Grassmannian}. The \emph{tautological bundle}\index{tautological bundle}
\[E \to \Gr(k, \R^n)\]
has fibre at \(x \in \Gr(k, \R^n)\) the linear subspace \(\inner{x}\) corresponding to \(x\). Concretely, we can define
\[E = \{(x, e) \in \Gr(k, \R^n) \times \R^n \mid e \in \inner{x} \sse \R^n\}\]
This is a vector bundle, with the natural projection map \(p : E \to \Gr(k, \R^n)\).

\begin{proof}
    Choose an inner product on \(\R^n\), given \(x \in \Gr(k, \R^n)\), let
    \[U = \left\{y \in \Gr(k, \R^n) \mid \inner{y} \cap \inner{x}^\perp = 0\right\}\]
    On this, we have a trivialisation
    \begin{align*}
        \Psi : E \vert_U &\to U \times \inner{x} \\
        (y, \xi) &\mapsto (y, \pr_{\inner{x}}(\xi))
    \end{align*}
    where
    \[\pr_{\inner{x}} : \R^n \to \inner{x}\]
    is the orthogonal projection. The definition for \(\psi\) shows that we have a local trivialisation.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    There is an obvious notion of a \emph{complex vector bundle}\index{complex vector bundle}, with fibres being complex vector bundles, and we have an associated tautological bundle \(E \to \Gr(k, \C^n)\) as well.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
    \(\Gr(1, \R^n) = \R\P^{n-1}\), has a tautological (real) line bundle, whereas the \(\Gr(1, \C^n) = \C\P^{n-1}\), we have a tautological (complex) line bundle.

    Note a complex line bundle as real dimension \(2\).
\end{example}

\begin{lemma}
    [partition of unity]
    Let \(X\) be (para)compact Hausdorff, if \(\{U_\alpha\}\) is an open cover of \(X\), then we have a subordinate partition of unity. That is, we have maps \(\{\lambda_\alpha : X \to [0, 1]\}\), such that
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \(\Supp(\lambda_\alpha) \sse U_\alpha\),
        \item at each \(x \in X\), the number of non-zero \(\lambda_\alpha\) is finite,
        \item \(\sum \lambda_\alpha(x) = 1\) for all \(x \in X\).
    \end{itemize}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Omitted.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    [inner product]
    An \emph{inner product}\index{inner product} on a vector bundle \(E\) is a continuous map \(\inner{\cdot, \cdot} : E \otimes E \to \R\), such that on each fibre the map is an inner product.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    A vector bundle \(p : E \to X\) over a (para)compact Hausdorff space has an inner product. Moroever, \(E\) is \emph{globally generated}. For all \(x \in X\), \(\xi \in E_x\), there exists \(s \in \Gamma(E)\) such that \(s(x) = \xi\). 
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Fix a trivialising open cover \(\{U_\alpha\}\) for \(E\), and an inner product on \(\R^d\), where \(d = \rank_\R(E)\). If \(\Psi_\alpha : E\vert_{U_\alpha} \to U_\alpha \times \R^d\) is a trivialisation, then we can use \(\Psi_\alpha\) to define an inner product \(\inner{\cdot, \cdot}_\alpha\) on \(E\vert_{U_\alpha}\).

    Let \(\{\lambda_\alpha\}\) is a subordinate partition of unity, then for \(u \otimes v \in E \otimes E\), define
    \[\inner{u, v} = \sum_\alpha\lambda_\alpha\inner{u, v}_\alpha\]
    Note that \(\inner{u, v}_\alpha\) is only defined if \(p(u\otimes v) \in U_\alpha\), but if this wasn't true, then \(\lambda_\alpha(p(u\otimes v)) = 0\). By definition, this is a finite sum, and that this defines an inner product.

    Now if \(x \in U_\alpha\), \(\xi \in E_x\), choose a section \(s_\alpha \in \Gamma(E\vert_{U_\alpha})\) with \(s_\alpha(x) = \xi\). We can do this as we can take something which is constant in a trivialisation. Let \(s = \sum_\alpha \lambda_\alpha s_\alpha\). Then \(s(x) = \xi\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    If \(X\) is compact Hausdorff, \(E \to X\) is a rank \(d\) vector bundle, then there exists \(N \in \N\) and \(f : \Gr(d, \R^N)\) such that \(E \cong f^*E_{\text{taut}}\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    Since \(X\) is compact, we have a finite set \(\{s_1, \dots, s_N\} \sse \Gamma(E)\), such that on each \(E_x\) the \(s_i(x)\) span. Fix an inner product \(\inner{\cdot, \cdot}\) on \(E\), and now consider the map
    \begin{align*}
        \alpha : E &\to X \times \R^N \\
        (x, \xi)&\mapsto (x, \inner{s_1(x), \xi}, \dots, \inner{s_n(x), \xi})
    \end{align*}
    Since the sections \(s_i\) span, we see that \(\alpha\) embeds \(E_x\) into \(\R^N\) for all \(x\). That is, it embeds \(E\) as a sub-bundle of a trivial bundle. But then we can just define
    \begin{align*}
        f : X &\to \Gr(d, \R^N) \\
        x &\mapsto \alpha(E_x) \sse \R^N
    \end{align*}
    By construction, the pullback of the tautological bundle is \(E\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    Our proof actually shows that if \(E\) is a vector bundle over a compact Hausdorff space, then there exists another vector bundle \(F \to X\) such that \(E \oplus F\) is the trivial bundle, since we can just take \(F_x = \alpha(E_x)^\perp\).
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
    In fact, for this class of \(X\),
    \[\frac{\{\text{vector bundles of rank }d\}}{\text{isomorphism}} \bij \text{homotopy classes of maps} X \to \Gr(k, \R^\infty) = [X, \Gr(k, \R^\infty)]\]
    where
    \[\Gr(k, \R^\infty) = \bigcup_{n \ge 0}\Gr(k, \R^n)\]
    is the infinite Grassmannian.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Cohomology}

First note that if \(E\) has rank \(d\), then \(\HH^d(E_x, E_x \setminus 0) = \Z\).

\begin{definition}
    [oriented]
    We say that a rank \(d\) vector bundle \(E\) is \emph{oriented}\index{oriented!vector bundle} if for all \(x \in X\), we have a generator \(\epsilon_x\) of \(\HH^d(E_x, E_x \setminus 0)\), which vary locally trivially. That is, if \(x \in U\) and \(E\) is trivialised over \(U\), say
    \[\Psi : E\vert_U \cong U \times \R^d\] 
    is a trivialisation, then this induces an isomorphism \(E_y \to \{y\} \times \R^d\). Using this, we have an isomorphism \(E_y \to E_x\) should send \(\epsilon_y\) to \(\epsilon_x\).
\end{definition}

\begin{notation}
    We will write \(E^\sharp\) for the complement of the zero section in \(E\).
\end{notation}

\begin{remark}
    If we have a coefficient ring \(R\), then we have a natural definition of \(R\)-orientation. In particular, every vector bundle is \(\Z/2\)-orientable.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
    [Thom isomorphism]
    Let \(\pi : E \to X\) be an oriented vector bundle of rank \(n\), then
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item \(\HH^k(E, E^\sharp) = 0\) for \(k < n\),
        \item there exists a unique element \(u_E \in \HH^n(E, E^\sharp)\) such that restricting, \(u_E\vert_x = \epsilon_x \in \HH^n(E_x, E_x\setminus 0)\).
        \item The map
        \begin{align*}
            \HH^k(X) &\to \HH^{k+n}(E, E^\#) \\
            \alpha &\mapsto \pi^*\alpha \smile u_E
        \end{align*}
        is an isomorphism.
    \end{enumerate}
    The class \(u_E\) is called the \emph{Thom class}\index{Thom class} of \(E\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    [Euler class]
    Consider the long exact seqeunce of the pair \((E, E^\sharp)\).  Then we have a natural map \(\HH^n(E, E^\#) \to \HH^n(E)\). Now \(\HH^n(E)\) is homotopy equivalent to \(\HH^n(X)\). The image of the Thom class under this map is the \emph{Euler class}\index{Euler class}
    \[e_E \in \HH^n(X)\]
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    A \emph{characteristic class}\index{characteristic class} for vector bundles (perhaps satisfying some conditions, such as orientability) is an assignment
    \[E \mapsto c(E) \in \HH^*(X)\]
    such that for all \(f : Y \to X\),
    \[c(f^*E) = f^*c(E)\]
    The uniqueness in the Thom isomorphism implies that the Euler class \(e_E\) is a characteristic class.
\end{remark}

\lec

\begin{definition}
    [sphere bundle]
    Let \(E \to X\) be a vector bundle, with an inner product on \(E\). We define the \emph{sphere bundle}\index{sphere bundle}
    \[\mathrm{S}(E) = \left\{e \in E \mid \inner{e, e} = 1\right\}\]
\end{definition}

Up to homotopy, this is independent of the choice of \(\inner{\cdot, \cdot}\), as the inclusion into \(E^\sharp\) is a homotopy equivalence. 

Assume \(E\) is oriented and of rank \(d\), then we have the long exact sequence of the pair \((E, E^\sharp)\) and using the Thom isomorphism, we have
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTIsWzEsMCwiXFxISF5pKEUsIEVeXFxzaGFycCkiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIXmkoRSkiXSxbMywwLCJcXEhIXmkoRV5cXHNoYXJwKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhee2krMX0oRSwgRV5cXHNoYXJwKSJdLFs1LDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMCwwLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzEsMSwiXFxISF57aS1kfShYKSJdLFsyLDEsIlxcSEheaShYKSJdLFszLDEsIlxcSEheaShcXG1hdGhybXtTfShFKSkiXSxbNCwxLCJcXEhIXntpLWQrMX0oWCkiXSxbNSwxLCJcXGNkb3RzIl0sWzAsMSwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFs1LDBdLFswLDFdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDRdLFsxMSw2XSxbNiw3XSxbNyw4XSxbOCw5XSxbOSwxMF0sWzAsNiwiXFxzaW0iLDEseyJzdHlsZSI6eyJ0YWlsIjp7Im5hbWUiOiJhcnJvd2hlYWQifX19XSxbMSw3LCJcXHNpbSIsMSx7InN0eWxlIjp7InRhaWwiOnsibmFtZSI6ImFycm93aGVhZCJ9fX1dLFsyLDgsIlxcc2ltIiwxLHsic3R5bGUiOnsidGFpbCI6eyJuYW1lIjoiYXJyb3doZWFkIn19fV0sWzMsOSwiXFxzaW0iLDEseyJzdHlsZSI6eyJ0YWlsIjp7Im5hbWUiOiJhcnJvd2hlYWQifX19XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH^i(E, E^\sharp)} & {\HH^i(E)} & {\HH^i(E^\sharp)} & {\HH^{i+1}(E, E^\sharp)} & \cdots \\
	\cdots & {\HH^{i-d}(X)} & {\HH^i(X)} & {\HH^i(\mathrm{S}(E))} & {\HH^{i-d+1}(X)} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
	\arrow[from=2-1, to=2-2]
	\arrow[from=2-2, to=2-3]
	\arrow[from=2-3, to=2-4]
	\arrow[from=2-4, to=2-5]
	\arrow[from=2-5, to=2-6]
	\arrow["\sim"{description}, tail reversed, from=1-2, to=2-2]
	\arrow["\sim"{description}, tail reversed, from=1-3, to=2-3]
	\arrow["\sim"{description}, tail reversed, from=1-4, to=2-4]
	\arrow["\sim"{description}, tail reversed, from=1-5, to=2-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
and so we obtain the \emph{Gysin sequence}\index{Gysin sequence}
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJcXEhIXmkoWCkiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIXntpK2R9KFgpIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxISF57aStkfShcXG1hdGhybXtTfShFKSkiXSxbNCwwLCJcXEhIXntpKzF9KFgpIl0sWzAsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFs1LDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbNCwwXSxbMCwxLCJcXHBoaSJdLFsxLDJdLFsyLDNdLFszLDVdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH^i(X)} & {\HH^{i+d}(X)} & {\HH^{i+d}(\mathrm{S}(E))} & {\HH^{i+1}(X)} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["\phi", from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
\end{tikzcd}\]
The map \(\phi\) is the cup product with the Euler class \(e_E\), basically by definition. In de Rhem theory, the map \(\HH^{i+d}(\mathrm{S}(E)) \to \HH^{i+1}(X)\) is given by integration over the \(S^{d-1}\)-fibre.

\begin{example}
    Let \(L \to \C\P^n\) be the tautological complex line bundle. Recall that a complex vector bundle is canonically oriented, since \(\GL(d, \C) \sse \GL_+(2d, \R)\).
    Hence \(L\) has a Thom class and an Euler class. But
    \[L = \left\{(x, v) \in \C\P^n \times \C^{n+1} \mid v \in \inner{x}\right\}\]
    using the usual inner product on \(\C^{n+1}\), we see that
    \[\mathrm{S}(L) = S^{2n+1} \sse \C^{n+1}\]
    The Gysin sequence becomes
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJcXEhIXntpKzF9KFNeezJuKzF9KSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcSEheaShcXENcXFBebikiXSxbMywwLCJcXEhIXntpKzJ9KFxcQ1xcUF5uKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEhee2krMX0oU157Mm4rMX0pIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFswLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbNSwwXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXSxbMyw0XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	\cdots & {\HH^{i+1}(S^{2n+1})} & {\HH^i(\C\P^n)} & {\HH^{i+2}(\C\P^n)} & {\HH^{i+1}(S^{2n+1})} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    If \(i \le 2n - 2\), then we have
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMSwwLCJcXEhIXmkoXFxDXFxQXm4pIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF57aSsyfShcXENcXFBebikiXSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzMsMCwiMCJdLFswLDFdLFsyLDBdLFsxLDNdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {\HH^i(\C\P^n)} & {\HH^{i+2}(\C\P^n)} & 0
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    and so setting \(x = e_L \in \HH^2(\C\P^n)\), we see that \(\HH^{2k}(\C\P^n)\) is generated by \(x^k\), and so we recover the result that
    \[\HH^*(\C\P^n) = \frac{\Z[x]}{\inner{x^{n+1}}}\]
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
    Clearly if \(E\) is trivial, then \(\mathrm{S}(E) = X \times S^{d-1}\), and so we can compute \(\HH^*(\mathrm{S}(E))\) using K\"unneth. So the fact that the Gysin sequence is interesting here is detecting the fact that \(L\) is non-trivial.   
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}
    If a(n oriented) vector bundle \(E \to X\) has a nowhere vanishing section, then \(e_E = 0\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose \(s \in \Gamma(E)\) is a section, which has image in \(E^\sharp\). Then
    \[e_E \in \Im(\HH^k(E^\sharp) \to \HH^k(X))\]
    where \(k = \rank(E)\). To see this,
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXEhIXmsoRSxFXlxcc2hhcnApIl0sWzIsMCwiXFxISF5rKEUpIl0sWzQsMCwiXFxISF5rKEVeXFxzaGFycCkiXSxbMiwyLCJcXEhIXmsoWCkiXSxbMSwzLCIoXFx0ZXh0e3plcm99KV4qPXNeKiIsMl0sWzEsMl0sWzAsMV0sWzIsMywic14qIl1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH^k(E,E^\sharp)} && {\HH^k(E)} && {\HH^k(E^\sharp)} \\
	\\
	&& {\HH^k(X)}
	\arrow["{(\text{zero})^*=s^*}"', from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{s^*}", from=1-5, to=3-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    note that any two sections are homotopic, and so the Euler class must be zero, as mapping the Thom class to \(\HH^k(E^\sharp)\) gives zero by exactness.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    If \(E\) is an oriented vector bundle of odd rank, the necessarily 
    \[2e_E = 0 \in \HH^{\rank(E)}(X)\]
    and so if \(\HH^{\rank(E)}(X)\) has no \(2\)-torsion, \(e_E = 0\). To see this, consider the map
    \begin{align*}
        \alpha : E &\to E \\
        v &\mapsto -v
    \end{align*}
    which reverses orientation, i.e. it acts by \(-1\) on \(\HH^{\rank(E)}(E_x, E_x\setminus 0)\). Hence \(\alpha^* u_E = -u_E\). But on the zero section, \(\alpha = \id\). Thus, pulling back to the zero section, \(e_E = -e_E\).
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
    [Proof of the Thom isomorphism]
    We will prove the Thom isomorphism by inducting on the number of trivialising neighbourhoods for \(E\). That is, we will assume this number is finite, for example if \(X\) is compact. Zorn's lemma shows the general case.
    
    For the base case \(E = X \times \R^d\) is a trivial bundle.

    \begin{lemma}
        [Relative K\"unneth]
        Suppose \(\HH^*(Y), \HH^*(B), \HH^*(Y, B)\) are all finitely generated and free for a good pair \((Y, B)\). Then for \(X\) which has the homotopy type of a cell complex, the map
        \[\HH^*(X) \otimes \HH^*(Y, B) \to \HH^*(X \times Y, X \times B)\]
        given by the cross product is an isomorphism.
    \end{lemma}

    \begin{proof}
        We have the diagram
        % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXEhIXiooWClcXG90aW1lc1xcSEheKihZLCBCKSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcSEheKihYIFxcdGltZXMgWSwgWCBcXHRpbWVzIEIpIl0sWzAsMiwiXFxISF4qKFgpXFxvdGltZXNcXEhIXiooWS9CLFxcdGV4dHtwdH0pIl0sWzIsMiwiXFxISF4qKFhcXHRpbWVzIFkvQiwgWCBcXHRpbWVzXFx0ZXh0e3B0fSkiXSxbMCwxLCJcXGNyb3NzIl0sWzIsM10sWzIsMCwiXFxpZFxcb3RpbWVzIHBeKiIsMV0sWzMsMSwicV4qIiwxXV0=
        \[\begin{tikzcd}
            {\HH^*(X)\otimes\HH^*(Y, B)} && {\HH^*(X \times Y, X \times B)} \\
            \\
            {\HH^*(X)\otimes\HH^*(Y/B,\text{pt})} && {\HH^*(X\times Y/B, X \times\text{pt})}
            \arrow["\cross", from=1-1, to=1-3]
            \arrow[from=3-1, to=3-3]
            \arrow["{\id\otimes p^*}"{description}, from=3-1, to=1-1]
            \arrow["{q^*}"{description}, from=3-3, to=1-3]
        \end{tikzcd}\]
        which commutes, it suffices to prove this for \(B = \text{pt}\). In the above, \(p, q\) are the quotient maps, and \(p^*, q^*\) are isomorphisms on homology. But
        % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMyxbMCwwLCJcXEhIXiooWSwgXFx0ZXh0e3B0fSkiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIXiooWSkiXSxbNCwwLCJcXEhIXiooXFx0ZXh0e3B0fSkiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH^*(Y, \text{pt})} && {\HH^*(Y)} && {\HH^*(\text{pt})}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
        splits if we choose a point in \(Y\). We know the result for \(Y\) and for the point by the K\"unneth theorem. Using the five lemma gives the result.
    \end{proof}

    Thus, we have that \(E = X \times \R^d, E^\sharp = X \times (\R^d \setminus 0)\), and so by the lemma,
    \[\HH^*(E, E^\sharp) = \HH^*(X) \otimes \HH^*(\R^d, \R^d\setminus 0)\]
    Fix a generator \(\epsilon_d\) for \(\HH^*(\R^d, \R^d\setminus 0)\), and so we can just define the Thom class to be \(1 \otimes \epsilon_d\). Everything else is clear.

    For the inductive step, assume the result is known for all oriented vector bundles with trivialising open covers with at most \(N\) open sets. Assume \(E \to X\) has a cover by \(N+1\) open sets. In this case, we can write
    \[X = A \cup B\]
    such that the result holds for \(E \vert_A, E\vert_B, E\vert_{A \cap B}\). By Mayer-Vietoris, we have a sequence
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMSwwLCJcXEhIXntpLTF9KEVcXHZlcnRfe0FcXGNhcCBCfSwgRV5cXHNoYXJwXFx2ZXJ0X3tBIFxcY2FwIEJ9KSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcSEheaShFLCBFXlxcc2hhcnApIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxISF5pKEVcXHZlcnRfQSwgRV5cXHNoYXJwXFx2ZXJ0X0EpXFxvcGx1c1xcSEheaShFXFx2ZXJ0X0IsIEVeXFxzaGFycFxcdmVydF9CKSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcSEheaShFXFx2ZXJ0X3tBIFxcY2FwIEJ9LCBFXlxcc2hhcnBcXHZlcnRfe0EgXFxjYXAgQn0pIl0sWzUsMCwiXFxjZG90cyJdLFswLDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMyw0XSxbMiwzXSxbMSwyXSxbMCwxXSxbNSwwXV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=small]
	\cdots & {\HH^{i-1}(E\vert_{A\cap B}, E^\sharp\vert_{A \cap B})} & {\HH^i(E, E^\sharp)} & {\HH^i(E\vert_A, E^\sharp\vert_A)\oplus\HH^i(E\vert_B, E^\sharp\vert_B)} & {\HH^i(E\vert_{A \cap B}, E^\sharp\vert_{A \cap B})} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-5, to=1-6]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    By this, if \(i < d = \rank(E)\), then \(\HH^i(E, E^\sharp) = 0\). When \(i = d\), we get
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNSxbMCwwLCIwIl0sWzEsMCwiXFxISF5kKEUsIEVeXFxzaGFycCkiXSxbMiwwLCJcXEhIXmQoRVxcdmVydF9BLCBFXlxcc2hhcnBcXHZlcnRfQSlcXG9wbHVzXFxISF5kKEVcXHZlcnRfQiwgRV5cXHNoYXJwXFx2ZXJ0X0IpIl0sWzMsMCwiXFxISF5kKEVcXHZlcnRfe0FcXGNhcCBCfSwgRV5cXHNoYXJwXFx2ZXJ0X3tBIFxcY2FwIEJ9KSJdLFs0LDAsIlxcY2RvdHMiXSxbMCwxXSxbMSwyXSxbMiwzXSxbMyw0XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	0 & {\HH^d(E, E^\sharp)} & {\HH^d(E\vert_A, E^\sharp\vert_A)\oplus\HH^d(E\vert_B, E^\sharp\vert_B)} & {\HH^d(E\vert_{A\cap B}, E^\sharp\vert_{A \cap B})} & \cdots
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow[from=1-2, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=1-3, to=1-4]
	\arrow[from=1-4, to=1-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    In this case, we have Thom classes \(u_{E\vert_A}, u_{E\vert_B}, u_{E\vert_{A \cap B}}\). By uniqueness, \(u_{E\vert_A}\vert_{A \cap B} = u_{E\vert_{A \cap B}} = u_{E\vert_B}\vert_{A \cap B}\), hence \((u_{E\vert_A}, u_{E\vert_B}) \mapsto 0\). With this, we must have a (unique by injectivity) class \(u_E\) such that \(u_E\vert_A = u_{E\vert_A}\) and \(u_E\vert_B = u_{E \vert_B}\).

    By construction, \(u_E\vert_{E_x} = \epsilon_x\) is the orientation generator. Thus, all we need to show is that the map
    \begin{align*}
        \HH^k(X) &\to \HH^{k+d}(E, E^\sharp) \\
        \alpha &\mapsto \pi^*\alpha \smile u_E
    \end{align*}
    is an isomorphism. The Thom map
    \[\alpha \mapsto \pi^*\alpha \smile \cdot\]
    maps the Mayer-Vietoris sequence for \(X = A \cup B\) to a Mayer-Vietoris map for \(E = E\vert_A \cup E\vert_B\). The result follows by the five lemma, once we show that the squares commute. 
    \lec
    The non-obvious case involves the boundary map. That is,
    % https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNCxbMCwwLCJcXEhIXmkoRVxcdmVydF97QSBcXGNhcCBCfSwgRV5cXHNoYXJwXFx2ZXJ0X3tBIFxcY2FwIEJ9KSJdLFsyLDAsIlxcSEhee2krMX0oRSwgRV5cXHNoYXJwKSJdLFswLDIsIlxcSEhee2ktZH0oQSBcXGNhcCBCKSJdLFsyLDIsIlxcSEhee2ktZCsxfShYKSJdLFswLDFdLFsyLDNdLFsyLDBdLFszLDFdXQ==
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{\HH^i(E\vert_{A \cap B}, E^\sharp\vert_{A \cap B})} && {\HH^{i+1}(E, E^\sharp)} \\
	\\
	{\HH^{i-d}(A \cap B)} && {\HH^{i-d+1}(X)}
	\arrow[from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow[from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow[from=3-1, to=1-1]
	\arrow[from=3-3, to=1-3]
\end{tikzcd}\]
    Let \(\varphi \in C^d(E, E^\sharp)\) be a cocycle representing the Thom class \(u_E\). Then \(\varphi\vert_{E\vert_A}\) represents \(u_{E\vert_A}\).

    If \(\alpha \in \HH^{i-d}(A \cap B)\) is a class, then we can write \(\alpha = \psi_A - \psi_B\), where \(\psi_A \in C^{i-d}(A)\) and \(\psi_B \in C^{i-d}(B)\). Then
    \[\partial^*[\alpha] = [\dd^*\psi_A]\]
    Hence along the bottom right, we get
    \[\alpha \mapsto \pi^*(\partial^*\psi_A)\cdot \varphi\]
    For the top left, note
    \[\pi^*\psi_A\cdot \varphi_{E\vert_A} - \pi^*\psi_B\cdot \varphi_{E\vert_B}\]
    is a difference of chains in
    \[C^i(E\vert_A, E^\sharp\vert_A) \qqtext{and} C^i(E\vert_B, E^\sharp\vert_B)\]
    and so the top left sends
    \[\alpha \mapsto \partial^*(\pi^*\psi_A \cdot \varphi_{E\vert_A}) = \partial^*(\pi^*\psi_A)\cdot \varphi\vert_{E\vert_A}\]
    These the agree.
\end{proof}
    
\subsection{Cup products on smooth manifolds}

Recall if \(M\) is a smooth manifold, it has a tangent bundle \(\T M \to M\) of rank \(\dim(M)\). If \(Y \sse M\) is a (smooth) submanifold, then \(\T Y \sse \T M\vert_Y\) is a subbundle. We define the \emph{normal bundle}
\[\nu_{Y/M} = \frac{\T M\vert_Y}{\T Y}\]
This is a vector bundle of rank \(\dim(M) - \dim(Y)\) on \(Y\). We'll write \(\nu_Y = \nu_{Y/M}\) when \(M\) is clear from context.

\begin{notation}
    We say that \(Y\) is \emph{co-oriented} in \(M\) if \(\nu_{Y/M}\) is an oriented vector bundle.
\end{notation}

\underline{Exercise:} If \(M\) is a smooth manifold, then an orientation on \(M\) as in this course, as
\[\epsilon_x \in \HH_n(M, M \setminus x)\]
is equivalent to an orientation of \(\T M\) as a vector bundle. One way of seeing this is using the exponential map of a Riemannian metric.

\begin{definition}
    [transverse]
    Let \(M\) be a smooth manifold, \(Y, Z \sse M\) are smooth submanifolds, then we say that \(Y\) and \(Z\) \emph{intersect transversely} if for all \(p \in Y \cap Z\),
    \[\T_pY + \T_pZ = \T_p M\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    [tubular neighbourhood]

    Let \(M\) be a smooth manifold, 
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item if \(Y \sse M\) a compact smooth submanifold, Then there exists an open neighbourhood \(U_Y\) of \(Y \sse M\), and a diffeomorphism \(\alpha: U_Y \to \nu_{Y/M}\), taking \(Y\) to the zero section. Moreover, both \(U_Y\) and \(\alpha\) are unique up to isotopy.
        \item if \(Y, Z \sse M\) are compact smooth manifolds which intersect transversally, then \(Y \cap Z\) is a smooth submanifold, with
        \[\codim(Y \cap Z) = \codim(Y) + \codim(Z)\]
        and we have an isomorphism of bundles
        \[\nu_{Y\cap Z} \cong \nu_Y\vert_{Y \cap Z} \oplus \nu_Z\vert_{Y \cap Z}\]
        and there are tubular neighbourhoods \(U_Y, U_Z\) of \(Y, Z\) respectively, with \(U_{Y \cap Z} = U_Y \cap U_Z\) compatible with the above isomorphism.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Omitted.
\end{proof}

Suppose \(M^d\) is a smooth oriented manifold, \(Y^k \sse M\) a smooth compact submanifold. Note that if \(V = V_1 \oplus V_2\) is a direct sum of vector spaces, oriented two of the three gives an orientation on the third. Thus, for \(Y\), orienting \(Y\) is the same as co-orienting \(Y\). Assume \(Y\) is oriented.

\(Y\) is a compact topological manifold, and so it has a fundamental class \([Y] \HH_k(Y)\) under the inclusion map \(i : Y \inj M\), we have a class
\[i_*[Y] = \HH_k(M) \cong \HH_\ct^{d-k}(M)\]
by Poincar\'e duality.

Alternatively, we can take the Thom class
\[\nu_Y \in \HH^{d-k}(\nu, \nu^\sharp) \stackrel{\text{tubular neighbourhood}}{\cong} \HH^{d-k}(U_Y, U_Y \setminus Y) \cong \HH^{d-k}(M, M\setminus Y) \to \HH^{d-k}_\ct(M) \cong \HH_k(M)\]

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:classes-agree}
    These two constructions agree.
\end{lemma}

\begin{notation}
    We will write \(\epsilon_Y\) for the cohomology class dual to an oriented compact submanifold \(Y\).
\end{notation}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{prop:class-intersection}
    If \(Y, Z\) are oriented compact smooth submanifolds of \(M\), which meet transversely, then
    \[\epsilon_{Y \cap Z} = \epsilon_Y \cdot \epsilon_Z\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
    \(\epsilon_Y \cdot \epsilon_Z = (-1)^{\codim(Y)\codim(Z)}\epsilon_Z \cdot \epsilon_Y\). Recall
    \[\nu_{Y \cap Z} = \nu_Y \oplus \nu_Z\]
    and so co-orientations of \(Y, Z\) and the ordering of \(Y\) and \(Z\) induce an orientation on \(Y \cap Z\). This makes the above an oriented isomorphism of vector bundles. Thus the proposition fits with skew-commutativity.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
    If \(M\) is oriented, a point \(p \in M\) is co-oriented. Thus, it has a well defined class \(\epsilon_p \in \HH^d_\ct(M)\) which is the orientation generator.

    Thus, if \(Y \cap Z\) is a transverse intersection, then \(\epsilon_{Y \cap Z}\) is non-zero. Hence \(\epsilon_Y, \epsilon_Z\) is non-zero. One example of this for \(\Sigma_2\),

    \textbf{picture}

    In particular, we can use the loops to compute the cohomology ring structure. 
\end{example}

\begin{proof}
    [Proof of proposition \cref{prop:class-intersection}] If \(E \to X, F \to X\) are oriented vector bundles, then the relative cross product defines a map
    \[\HH^i(E, E^\sharp) \otimes \HH^j(F, F^\sharp) \to \HH^{i + j}(E \oplus F, (E \oplus F)^\sharp)\]
    Noting that \((E \times F)\vert_{\Delta_X} = E \oplus F\). Moreover, we have that
    \[u_{E \oplus F} = u_E \cross u_F\]
    uner this map, since we have an isomorphism
    \[\HH^i(\R^i, \R^i \setminus 0) \otimes \HH^j(\R^j, \R^j \setminus 0) \cong \HH^{i + j}(\R^{i + j}, \R^{i + j}\setminus 0)\]
    Now
    \[\epsilon_{Y \cap Z} = u_{\nu_{Y \cap Z}} = u_{\nu_Y}\cdot u_{\nu_Z} = \epsilon_Y \cdot \epsilon_Z\]
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    If \(i : Y^{n-k} \inj M^n\) are as above, given \(\alpha \in \HH^{n-k}(M)\), then we can compute
    \[\inner{i^*\alpha, [Y]} = \inner{\epsilon_Y \cdot \alpha, [M]}\]
    where \(\inner{\cdot, \cdot}\) denotes the natural pairing between homology and cohomology.

    So \(\epsilon_Y\) behaves like a Dirac delta along \(Y\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    \[\inner{\epsilon_y \cdot \alpha, [M]} = \alpha([M] \cap \epsilon_Y) = \alpha = \alpha(D(\epsilon_Y)) = \alpha(i_*[Y]) = i^*\alpha([Y])\]
\end{proof}

Take coefficients in a field \(F\), which we will omit from the notation. Then Poincar\'e duality for a compact \(F\)-oriented manifold \(M\) says that we have a non-degenerate pairing
\begin{align*}
    \HH^k(M) \otimes \HH^{n-k}(M) &\to F \\
    (\alpha, \beta) &\mapsto \alpha \cdot \beta = \inner{\alpha \cdot \beta, [M]}
\end{align*}
Let \(\{a_i\}\) be a basis of \(\HH^*(M)\), \(\{b_j\}\) the corresponding dual basis, so \(a_i \cdot b_j = \delta_{ij}\).
Note by K\"unneth that
\[\HH^*(M \times M) \cong \HH^*(M) \otimes_F \HH^*(M)\]
Let \(\Delta \sse M \times M\) be the diagonal. Then we have
\[\epsilon_\Delta  \in \HH^*(M) \otimes \HH^*(M)\]
\begin{lemma}
    \[\epsilon_\Delta = \sum_i (-1)^{\abs{b_i}}a_i \otimes b_i\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Note that by non-degeneracy of the cup product, it suffices to show both sides evaluaed agains \(b_k \otimes a_\ell\) gives the same result. We can write
    \[\epsilon_\Delta = \sum_{i, j}c_{ij}a_i \otimes b_j\]
    for some coefficients \(c_{ij}\). So
    \begin{align*}
        \inner{\epsilon_\Delta \cdot (b_k \otimes a_\ell), [M \times M]} &= \sum c_{ij} \inner{(a_i \otimes b_j)\cdot (b_k \otimes a_\ell), [M \times M]} \\
        &= \sum (-1)^{\abs{b_j}\abs{b_k}}c_{ij}\inner{a_ib_k \otimes b_ja_\ell, [M \times M]} \\
        &= \sum c_{ik}(-1)^{\abs{b_j}\abs{b_\ell}}\delta_{ik}\delta_{j\ell}(-1)^{\abs{a_\ell}\abs{b_j}} \\
        &= (-1)^{\abs{b_\ell}(\abs{b_k} + \abs{a_\ell})}c_{k\ell}
    \end{align*}
    On the other hand,
    \begin{align*}
        \inner{\epsilon_\Delta \cdot (b_k \otimes a_\ell), [M \times M]} &= \inner{b_k \otimes a_\ell,[\Delta]} \\
        &= (-1)^{\abs{a_k}\abs{b_\ell}}\delta_{k\ell}
    \end{align*}
    Hence
    \[c_{k\ell} = (-1)^{\abs{b_k}\abs{b_\ell}}\delta_{k\ell} = (-1)^{\abs{b_k}}\delta_{\ell k}\]
    as required.
\end{proof}

\textbf{Warning:} Many books (e.g. Milnor Characteristic Classes) gives
\[\epsilon_\Delta = \sum_i (-1)^{\abs{a_i}}a_i \otimes b_i\]
which is \((-1)^{\dim(M)}\) times what we said. They define cap product so that
\[\inner{a, \delta \frown b} = \inner{a\cdot b, \delta}\]
That is, the define
\[\sigma \frown \phi\]
by feeding the \emph{back} face of \(\sigma\) into \(\phi\), whereas we fed the front face into the front face. This changes the isomorphism \(D\).

\subsection{Fixed points}

Let \(M\) be a closed smooth manifold, oriented over \(F\).

\begin{definition}
    [non-degenerate fixed points]
    If \(f : M \to M\) is smooth, we say \emph{\(f\) has nondegenerate fixed points} if \(\Gamma_f\) and \(\Delta\) intersect transversely in \(M \times M\).
\end{definition}

In this case, \(\Gamma_f \cap \Delta\) is a finite set. The \emph{sign} of a non-degenerate fixed point is 
\[\sign(\det(\id - \dd f_x))\]
Then at \((x, x)\),
\[\T_(x, x)\Gamma_f \oplus \T_{(x, x)}\Delta = \T_{(x, x)}(M \times M)\]
The sum is direct by dimension counting. Now consider the map
\begin{align*}
    F + \Delta : \T M \oplus \T M \to \T M \oplus \T M
\end{align*}
where \(F(x, x) = (x, f(x))\). This has
\[D(F + \Delta) = \begin{pmatrix}
    I & \dd f \\
    I & I
\end{pmatrix}\]
and so the sign of the fixed point says whether \(F + \Delta\) has orientation preserving determinant at \((x, x)\) or not.

In this case, 
\[\epsilon_{\Gamma \cap \Delta} = \sum_{x \in \Fix(f)}\pm \epsilon_x\]
where the sign dependes on the sign of \(x\) as above.

\begin{definition}
    [Lefschetz number] The \emph{Lefschetz number} of \(f : M \to M\) is
    \[\mathrm{STr}(f) = L(f) = \sum_{k \ge 0}(-1)^k\tr(f^* : \HH^k(M) \to \HH^k(M))\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    [Lefschetz fixed point] If \(f\) has non-degenerate fixed points, then
    \[L(f) = \sum_{x \in \Fix(f)}\sign(x)\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    We've observed
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{x \in \Fix(f)}\sign(x) &= \epsilon_{\Gamma(f) \cap \Delta} \\
        &= \inner{\epsilon_{\Gamma_f}\cdot\epsilon_\delta, [M \times M]} \\
        &= \inner{i_{\Gamma_f}^*\epsilon_\Delta, [\Gamma_f]} \\
        &= \inner{(\id \times f)^*\epsilon_\Delta, M} \\
        &= \sum_i(-1)^{\abs{b_i}}\inner{a_i \otimes f^*b_i, [M]}
    \end{align*}
    Write \(f^*b_i = \sum q_{ij} b_j\). Then \(\inner{a_i \cdot f^*b_i, [M]} = q_{ii}\) since the \(a_i, b_i\) are dual bases. So this \(q_{ii}\) is the \(ii\)-th entry of \(f\) in the \(b_j\) basis.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    Any map \(f : \C\P^{2k} \to \C\P^{2k}\) has a fixed point. In particular, no finite group can act freely on \(\C\P^{2k}\).

    Suppose \(f\) is is smooth with non-degenerate fixed points. Then 
    \[\HH^*(\C\P^{2k}) = \frac{\Z[x]}{x^{2k+1}}\]
    Suppose \(f^*(x) = \ell x\) for some \(\ell \in \Z\). But then \(f^*(x) = \ell^ix^i\) for all \(i\). These all live in even degree, and so the Lefschetz number is
    \[L(f) = 1 + \ell + \ell^2 + \cdots + \ell^{2k}\]
    which is non-zero for any \(\ell\). 
    
    Now suppose \(f : \C\P^{2k} \to \C\P^{2k}\) is continuous and has no fixed points. Then we have a nearby smooth \(\tilde f\) which still has no fixed points. Contradiction.
\end{example}
